On subgroups of finite classical groups with exactly two orbits on singular or isotropic points
Tao Feng, Qing Xiang

Abstract
. In this paper, we classify the groups of semisimilarities of finite classical polar spaces with exactly two orbits on the singular or isotropic points. As a byproduct, we obtain many highly symmetric regular sets in the point graphs of finite classical polar spaces.



1.
Introduction

The classification of finite primitive permutation groups of low rank has attracted much attention of group theorists due to applications in design theory, graph theory and finite geometry. The rank
2
case corresponds to multiply transitive permutation groups, cf.
[
21
, Chapter 7]
. The classification in the rank
3
case is accomplished in a series of papers by several researchers, and we refer the reader to
[
39
]
,
[
47
]
and
[
49
]
for the complete classification. The classification in the rank
4
case seems incomplete, and we refer the reader to the PhD theses
[
19
,
62
]
. For partial results in the other low rank cases, we refer the reader to
[
20
]
,
[
25
]
and
[
51
]
. The paper
[
51
]
contains a nice exposition about the historical developments as well as the state of the art.

In this paper, we consider an analogous problem for finite classical polar spaces; that is, we would like to determine groups of semisimilarities that have few orbits on the singular or isotropic points. Suppose that
{\mathcal{P}}
is a finite classical polar space of rank at least
2
, and let
H
be a group of semisimilarities that has
t
orbits on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
. We refer the reader to
[
28
]
for the classification results in the
t=1
case; and we will assume that
t>1
in the sequel. The
point graph
(or
collinearity graph
) of
{\mathcal{P}}
has the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
as vertices, and two distinct points are adjacent if and only if they are perpendicular. It is known that the point graph of
{\mathcal{P}}
is strongly regular. The
H
-orbits on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
form an equitable partition of the vertices of the point graph. If further
t=2
, then each orbit is a regular set of the graph, cf.
[
9
]
. A regular set of the point graph is also called an intriguing set in the literature. There are two kinds of intriguing sets, namely tight sets and
m
-ovoids, cf.
[
3
]
. The concept of tight sets and
m
-ovoids were first introduced by Payne
[
53
]
and Thas
[
61
]
respectively for finite generalized quadrangles, and were later generalized to finite polar spaces in
[
22
]
and
[
60
]
. They are important geometric objects which have close connection with strongly regular graphs and projective two-weight codes, cf.
[
9
,
17
]
. The notion of intriguing sets was introduced in
[
3
]
and
[
4
]
to unify the two concepts. In the present paper, we aim to classify the groups of semisimilarities that have exactly two orbits on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
. This is achieved in Theorems
1.1
,
1.3
and
1.6
which we state below.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
V
be a vector space of dimension
d
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
equipped with a nondegenerate quadratic, unitary or symplectic form
\kappa
, so that the associated polar space
{\mathcal{P}}
has rank
r\geq 2
and is not
Q^{+}(3,q)
. Suppose that
H
is a reducible subgroup of semisimilarities that has two orbits
O_{1}
,
O_{2}
on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
. Let
W
be an
H
-invariant subspace of the lowest dimension. Then
O_{1}
is the set of singular or isotropic points in
W^{\perp}
and
O_{2}
is its complement in
{\mathcal{P}}
, and we have one of the following cases:

(R1)

W
is a maximal totally singular or isotropic subspace,

(R2)

V=W\oplus W^{\perp}
with
\dim(W)=1
, and
H|_{W^{\perp}}
is transitive on both
O_{1}
and the isometry class of nonsingular points in
W^{\perp}
of size
\frac{1}{2}|O_{2}|
,

(R3)

{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{\pm}(d-1,q)
with
d
even,
W
is elliptic of dimension
2
, and
H|_{W^{\perp}}
is transitive on both
O_{1}
and the set of all nonsingular points in
W^{\perp}
,

(R4)

{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{\pm}(2m+1,q)
with
q
even, and either
\textup{Sp}_{2a}(b)^{\prime}\unlhd H
(
m=ab
) or
G_{2}(q^{b})^{\prime}\unlhd H
(
m=3b
).

Remark 1.2
.

Take the same notation as in Theorem
1.1
. The orbits of the isometry group of
(V,\kappa)
on the nonsingular points are referred to as isometry classes of nonsingular points when
\kappa
is orthogonal or unitary. For case (R1)
O_{1}
is a
1
-tight set, and we are not able to explicitly determine all such subgroups in this case. For (R2) the parameter of
O_{1}
is given in
[
3
, Lemma 7]
, and the possible group structures of
H|_{W^{\perp}}
will be determined in Section
2.2
. For (R3)
O_{1}
is a
(q^{r-1}+1)
-tight set of
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(2r+1,q)
if
d=2r+2
, and is a
\frac{q^{r-2}-1}{q-1}
-ovoid of
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(2r-1,q)
if
d=2r
. The possible group structures of
H|_{W^{\perp}}
will be determined in Section
2.2
. For (R4) the parameter of
O_{1}
is also given in
[
3
, Lemma 7]
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
V
be a vector space over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
of dimension
d
equipped with a nondegenerate quadratic, unitary or symplectic form
\kappa
, so that the associated polar space
{\mathcal{P}}
has rank
r\geq 2
and is not
Q^{+}(3,q)
. Suppose that
H
is an irreducible subgroup of semisimilarities that has two orbits
O_{1}
,
O_{2}
on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
which are
i_{1}
- and
i_{2}
-tight sets respectively. Then either it occurs in Tables
1.2
-
1.6
, or it is one of the following:

(T1)

H
preserves a decomposition
{\mathcal{D}}:\,V=V_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus V_{t}
with
\dim(V_{i})=m
for each
i
, either
t=d
and we have the cases in Table
1.1
, or
t=2
and we have one of: (a)
{\mathcal{P}}=W(2m-1,q)
and
V_{1}
,
V_{2}
are nondegenerate, (b)
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(2m-1,q)
and
V_{1}
,
V_{2}
are totally singular. For (b), either
\textup{SL}_{m}(q)\unlhd H_{V_{1}}^{V_{1}}
, or
(q,m)=(2,4)
and
A_{7}\unlhd H_{V_{1}}^{V_{1}}
, or
(q,m)\in\{(2,3),(8,3)\}
and
H_{V_{1}}^{V_{1}}\unlhd\Gamma\textup{L}_{1}(q^{3})
.

(T2)

{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(2m-1,q)
,
\{i_{1},i_{2}\}=\{q+1,q^{m-1}-q\}
, and
H
preserves a decomposition
(V,\kappa)=(V_{1},f_{1})\otimes(V_{2},f_{2})
, where
\dim(V_{1})=2
,
\dim(V_{2})=m\geq 4
and
f_{1},f_{2}
are symplectic.

(T3)

{\mathcal{P}}=H(2m-1,q)
,
O_{1}=W(2m-1,q^{1/2})
which is a
(q^{1/2}+1)
-tight set, and
\textup{Sp}_{2m}(q^{1/2})^{\prime}\unlhd H
.

(T4)

{\mathcal{P}}=W(d-1,q)
with
q
even,
H
stabilizes
Q^{+}(d-1,q)
which is a
(q^{d/2-1}+1)
-tight set.

Remark 1.4
.

In the third block of Table
1.3
, we list the Aschbacher class of
H
as a subgroup of the semisimilarity group of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
. For Table
1.5
, Cohen and Cooperstein
[
12
]
showed that
F_{4}(q)
has two orbits on the singular points of
Q(24,q)
in its action on the minimal module, and Cardinali and De Bruyn
[
10
]
constructed the tight sets that arise from spin modules. The remaining two infinite families of tight sets in Table
1.5
seem to be new.

Remark 1.5
.

Below are some comments on the group structure of
H
in Theorem
1.3
.

(1)

The group
H_{V_{1}}^{V_{1}}
in (T1) is the induced action of the stabilizer of
V_{1}
in
H
on the subspace
V_{1}
. We refer the reader to
[
2
, Theorem 3.3]
for more details on the possible structures of
H
for (T1-a).

(2)

We refer the reader to Remark
5.11
for the possible structures of
H
for (T2).

(3)

The group
H
is transitive on both the set of singular points and the set of nonsingular points of
Q^{+}(d-1,q)
for (T4), and we refer the reader to Section
2.2
for the possible structures of
H
for
d\geq 6
.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
V
be a vector space over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
of dimension
d
equipped with a nondegenerate quadratic, unitary or symplectic form
\kappa
, so that the associated polar space
{\mathcal{P}}
has rank
r\geq 2
and is not
Q^{+}(3,q)
. Suppose that
H
is an irreducible subgroup of semisimilarities that has two orbits
O_{1}
,
O_{2}
on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
which are
m_{1}
- and
m_{2}
-ovoids respectively. Then either it occurs in Tables
1.1
,
1.7
,
1.8
and
1.9
, or it is one of the following:

(M1)

{\mathcal{P}}=W(3,3)
,
H=2^{.}\textup{P}\Omega^{-}_{4}(2)\leq N_{\Gamma}(2_{-}^{1+4})
and
m_{1}=m_{2}=2
,

(M2)

{\mathcal{P}}=W(d-1,q)
with
q
even, and one
H
-orbit is an elliptic quadric
Q^{-}(d-1,q)
.

In
[
41
]
, Kelly described a method to obtain intriguing sets of one polar space from those of another with a smaller rank via field reduction. The intriguing sets in high rank polar spaces that do not arise from field reduction are rare. The abundance of examples that show up in our classification indicates that there are highly symmetric intriguing sets in high rank polar spaces yet to be discovered.

Below are some comments on the proofs of Theorems
1.3
and
1.6
. The symplectic case of Theorem
1.3
can also be deduced from
[
47
]
, and for this purpose we need to determine which subgroups in the main theorem of
[
47
]
preserve a nondegenerate symplectic form. We shall do so only for subgroups of Aschbacher classes
{\mathcal{S}}
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{6}
in Propositions
4.1
and
5.14
respectively, and for the remaining geometric classes we will handle the symplectic case together with the orthogonal and unitary cases in a unified manner. Theorem
1.6
follows from
[
23
]
in which the
m
-ovoids of finite classical polar spaces that admit a transitive automorphism group that acts irreducibly on the ambient space are classified. The proof of
[
23
]
relies on the classification of subgroups of classical groups with a large prime divisor in
[
5
]
, and the latter is based on
[
30
]
.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section 2 we first present some basic definitions and group theoretical lemmas, and then handle some specific subgroups of Aschbacher class
{\mathcal{S}}
. Those subgroups arise in the proof of Theorem
1.3
, and we handle them altogether in this section to streamline the proof of Theorem
1.3
. In Section 3, we prove Theorem
1.1
which handles the case where the subgroup
H
is reducible. We present the proof of Theorem
1.3
in the next two sections which deal with the non-geometric and geometric subgroups respectively. In Section 6, we give a short proof of Theorem
1.6
based on the classification results in
[
23
]
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notation and basic definitions

In this subsection, we introduce some basic definitions and the notation that we will use throughout this paper. We use the standard group theory notation as in
[
7
, Section 1.2]
. For a property
P
, we use the Iverson bracket notation
[\![P]\!]
which takes value
1
or
0
according as
P
holds or not.

The classical groups

Let
q=p^{f}
be a prime power with
p
prime, and use
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
to denote the finite field of size
q
. Let
V
be a vector space of dimension
d
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
equipped with a nondegenerate reflexive sesquilinear form or quadratic form
\kappa
. We write
\Gamma\textup{L}(V)
for the group of invertible semilinear transformations of
V
, and write
\textup{PG}(V)
for the projective geometry associated with
V
. Let
{\mathcal{P}}
be the associated polar space of
(V,\kappa)
and write
r
for its rank. We write
\perp
for the polarity of
{\mathcal{P}}
, and assume that
r\geq 2
throughout this paper. We have
2r\leq d\leq 2r+2
, and
d=2r+2
if and only if
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(2r+1,q)
. An
ovoid
of
{\mathcal{P}}
is a set of points which intersects each maximal totally singular or isotropic subspace in exactly one point. We denote the size of a putative ovoid by
\theta_{r}
, which we call the
ovoid number
of
{\mathcal{P}}
. We have
\theta_{r}=q^{d-r-\epsilon}+1
, where
\epsilon=0
,
\frac{1}{2}
or
1
according as
\kappa
is symplectic, unitary or orthogonal, cf. Table
2.1
. The number of points in
{\mathcal{P}}
equals
\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}\cdot\theta_{r}
. We take the same notation for classical groups as in
[
43
]
except for that we regard the ambient space
V
as a vector space over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
also in the unitary case. If
\kappa
is symplectic or unitary, its semisimilarity group
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
is defined as follows:

(2.1)

\Gamma(V,\kappa):=\{g\in\Gamma\textup{L}(V)\mid\kappa(xg,yg)=\lambda(g)\kappa(x,y)^{\sigma(g)}\textup{ for all }x\in V\},

where
\sigma(g)\in\textup{Gal}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}/{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
,
\lambda(g)\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
. If
\kappa
is a quadratic form, the group
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
is defined similarly. We refer the reader to
[
43
, p. 13]
for the definitions of its subgroups
\Delta(V,\kappa)
,
I(V,\kappa)
,
S(V,\kappa)
and
\Omega(V,\kappa)
. We shall write
\Gamma(V)
or
\Gamma
instead of
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
if
(V,\kappa)
is clear from the context. We put the letter P in front of those classical groups for the respective quotient groups modulo their intersections with the center of
\textup{GL}(V)
.

Table 2.1.

r
and their ovoid numbers

Intriguing sets

Take
(V,\kappa)
as above, and write
d=\dim(V)
. Let
r
be the rank of the associated polar space
{\mathcal{P}}
, and write
\theta_{r}
for the ovoid number of
{\mathcal{P}}
as in Table
2.1
. Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a nonempty subset of points of
{\mathcal{P}}
. We say that
{\mathcal{M}}
is an
intriguing set
if there exist some constants
h_{1},h_{2}
such that for each point
P
of
{\mathcal{P}}
we have
|P^{\perp}\cap{\mathcal{M}}|=h_{1}
or
h_{2}
according as
P\in{\mathcal{M}}
or not. By
[
3
, Section 4]
, there are exactly two types of intriguing sets:

(1)

i
-tight sets:
{\mathcal{M}}
has size
i\cdot\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
for some integer
i
, and
h_{1}=q^{r-1}+i\cdot\frac{q^{r-1}-1}{q-1}
,
h_{2}=i\cdot\frac{q^{r-1}-1}{q-1}
;

(2)

m
-ovoids:
{\mathcal{M}}
has size
m\theta_{r}
for some integer
m
, and
h_{1}=(m-1)\theta_{r-1}+1
,
h_{2}=m\theta_{r-1}
.

We call
i
and
m
the parameters of
{\mathcal{M}}
respectively. The set of all the singular points of
{\mathcal{P}}
is both a
\theta_{r}
-tight set and a
\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
-ovoid, which is the trivial intriguing set. We refer the reader to
[
9
, Chapter 2]
and the references therein for the known constructions of intriguing sets in various classical polar spaces.

If a subgroup
H_{0}
of
\textup{P}\Gamma(V,\kappa)
has two orbits
O_{1}
,
O_{2}
on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
, then each
H_{0}
-orbit forms an intriguing set. Suppose further that the two
H_{0}
-orbits are
i_{1}
- and
i_{2}
-tight sets respectively. Then
i_{1}+i_{2}=\theta_{r}
, and
\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
divides the sizes of
O_{1}
and
O_{2}
. Since each
|O_{i}|
divides the order of
H_{0}
, we deduce that
\textup{lcm}(|O_{1}|,|O_{2}|)
divides
|H_{0}|
. In particular, we have the trivial bound
|H_{0}|\geq\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{P}}|
. Recall that
|{\mathcal{P}}|=(q^{d-\epsilon-r}+1)\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
, where
\epsilon=0
,
\frac{1}{2}
or
1
according as
\kappa
is symplectic, unitary or orthogonal. It follows that
|{\mathcal{P}}|>q^{d-1-\epsilon}
, and so
d<1+\epsilon+\log_{q}(2\cdot|H_{0}|)
. We summarize these observations as a lemma for later reference.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{P}}
be a classical polar space of rank
r\geq 2
associated with
(V,\kappa)
, and
set
\epsilon:=0
,
\frac{1}{2}
or
1
according as
{\mathcal{P}}
is symplectic, unitary or orthogonal. Suppose that
H_{0}
is a group of semisimilarities that has two orbits
O_{1}
,
O_{2}
on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
which are respectively
i_{1}
- and
i_{2}
-tight sets. Then

\dim(V)<1+\epsilon+\log_{q}(2\cdot|H_{0}|),

and
\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
divides
\gcd(|O_{1}|,|O_{2}|)
,
\textup{lcm}(|O_{1}|,|O_{2}|)
divides
|H_{0}|
.

The field reduction

Let
b
be a divisor of an integer
d
with
b>1
. Suppose that
V^{\prime}
is a
d/b
-dimensional vector space over the finite field
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}
, equipped with a non-degenerate reflexive sesquilinear or quadratic form
\kappa^{\prime}
. Let
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
be the polar space associated with
(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
. Let
\textup{Tr}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}/{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}
be the relative trace function from the field
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}
to
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. We regard
V^{\prime}
as a
d
-dimensional vector space
V
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. By composing
\kappa^{\prime}
and the relative trace function, we obtain a new form
\kappa
on
V
. Let
{\mathcal{P}}
be the polar space defined by
(V,\kappa)
. Such a process is called
field reduction
. In Table
2.2
, we list all the cases that can be obtained by iteratively composing the field reductions in
[
43
, Table 4.3A]
. We refer the reader to
[
27
]
for restrictions on
\alpha
in rows 11, 12 of Table
2.2
. The table also lists the sizes of
{\mathcal{P}}
and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
for reference. The following group

(2.2)

\Gamma^{\#}(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})=\{g\in\Gamma(V,\kappa)\mid\alpha^{1-\sigma(g)}\lambda(g)\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}\}

is a subgroup of
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
of Aschbacher class
{\mathcal{C}}_{5}
, where we set
\alpha:=1
by default if there is no parameter
\alpha
in the corresponding row in Table
2.2
. For later use, we shall need the following subset of
{\mathcal{P}}
:

(2.3)

{\mathcal{M}}_{1}=\{\langle\eta v\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}\mid\eta\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}^{*},\,\langle v\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}\}.

The weak equivalence of representations

Let
\rho_{1}:\,G\rightarrow\textup{GL}_{n}(q)
be the representation of a finite group
G
that is afforded by a
G
-module
V_{1}
with respect to a chosen basis. Take a field automorphism
\theta
of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
and a group automorphism
\alpha\in\textup{Aut}(G)
. For a vector
v
and a matrix
m
, we write
v^{\theta}
and
m^{\theta}
for the vector and the matrix obtained by applying
\theta
to their entries respectively. We define
V_{1}^{\theta}
as the
G
-module with the same ambient space as
V_{1}
and
G
-action
v.g=v(g\rho_{1})^{\theta}
, and let
\rho_{1}^{\theta}
for the corresponding representation. Similarly, let
{}^{\alpha}V_{1}
be the
G
-module with the same ambient space as
V_{1}
and
G
-action
v.g=v(g^{\alpha})\rho_{1}
, and write
{}^{\alpha}\rho_{1}
for the corresponding representation. Let
V_{2}
be another
G
-module. If
V_{2}
is equivalent to
{}^{\alpha}V_{1}
for some
\alpha\in\textup{Aut}(G)
, then we say that
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are
quasi-equivalent
. If
V_{2}
is equivalent to
V_{1}^{\theta}
for some field automorphism
\theta
, then we say that
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are
algebraically conjugate
. If
V_{2}
is equivalent to one of the
G
-modules obtained from
V_{1}
by recursive applications of group automorphisms, field automorphisms and duality, then we say that
V_{1}
is
weakly equivalent
to
V_{2}
.

Suppose that two
G
-modules
V_{1}
,
V_{2}
are weakly equivalent and
V_{1}
has a nondegenerate
G
-invariant reflexive sesquilinear or quadratic form
\kappa_{1}
. It is routine to show that there is a corresponding nondegenerate
G
-invariant form
\kappa_{2}
on
V_{2}
such that
(V_{1},\kappa_{1})
and
(V_{2},\kappa_{2})
define the same classical polar space
{\mathcal{P}}
, and
G
has the same orbit structure on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
. Let
\rho_{i}:\,G\rightarrow\textup{GL}(V_{i})
be the respective representations for
i=1,~2
. Suppose further that
G\rho_{1}
is a subgroup of
\Gamma(V_{1},\kappa_{1})
of Aschbacher class
{\mathcal{S}}
that has two orbits on the singular points of
{\mathcal{P}}
. Then
G\rho_{2}
is also a subgroup of
\Gamma(V_{2},\kappa_{2})
of Aschbacher class
{\mathcal{S}}
with the same properties.

Primitive prime divisors

Let
n
,
k
be positive integers. A prime divisor
p
of
n^{k}-1
is called
primitive
if
p
divides none of
n^{i}-1
,
1\leq i\leq k-1
. For a primitive prime divisor
p
of
n^{k}-1
, we have
\textup{ord}_{p}(n)=k
, where we write
\textup{ord}_{p}(n)
for the multiplicative order of
n
modulo
p
. If
k=2
, then each odd prime divisor of
n+1
is a primitive divisor of
n^{2}-1
by the fact that
\gcd(n+1,n-1)
is at most
2
. It follows that
n^{2}-1
has no primitive prime divisor only if
n+1
is a power of
2
. For
k\geq 3
, we have the following classical result.

Lemma 2.2
.

[
68
]

Let
n
and
k
be positive integers such that
n>1
,
k\geq 3
and
(n,k)\neq(2,6)
. Then
n^{k}-1
has at least one primitive prime divisor.

2.2.
Some group theoretic lemmas

Suppose that
V
is a vector space of dimension
d
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
equipped with a nondegenerate form
\kappa
, and write
{\mathcal{P}}
for the associated polar space. We call an
I(V,\kappa)
-orbit on the nonsingular points an isometry class of nonsingular points. There are at most two such classes of nonsingular points, and there is exactly one class only if either
\kappa
is unitary, or
\kappa
is orthogonal and
q
is even. When
q
is odd, let
\eta
be the quadratic character of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
such that
\eta(a)=1
or
-1
according as
a
is a square or nonsquare. If
\kappa
is orthogonal and
q
is odd, then each class of nonsingular points has the same value
\eta(\kappa(v))
for its element
\langle v\rangle
. If
d
is odd, then the two classes have different sizes. If
d
is even, the two classes have the same sizes and are swapped by a similarity. In
[
28
]
the authors classified all groups of semisimilarities that act transitively on the set of all subspaces of a given isometry type, where the subspaces are either nondegenerate or totally singular. We shall need the classification of subgroups of
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
that are transitive on both the set of singular points and one isometry class of nonsingular points for the proofs of our main theorems. We determine such subgroups of the unitary and orthogonal groups in the next few lemmas.

Lemma 2.3
.

Suppose that
V
is a vector space over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
with a nondegenerate Hermitian form
\kappa
, where
q
is a square and
d=\dim(V)\geq 2
. Let
H
be a group of semisimilarities that is transitive on both the singular points and the nonsingular points. Then either
\textup{SU}_{d}(q^{1/2})\leq H
, or
(d,q)=(3,2^{2})
and
H=3_{+}^{1+2}\rtimes C_{8}
, or
(d,q)=(2,2^{2})
.

Proof.

The group
\textup{SU}_{d}(q^{1/2})
is transitive on both the singular points and the nonsingular points by
[
43
, Lemma 2.10.5]
. Assume that
H
does not contain
\textup{SU}_{d}(q^{1/2})
. Let
n
be the least common multiple of the numbers of singular and nonsingular points. Then
n
divides
|H|
. First suppose that
d\geq 3
. Since
H
is transitive on the singular points, by
[
28
, Theorem 4.1 (b)]

(d,q)
is one of
(3,2^{2})
,
(3,3^{2})
,
(3,5^{2})
,
(3,8^{2})
,
(4,3^{2})
and
(9,2^{2})
. Since
H
is transitive on the nonsingular points, either
(d,q)=(3,2^{2})
or
d
is even by
[
28
, Theorem 4.1 (a)]
. Therefore,
(d,q)
is either
(3,2^{2})
or
(4,3^{2})
. For
(d,q)=(3,2^{2})
, we have
H=3_{+}^{1+2}\rtimes C_{8}
; for
(d,q)=(4,3^{2})
, there is no such subgroup
H
by examining all the subgroups whose orders are multiples of
n
by Magma
[
6
]
. Next suppose that
d=2
. By
[
28
, Theorem 3.7 (b)]
, we deduce that
q=2^{2}
or
16^{2}
. For
q=16^{2}
, the groups of semisimilarities whose orders are multiples of
n
all contain
\textup{SU}_{2}(16)
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

Suppose that
(V,\kappa)
is a quadratic space of dimension
d\geq 3
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, where
qd
is odd. Let
H
be a group of semisimilarities that is transitive on both the set of singular points and one isometry class of nonsingular points. Then either
\Omega_{d}(q)\unlhd H
, or
d=7
and
G_{2}(q)\unlhd H
. Moreover,
H^{\infty}
is transitive on both isometry classes of nonsingular points in both cases.

Proof.

The group
\Omega_{d}(q)
is transitive on both the set of singular points and each isometry class of nonsingular points by
[
43
, Lemma 2.10.5]
. Suppose that
H
does not contain
\Omega_{d}(q)
. By
[
28
, Theorem 3.3]

H
is transitive on one class of nonsingular points only if it fixes a singular point for
d=3
, so
d\neq 3
. By
[
28
, Theorem 5.3]

H
is transitive on one class of nonsingular points only if it fixes a totally singular line for
d=5
, so
d\neq 5
. By
[
28
, Theorem 7.1]
,
H
is transitive on the set of singular points only if
(d,H^{\infty})=(7,G_{2}(q))
for
d\geq 7
. There is a unique conjugacy class of
G_{2}(q)
in the isometry group by
[
7
, Table 8.40]
, and it is transitive on both classes of nonsingular points by
[
28
, Theorem 7.1]
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.5
.

Suppose that
(V,\kappa)
is an elliptic quadratic space and
\dim(V)=2m\geq 4
. Let
H
be a subgroup of semisimilarities that is transitive on both the set of singular points and one isometry class of nonsingular points. Then either
\Omega^{-}_{2m}(q)\unlhd H
, or one of the following occurs:

(1)

m
is odd, and
\textup{SU}_{m}(q)\unlhd H
,

(2)

m=2
,
q=2
and
H=\textup{GO}_{2}^{-}(4).2
, or
q=4
and
\textup{GO}_{2}^{-}(4^{2}).4\unlhd H
,

(3)

m=3
,
q=2
and
3_{+}^{1+2}\unlhd H
, or
q=3
and
2.\textup{PSL}_{3}(4)\unlhd H
.

For
(m,q)=(3,3)
, the normalizer of
2.\textup{PSL}_{3}(4)
in
\Delta(V)
is transitive on all nonsingular points.

Proof.

If either
\Omega_{2m}^{-}(q)\leq H
, or
m
is odd and
\textup{SU}_{m}(q)\unlhd H
, then
H
transitive on both the set of singular points and one class of nonsingular points by
[
43
, Lemma 2.10.5]
. Suppose that
H
is in neither of the above two cases. By examining the
(m,q)
pairs that admit transitive
H
-action on the singular points and one class of nonsingular points in
[
28
, Theorems 3.4, 4.2, 6.2]
, we deduce that
(m,q)
is one of
(2,3)
,
(2,2)
,
(2,4)
,
(3,2)
and
(3,3)
. For each
(m,q)
pair, we examine the orbits of all groups of semisimilarities whose orders are multiples of the least common multiple of the numbers of singular points and nonsingular points in one class by Magma
[
6
]
.
It turns out that
H=\textup{GO}_{2}^{-}(4).2
for
(m,q)=(2,2)
,
\textup{GO}_{2}^{-}(4^{2}).4\unlhd H
for
(m,q)=(2,4)
,
3_{+}^{1+2}\unlhd H
for
(m,q)=(3,2)
,
2.\textup{PSL}_{3}(4)\unlhd H
for
(m,q)=(3,2)
, and there is no such subgroup
H
for
(m,q)=(2,3)
. The last claim is verified by Magma. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

Suppose that
(V,\kappa)
is a hyperbolic quadratic space and
\dim(V)=2m\geq 6
. Let
H
be a subgroup of semisimilarities that is transitive on both the set of singular points and one isometry class of nonsingular points. Then either
\Omega^{+}_{2m}(q)\unlhd H
, or one of the following occurs:

(1)

m
is even, and
H^{(\infty)}=\textup{SU}_{m}(q)
,

(2)

m=4
,
H^{(\infty)}=\textup{Spin}_{7}(q)
which is irreducible on
V
,

(3)

(m,q)=(4,2)
, and
H^{\infty}=A_{9}
.

Moreover, the normalizer of
H^{\infty}
in
\Gamma(V)
is transitive on the set of all nonsingular points in each case.

Proof.

If
H
contains either
\Omega^{+}_{2m}(q)
or
\textup{SU}_{m}(q)
(
m
even), then it is transitive on both the set of singular points and each class of nonsingular points by
[
43
, Lemma 2.10.5]
. Assume that
H
is in neither of the two cases. The group
H
is transitive on the singular points, so by
[
28
, Theorem 8.4]
we have the following possibilities: (a)
m=4
and
H^{\infty}=\textup{Spin}_{7}(q)
which is irreducible on
V
, (b)
(m,q)
is one of
(3,2)
,
(4,2)
,
(4,3)
. For case (a), there is a unique conjugacy class of irreducible
\textup{Spin}_{7}(q)
in
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
by
[
7
, Table 8.50]
and
[
44
]
. The claims on transitivity for this case will be established in Proposition
2.21
below. Suppose that
H
is in case (b) and is not covered by (a). If
(m,q)=(3,2)
, then we check by Magma
[
6
]
that
H
is transitive on the singular points only if
H^{\infty}=A_{7}
but the normalizer of
A_{7}
is not transitive on the nonsingular points. If
(m,q)=(4,2)
, then
H
is transitive on both singular and nonsingular points only if
H^{\infty}=A_{9}
. If
(m,q)=(4,3)
, then there is no such subgroup
H
. The last claim is verified by Magma. This completes the proof.
∎

We shall also need the following result in the symplectic case.

Proposition 2.7
.

Suppose that
(V,\kappa)
is a symplectic space, and
\dim(V)=2m\geq 4
. Let
H
be a group of semisimilarities that is transitive on both the isotropic points and the totally isotropic lines. Then either
\textup{Sp}_{2m}(q)^{\prime}\unlhd H
, or
(m,q)=(2,3)
and
2_{-}^{1+4}.A_{5}\unlhd H
.

Proof.

The group
\textup{Sp}_{2m}(q)
has the desired property by Witt’s Lemma
[
43
, Proposition 2.1.6]
, and
\textup{Sp}_{4}(2)^{\prime}
also has the property (this was checked by using a computer). Assume that
H
does not contain
\textup{Sp}_{2m}(q)^{\prime}
. Since
H
is transitive on the set of totally singular lines, we have
m=2
by
[
28
, Theorem 5.2]
. Since it is also transitive on the isotropic points, we have
q=3
by the same theorem. There are
40
isotropic points and
40
totally isotropic lines in this case. We check by Magma that
2_{-}^{1+4}.A_{5}\unlhd H
by examining all the subgroups whose orders are divisible by
40
. This completes the proof.
∎

2.3.
The adjoint module

Suppose that
G
is one of
\textup{SL}_{3}(q)
,
\textup{SU}_{3}(q)
and
\textup{SU}_{4}(q)
. We set
{\mathbb{F}}={\mathbb{F}}_{q}
or
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}
respectively according as
G
is linear or unitary, and let
\sigma
be the field automorphism
x\mapsto x^{q}
of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}
. Let
M_{n}({\mathbb{F}})
be the set of all
n\times n
matrices over
{\mathbb{F}}
, and for
1\leq i,j\leq n
write
E_{ij}
for the
0
-
1
matrix of order
n
whose only nonzero entry is the
(i,j)
-th. We regard
G
as a matrix group; in particular,
\textup{SU}_{n}(q)=\{g\in\textup{SL}_{n}(q^{2})\mid gg^{\sigma\top}=I_{n}\}
. The group
G
acts on
M_{n}({\mathbb{F}})
via
X.g=g^{-1}Xg
for
g\in G
,
X\in M_{n}({\mathbb{F}})
. For
\textup{SL}_{n}(q)
set
M=M_{n}({\mathbb{F}})
, and for
\textup{SU}_{n}(q)
set
M=\{X\in M_{n}({\mathbb{F}})\mid X^{\top}=X^{\sigma}\}
. Let
U
be the subspace of
M
consisting of the matrices of trace
0
, and let
U^{\prime}
be the subspace consisting of scalar matrices in
M
. For
A\in U
, define
Q(A)=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(A_{ij}A_{ji}-A_{ii}A_{jj})
. Note that
Q(A)
is the negative of the coefficient of
x^{2}
in the characteristic polynomial
\det(xI_{n}-A)
of
A
, so
Q
is
G
-invariant. The adjoint module of
G
is
V:=U/(U\cap U^{\prime})
, which has dimension
n^{2}-1-[\![p\mid n]\!]
. For
X\in U
, we write
\overline{X}
for its image in
V
. The form
Q
induces a nondegenerate
G
-invariant form
\kappa
on
V
, and we have
\kappa=Q
if
p
does not divide
n
. We refer the reader to
[
7
, Section 5.4.1]
and
[
56
, Section 8.4]
for more details on the adjoint modules.

Since the nonzero scalar matrices acts on
V
trivially, we have an embedding of
L:=G/Z(G)
in
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
, where
Z(G)
is the center of
G
. Let
\phi
be the field automorphism
x\mapsto x^{p}
of
{\mathbb{F}}
applied to matrices entrywise, and define
X.(-\top)=X^{\top}
for
X\in M
. The normalizer
K
of
L
in
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
is the induced action of
\langle\textup{GL}^{\pm}_{n}(q),\phi,-\top\rangle
on
V
, where
\textup{GL}^{+}=\textup{GL}
and
\textup{GL}^{-}=\textup{GU}
.

Proposition 2.8
.

If
G=\textup{SL}_{3}(q)
, then
K
has at least three orbits on the singular points for
p\neq 3
, and
G
has two orbits which are
(q+1)
- and
(q^{3}-q)
-tight sets of
Q(6,q)
respectively for
p=3
.

Proof.

The vectors
v_{1}=E_{12}
,
v_{2}=E_{12}+E_{23}
and
v_{3}=E_{12}+E_{23}+E_{31}
in
U
are singular vectors for
Q
. They have different ranks, so
\langle v_{1}\rangle
,
\langle v_{2}\rangle
and
\langle v_{3}\rangle
are not in the same
K
-orbits. This establishes the claim for
p\neq 3
. Suppose that
p=3
, so that
(V,\kappa)
is parabolic. It is straightforward to show that the stabilizer of
\langle\overline{v_{1}}\rangle
in
\textup{SL}_{3}(q)
consists of
\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&a&b\\
0&\mu&0\\
0&c&\lambda^{-1}\mu^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
with
\lambda,\mu\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
and
a,b,c\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, so its
\textup{SL}_{3}(q)
-orbits has size
(q+1)\frac{q^{3}-1}{q-1}
. Also, the stabilizer of
\langle\overline{v_{2}}\rangle
in
\textup{SL}_{3}(q)
consists of
\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&b&c\\
0&1&\lambda^{-1}b\\
0&0&\lambda^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
with
\lambda\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
and
b,c\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, so its
\textup{SL}_{3}(q)
-orbit has size
(q^{3}-q)\frac{q^{3}-1}{q-1}
. The two orbits comprises all the singular points by comparing sizes, and so they are intriguing sets by Section
2.1
.
Since the ovoid number
q^{3}+1
does not divide their sizes, they are
both tight sets. This establishes the claim for
p=3
.
∎

Proposition 2.9
.

If
G=\textup{SU}_{3}(q)
, then
K
has at least three orbits on the singular points if
p\neq 3
, and
G
has exactly two orbits one of which is Kantor’s unitary ovoid if
p=3
.

Proof.

For the case
p=3
, we refer the reader to
[
40
, Section 4]
. If
q\equiv 2\pmod{3}
, then there are three
K
-orbits which are
1
-,
(q^{2}+q)
- and
q^{3}
-ovoids of
Q^{+}(7,q)
respectively, cf.
[
40
, Section 4]
and
[
23
, Example 3.9]
. If
q\equiv 1\pmod{3}
, then
Q
has minus sign, cf.
[
7
, Table 5.6]
. The ovoid number is
q^{4}+1
and the rank is
r=3
. As in
[
40
, Section 4]
, we deduce that there is a
K
-orbit
{\mathcal{N}}
of size
q^{3}+1
on the singular points that correspond to the rank-
1
matrices in
U
. Since the size of
{\mathcal{N}}
is not divisible by
q^{4}+1
or
\frac{q^{3}-1}{q-1}
, it is not an intriguing set. We conclude that there are more than two orbits for
q\equiv 1\pmod{3}
, cf. Section
2.1
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 2.10
.

If
G=\textup{SU}_{4}(q)
, then
K
has at least three orbits on the singular points.

Proof.

Let
Y
be the set of rank-
1
matrices in
U
. Then
Y=\{\lambda vv^{\sigma\top}\mid\lambda\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*},\,v^{\sigma\top}v=0\}
, which has size
(q-1)(q^{2}+1)(q^{3}+1)
and forms a
K
-orbit of singular vectors of
Q
. Since the difference of two rank-
1
matrices has rank at most
2
, we deduce that
\{y+\lambda I_{4}\mid\lambda\in{\mathbb{F}}\}
’s with
y\in Y
are pairwise distinct. It follows that elements of
Y
yield a
K
-orbit
{\mathcal{N}}
of singular points of size
(q^{2}+1)(q^{3}+1)
. Suppose to the contrary that
K
has two orbits on the singular points. Then
{\mathcal{N}}
is an intriguing set by Section
2.1
. If
q
is odd, then the associated polar space has rank
r=7
and its ovoid number is
\theta_{r}=q^{7}+1
. If
q=2^{f}
, then
Q
has plus sign if and only if
f
is even by
[
56
, Corollary 8.4.6]
. If
f
is even, then the rank
r=7
and the ovoid number is
\theta_{r}=q^{6}+1
; if
f
is odd, then the rank
r=6
and the ovoid number is
\theta_{r}=q^{7}+1
. The size of
{\mathcal{N}}
is divisible by neither
\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
nor
\theta_{r}
in each case: a contradiction. This completes the proof.
∎

2.4.
The symmetric and antisymmetric squares

Suppose that
G=\textup{Sp}_{2m}(q)
with
m\geq 2
. Let
W
be its natural module of dimension
2m
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, and let
\kappa_{1}
be a nondegenerate
G
-invariant symplectic form on
W
. We choose a symplectic basis
e_{1},\ldots,e_{2m}
of
W
, i.e.,
\kappa_{1}(e_{i},e_{j})=1
if
j=i+m
and
\kappa_{1}(e_{i},e_{j})=0
if
j-i\neq\pm m
. We identify
W
with
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{2m}
and regard
G
as a matrix group with respect to this basis. Then
G=\{g\in\textup{GL}_{n}(q)\mid gJg^{\top}=J\}
, where
J
is the matrix of order
2m
whose
(i,j)
-th entry is
\kappa_{1}(e_{i},e_{j})
. We define the similarity
\delta=\textup{diag}(w,\ldots,w,1,\ldots,1)
for a primitive element
w
of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, and define
\phi
as the field automorphism
a\mapsto a^{p}
of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
applied to the vectors of
W
entrywise. We refer the reader to
[
7
, Section 5.2.1]
for the definitions of the symmetric square
S^{2}(W)
and antisymmetric square
\wedge^{2}(W)
of
W
.

Remark 2.11
.

Let
M
be the set of skew-symmetric matrices of order
2m
with zero diagonal entries over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. There is a vector space endomorphism

\psi:\,\wedge^{2}(W)\rightarrow M
,
u\wedge v\mapsto u^{\top}v-v^{\top}u

by the universality of exterior squares
[
52
]
. It maps a nonzero element
u\wedge v
to a rank-
2
skew-symmetric matrix whose row space is
\langle u,v\rangle
. By linear algebra each element of
M
is the sum of rank-
2
skew-symmetric matrices, so
\psi
is surjective. It is an isomorphism by comparing dimensions. For
x\in\wedge^{2}(W)
we define its rank as that of
\psi(x)
. We equip
M
with the
G
-action
X.g=g^{\top}Xg
, so that
\psi
is a
G
-module isomorphism. We can similarly build a
G
-module isomorphism between
S^{2}(W)
and the set of symmetric matrices of order
2m
via
uv\mapsto\frac{1}{2}(u^{\top}v+v^{\top}u)
for
q
odd, so that it makes sense to talk about the ranks of its elements.

For
q
odd, there is a nondegenerate
G
-invariant symmetric form
B
on
S^{2}(W)
such that

B(u_{1}u_{2},v_{1}v_{2})=\kappa_{1}(u_{1},v_{1})\kappa_{1}(u_{2},v_{2})+\kappa_{1}(u_{1},v_{2})\kappa_{1}(u_{2},v_{1})

by the universality of symmetric squares. Let
\kappa_{S}
be the quadratic form on
S^{2}(W)
such that
\kappa_{S}(x)=\frac{1}{2}B(x,x)
. The action of
G
on
S^{2}(W)
induces an embedding of
L=\textup{PSp}_{2m}(q)
in the isometry group of
\kappa_{S}
, and let
K
be the normalizer of
L
in the semisimilarity group. The group
K
is generated by the induced action of
\langle G,\delta,\phi\rangle
on
S^{2}(W)
.

Proposition 2.12
.

If
G=\textup{Sp}_{4}(q)
with
q
odd, then
K
has at least three orbits on the singular points of
S^{2}(W)
.

Proof.

The vectors
v_{1}=e_{1}^{2}
,
v_{2}=e_{1}e_{2}
,
v_{3}=e_{1}e_{2}+e_{3}e_{3}
are singular and have different ranks. We deduce that three corresponding projective points are in different
K
-orbits. This completes the proof.
∎

Suppose that
G=\textup{Sp}_{6}(q)
from now on. There is a nondegenerate
G
-invariant symmetric bilinear form
\beta_{1}
on
\wedge^{2}(W)
such that

\beta_{1}(u_{1}\wedge v_{1},u_{2}\wedge v_{2})=\kappa_{1}(u_{1},u_{2})\kappa_{1}(v_{1},v_{2})-\kappa_{1}(u_{1},v_{2})\kappa_{1}(v_{1},u_{2}).

By the universality of exterior squares
[
52
, p. 5]
, there is an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear map
\ell:\,\wedge^{2}(W)\rightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
such that
\ell(u\wedge v)=\kappa_{1}(u,v)
. Since
\beta_{1}
is nondegenerate, there is a unique element
h\in\wedge^{2}(W)
such that
\beta_{1}(h,u\wedge v)=\kappa_{1}(u,v)
for
u,v\in V
. The uniqueness implies that
h
is
G
-invariant. It is routine to check that
h=e_{1}\wedge e_{4}+e_{2}\wedge e_{5}+e_{3}\wedge e_{6}
. Let
U^{\prime}
be the subspace spanned by
h
, and set
U:=\{x\in\wedge^{2}(W)\mid\beta_{1}(h,x)=0\}
,
V:=U/(U\cap U^{\prime})
. Since
\beta_{1}(h,h)=3
,
U^{\prime}
is contained in
U
if and only if
p=3
. The module
V
is absolutely irreducible, cf. the proof of
[
50
, Theorem 5.1]
. By
[
56
, Section 9.3]
,
V
is not realized over a proper subfield and has a nondegenerate quadratic form
\kappa
. For
x\in U
, we write
\bar{x}
for its image in
V
. Then
\beta(\bar{x},\bar{y}):=\beta_{1}(x,y)
is the associated bilinear form of
\kappa
by rescaling
\kappa
properly. The action of
G
on
V
induces an embedding of
L=\textup{PSp}_{6}(q)
in the isometry group of
(V,\kappa)
, and let
K
be the normalizer of
L
in the semisimilarity group. The group
K
is generated by the induced action of
\langle G,\delta,\phi\rangle
on
V
.

Proposition 2.13
.

If
G=\textup{Sp}_{6}(q)
, then
K
has at least three orbits on the singular points of
V
for
p\neq 3
, and
G
has exactly two orbits which are
(q^{2}+1)
- and
(q^{6}-q^{2})
-tight sets of
Q(12,q)
for
p=3
.

Proof.

Take a nonzero vector
u\wedge v
in
U
, so that
\beta_{1}(h,u\wedge v)=\kappa_{1}(u,v)=0
. We claim that
\kappa(\overline{u\wedge v})=0
. This is clear for
q
odd since
\kappa(\overline{u\wedge v})=\frac{1}{2}\beta_{1}(u\wedge v,u\wedge v)=0
, so assume that
q
is even. Take a vector
w
of
W
that is perpendicular to
\langle u,v\rangle
and not in
\langle u,v\rangle
. By Witt’s Lemma, there are elements of
\textup{Sp}_{6}(q)
that map the pair
(u,v)
to
(w,v)
,
(u+w,v)
respectively. By the
G
-invariance of
\kappa
, the vectors
\overline{(u+w)\wedge v}
,
\overline{u\wedge v}
and
\overline{w\wedge v}
have the same
\kappa
-value
c
. We expand
\kappa(\overline{u\wedge v}+\overline{w\wedge v})=c
to obtain
c=0
. This establishes the claim.

For
p\neq 3
,
e_{1}\wedge e_{2}
,
e_{1}\wedge e_{2}+e_{3}\wedge e_{4}
,
e_{1}\wedge e_{2}+e_{3}\wedge e_{4}+e_{5}\wedge e_{6}
are singular vectors in
U
and have different ranks. Therefore, there are at least three
K
-orbits on the singular points for
p\neq 3
. Suppose that
p=3
in the sequel. We define
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}:=\{\langle\overline{u\wedge v}\rangle\mid\kappa_{1}(u,v)=0\}
which is
\textup{Sp}_{6}(q)
-transitive and consists of singular points. There is a bijection between elements of
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
and the totally singular lines of
W
, so
|{\mathcal{N}}_{1}|=(q^{2}+1)\frac{q^{6}-1}{q-1}
. We set
z:=e_{1}\wedge e_{2}+e_{3}\wedge e_{4}
, whose row space is
\langle e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}\rangle
. Its rank is
4
and
z+\lambda h
has rank
6
for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
, so the
\textup{Sp}_{6}(q)
-orbit of
\langle\bar{z}\rangle
has the same size as that of
\langle z\rangle
. The singular vectors in
U
with row space
\langle e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}\rangle
are of the form
x=e_{2}\wedge(ae_{1}+ce_{4})+e_{3}\wedge(be_{1}+de_{4}+ke_{2})
, where
a,b,c,d,k
are elements of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
such that
D:=ad-bc\neq 0
. In particular,
x=\lambda z
if we specify
-a=d=\lambda\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
and
b=c=k=0
. By Witt’s Lemma there is an element
g\in\textup{Sp}_{6}(q)
such that

g:\,(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4})\mapsto(ae_{1}+ce_{4},e_{2},De_{3},D^{-1}(be_{1}+de_{4}+ke_{2})),

and it maps
z
to
x
. Therefore, those
(q^{4}-q^{2})(q-1)
singular vectors with row space
\langle e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}\rangle
are in the
\textup{Sp}_{6}(q)
-orbit of
z
. The stabilizer of the subspace
\langle e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}\rangle
in
\textup{Sp}_{6}(q)
has size
q^{9}(q^{2}-1)^{2}(q-1)
, so that its
\textup{Sp}_{6}(q)
-orbit has size
(q^{2}+1)\frac{q^{6}-1}{q-1}
. We thus deduce that the
\textup{Sp}_{6}(q)
-orbit
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
of
\langle\bar{z}\rangle
has size
(q^{6}-q^{2})\frac{q^{6}-1}{q-1}
. Since
|{\mathcal{N}}_{1}|+|{\mathcal{N}}_{2}|=\frac{q^{12}-1}{q-1}
, we deduce that
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
are the only two
\textup{Sp}_{6}(q)
-orbits on the singular points. By Section
2.1
they are both intriguing sets of
Q(12,q)
. The ovoid number
q^{6}+1
does not divide their sizes, so
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
are tight sets rather than
m
-ovoids. This completes the proof.
∎

2.5.
The antisymmetric cube

Suppose that
G=\textup{SU}_{6}(q)
, where
q=p^{f}
with
p
prime. Let
W
be the natural
G
-module over
{\mathbb{F}}={\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}
, and write
\kappa_{1}
for the associated unitary form on
W
. We choose an orthonormal basis
e_{1},\ldots,e_{6}
of
W
such that
\kappa_{1}(e_{i},e_{j})=[\![i=j]\!]
, and identify
W
with
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}^{6}
with respect to this basis. Let
\phi
be the field automorphism
a\mapsto a^{p}
of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}
, and set
\sigma=\phi^{f}
. Then
G=\{g\in\textup{GL}_{6}(q^{2})\mid gg^{\sigma\top}=I_{6}\}
, where
I_{6}
is the identity matrix of order
6
. Write
X:=\{1,\ldots,6\}
, and let
\binom{X}{3}
be the set of all
3
-subsets of
X
. Let
U
be the antisymmetric cube
\wedge^{3}(W)
. For a
3
-subset
I=\{i,j,k\}
of
X
with
i<j<k
, write both
e_{I}
and
e_{ijk}
for
e_{i}\wedge e_{j}\wedge e_{k}
. Then
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}:=\{e_{I}\mid I\in\binom{X}{3}\}
is a basis of
U
.

Remark 2.14
.

We shall work inside the exterior algebra
\wedge(W)
of
W
, cf.
[
52
]
. For each
i
-dimensional subspace
W_{1}=\langle u_{1},\ldots,u_{i}\rangle
of
W
, it corresponds to a
1
-dimensional subspace
\langle u_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge u_{i}\rangle
of
\wedge^{i}(W)
by
[
52
, p. 91]
. The latter is independent of the choice of the basis of
W_{1}
, so we denote it by
\langle\det(W_{1})\rangle
.

Set
z:=e_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{6}
. For
x,y\in U
, we define
\beta(x,y)
to be the coefficient of
z
in
x\wedge y
. It is a nondegenerate alternating form, and
\beta(e_{I},e_{J})
is nonzero if and only if
I\cap J=\emptyset
for
e_{I},e_{J}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
. For
g\in G
, we have
z.g=e_{1}g\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{6}g=\det(g)z
, cf.
[
52
, p. 91]
. It follows that
\beta
is
G
-invariant.

Lemma 2.15
.

There is a
G
-invariant Hermitian form
\beta^{\prime}
on
U
such that

\beta^{\prime}(u_{1}\wedge u_{2}\wedge u_{3},v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge v_{3})=\det\left(\begin{pmatrix}\kappa_{1}(u_{i},v_{j})\end{pmatrix}_{1\leq i,j\leq 3}\right).

Proof.

We write
U^{*}
for the dual of
U
. We define
f:\,W^{6}\rightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}
such that

f(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})=\det\left(\begin{pmatrix}\kappa_{1}(v_{i},u_{j})\end{pmatrix}_{1\leq i,j\leq 3}\right).

Since
f
is alternating multilinear in the last three coordinates, for each tuple
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
there is a well-defined element
\ell(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\in U^{*}
that maps
v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge v_{3}
to
f(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})
by the universality of exterior powers
[
52
]
. The mapping
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\mapsto\sigma\circ\ell(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
is alternating multilinear, so there is a well-defined mapping
\psi:\,U\rightarrow U^{*}
such that
\psi(u_{1}\wedge u_{2}\wedge u_{3})=\sigma\circ\ell(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
. We thus have a mapping
\beta^{\prime}:\,U\times U\rightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}
such that

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}

\displaystyle(u_{1}\wedge u_{2}\wedge u_{3},v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge v_{3})=\psi(u_{1}\wedge u_{2}\wedge u_{3})(v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge v_{3})

\displaystyle=f(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})^{\sigma}=\det\left(\begin{pmatrix}\kappa_{1}(u_{i},v_{j})\end{pmatrix}_{1\leq i,j\leq 3}\right).

The form
\beta^{\prime}
is a nondegenerate
G
-invariant Hermitian form on
U
. This completes the proof.
∎

In the sequel, we assume that
q
is even. Let
k
be the algebraic closure of
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
. Then
U\otimes k
is the restriction of the irreducible
\textup{SL}_{6}(k)
-module
L(p^{i}\omega_{3})
for some
i
by
[
50
]
. We deduce that
U
can be realized over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
but no smaller fields by
[
7
, Theorem 5.1.13]
. Take
\eta\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}\setminus{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
such that
\eta^{q+1}=1
. Let
V
be the
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-span of the following basis of
U
:

(2.4)

{\mathcal{B}}=\left\{e_{I}+e_{X\setminus I},\frac{1}{1+\eta}e_{I}+\frac{\eta}{1+\eta}e_{X\setminus I}\mid I\in\binom{X}{3},1\in I\right\}.

It follows that
V\otimes{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}=U
, and for
\sum_{I}a_{I}e_{I}\in V
we have
a_{X\setminus I}=a_{I}^{q}
for each
I\in\binom{X}{3}
.

Lemma 2.16
.

The
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-space
V
is
G
-invariant.

Proof.

Let
\rho:\,G\rightarrow\textup{GL}_{20}(q^{2})
be the matrix representation of
g\in G
afforded by
U
with respect to the basis
{\mathcal{B}}
. We claim that
g\rho
has entries in
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
for
g\in G
, so that the
G
-invariance of
V
follows. Let
\beta^{\prime}
be the unitary form on
U
in Lemma
2.15
. The Gram matrices of the forms
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
with respect to the basis
{\mathcal{B}}
in (
2.4
) coincide and are block diagonal with diagonal entries
\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
, which we denote by
B
. Then
\left((g\rho)^{\sigma}\right)^{\top}B(g\rho)=B
,
(g\rho)^{\top}B(g\rho)=B
, from which we deduce that
(g\rho)^{\sigma}=g\rho
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.17
.

For
p=2
,
there is a nondegenerate
G
-invariant quadratic form
\kappa
on
V
whose associated bilinear form is the restriction of
\beta
to
V
.

Proof.

We define the following quadratic form
Q
on
U
: for
x=\sum_{I}x_{I}e_{I}\in W
we set
Q(x)=\sum_{I}^{\prime}x_{I}x_{X\setminus I}
, where the summation
\sum^{\prime}
is taken over the
3
-subsets of
X
that contains
1
. Its associated bilinear form is
\beta
. It remains to show that
Q
is preserved by
G
, so that its restriction to
V
is the desired form
\kappa
.

Let
\Lambda
be the set of
\langle\det(W_{1})\rangle
’s, where
W_{1}
ranges over all
3
-dimensional subspaces of
W
. We claim that
Q
vanishes on
\Lambda
. Let
W_{1}=\langle x,y,z\rangle
be a
3
-dimensional subspace of
W
, where
x=\sum_{i=1}^{6}x_{i}e_{i}
,
y=\sum_{i=1}^{6}y_{i}e_{i}
,
z=\sum_{i=1}^{6}z_{i}e_{i}
. Set
l_{ij}:=y_{i}z_{j}+y_{j}z_{i}
for
1\leq i<j\leq 6
. We regard
Q(x\wedge y\wedge z)
as a homogenous polynomial of degree
2
in the indeterminants
x_{1},\ldots,x_{6}
. For
i<j
the coefficient of
x_{i}x_{j}
is
\sum l_{ab}l_{cd}=0
, where the summation is take over the tuples
(a,b,c,d)
such that
\{i,j,a,b,c,d\}=X
,
a<b
and
c<d
. It follows that
Q(x\wedge y\wedge z)=0
as desired, so
Q
vanishes on
\Lambda
.

Since
U
is an irreducible
G
-module, the
G
-invariant set
\Lambda
spans
U
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}
. Then
Q
is the unique quadratic form on
U
that vanishes on the
G
-invariant set
\Lambda
and polarizes to the
G
-invariant bilinear form
\beta
, so
Q
is
G
-invariant. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
\kappa
be as in Lemma
2.17
, and let
K
be the normalizer of
G
in
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
. Then
K
has at least three orbits on the singular points.

Proof.

For
q=2
we use the online Atlas data
[
13
]
to check that
K
has three orbits on the singular points of
(V,\kappa)
, so assume that
q>2
. Take
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}^{*}
such that
1+\alpha^{q+1}+\beta^{q+1}=0
; such elements exist when
q>2
. Then
x=(e_{1}+e_{2})\wedge(e_{3}+e_{4})\wedge(e_{5}+4_{6})
,
y=e_{123}+e_{456}+\eta e_{124}+\eta^{q}e_{356}
,
z=e_{123}+e_{456}+\alpha e_{124}+\alpha^{q}e_{356}+\beta e_{135}+\beta^{q}e_{246}
are singular vectors in
V
, where we use the expression of
\kappa
as in the proof of Lemma
2.17
. The dimension of
\{u\in W\mid u\wedge w=0\}
is
3,1,0
for
w=x,y,z
respectively, so
\langle x\rangle
,
\langle y\rangle
,
\langle z\rangle
are in distinct
K
-orbits. This completes the proof.
∎

2.6.
The twisted tensor modules

Suppose that
G=\textup{Sp}_{2m}(q^{2})
with
m\geq 2
. Let
\sigma
be the involutionary field automorphism of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}
. Let
W
be the natural module of
G
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}
, and write
\kappa_{1}
for the associated symplectic form. We choose a symplectic basis
e_{1},\ldots,e_{2m}
of
W
such that
\kappa_{1}(e_{i},e_{j})=1
if and only if
j=i+m
and
\kappa_{1}(e_{i},e_{j})=0
if
j-i\neq\pm m
. Let
J
be the matrix of order
2m
whose
(i,j)
-th entry is
\kappa_{1}(e_{i},e_{j})
. We identify
W
with
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}^{2m}
and regard
G
as the matrix group
\{g\in\textup{GL}_{2m}(q^{2})\mid gJg^{\top}=J\}
with respect to the chosen basis. Let
W^{\sigma}
be the
G
-module with the same ambient space as
W
and
G
-action
v.g=vg^{\sigma}
. We set
U=W\otimes W^{\sigma}
, which has a nondegenerate bilinear form
\beta^{\prime}
such that
\beta^{\prime}(u\otimes v,u^{\prime}\otimes v^{\prime})=uJu^{\prime\top}vJ^{\sigma}v^{\prime\top}
.
If
q
is odd,
\kappa^{\prime}(x):=\frac{1}{2}\beta^{\prime}(x,x)
is a quadratic form on
U
. If
q
is even, then there is a unique quadratic form
\kappa^{\prime}
on
U
that vanishes on the
u\otimes v
’s and has
\beta^{\prime}
as its associated bilinear form, cf.
[
7
, Proposition 1.9.4]
. We define
V:=\{x\in U\mid f(x)=x\}
, where

f(\lambda u\otimes v)=\lambda^{q}v^{\sigma}\otimes u^{\sigma}
is a semilinear mapping that commutes with the action of
G
. Then
V\otimes{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}=U
, and
V
is an absolutely irreducible
G
-module. The restriction of
\kappa^{\prime}
to
V
, denoted by
\kappa
, is nondegenerate. This gives an embedding of
\textup{PSp}_{2m}(q^{2})
in
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
, and we write
K
for its normalizer in the latter group. Please refer to
[
59
,
14
,
42
]
for more details.

Proposition 2.19
.

If
G=\textup{Sp}_{4}(q^{2})
, then
K
has at least three orbits on the singular points of
(V,\kappa)
.

Proof.

We write
M=M_{4}({\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}})
with
G
-action
X.g=g^{\top}Xg^{\sigma}
. There is a
G
-module isomorphism
\psi:\,U\rightarrow M
such that
u\otimes v\mapsto u^{\top}v
by the universality of tensors, cf.
[
52
]
. We write
f^{\prime}
,
V^{\prime}
for the respective counterparts of
f
,
V
for
M
. Then
f^{\prime}(X)=X^{\sigma\top}
for
X\in M
, and
V^{\prime}
is the set of Hermitian matrices in
M
. The vectors
e_{1}\otimes e_{1}
,
e_{1}\otimes e_{2}+e_{2}\otimes e_{1}
,
e_{1}\otimes e_{2}+e_{2}\otimes e_{1}+e_{3}\otimes e_{3}
in
V
are singular, and their images under
\psi
have different ranks. Therefore, they are in distinct
K
-orbits. This completes the proof.
∎

2.7.
The spin modules

Let
(V,Q)
be a quadratic space over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, where
\dim(V)\geq 5
and
q=p^{f}
with
p
prime. Here, we allow
q
to be even when
\dim(V)
is odd. We define
B(x,y)=Q(x+y)-Q(x)-Q(y)
. For a nonzero vector
v
, we define

v^{\perp,B}:=\{x\in V\mid B(v,x)=0\}
. We write
C(V,Q)
for the Clifford algebra associated with the quadratic space
(V,Q)
and let
\bar{\cdot}
be the anti-isomorphism of
C(V)
that extends the identity mapping of
V
, cf.
[
29
]
. If clear from the context, we will write
C(V)
instead of
C(V,Q)
for brevity. The Clifford group is the normalizer of
V
in the group of invertible elements in
C(V)
. The even Clifford group consists of elements of the form
u_{1}\cdots u_{2s}
, where
u_{i}
’s are nonsingular vectors of
V
. For such an element
g
, define its spin norm as
N(g)=g\bar{g}
. The spin group
\textup{Spin}(V)
is the kernel of
N
. For a nonsingular vector
v
, we define the reflection
r_{v}
of
V
such that
xr_{v}=x-\frac{B(v,x)}{Q(v)}v
for
x\in V
. In
C(V)
, we have
xr_{v}=-vxv^{-1}
for
x\in V
.

We suppose that
\dim(V)=2m\geq 8
and
Q
has plus sign for the rest of this subsection. We specify a basis
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{m},f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\}
of
V
such that
U=\langle f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\rangle
and
W=\langle e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}\rangle
are totally singular and
B(e_{i},f_{j})=[\![i=j]\!]
. We set
X:=\{1,\ldots,m\}
. For subsets
I,J
of
X
, we write
e_{I}=\prod_{i\in I}e_{i}
,
f_{J}:=\prod_{i\in J}f_{j}
, where the subscripts are arranged in the increasing order. In particular,
e_{\emptyset}=f_{\emptyset}=1
. Let
C_{+}(V)
be the subspaces spanned by the
e_{I}f_{J}
’s with
|I|+|J|
even. Set
S_{U}:=C(V)/C(V)\cdot U
, where
C(V)\cdot U
is the left ideal generated by all the elements of
U
. We write
e_{I}
’s for their corresponding images in
S_{U}
by abuse of notation. Let
S_{U}^{+}
be the quotient image of
C_{+}(V)
in
S_{U}
, which has dimension
2^{m-1}
and has the
e_{I}
’s with
I
ranging over even subsets of
X
as a basis. The even Clifford group acts on
S_{U}^{+}
via left multiplication. In particular,
S_{U}^{+}
is the half spin module of
\textup{Spin}(V)
.

For a maximal totally singular subspace
U^{\prime}
of
V
, the subspace
\{x\in S_{U}\mid ux=0\textup{ for all }u\in U^{\prime}\}
has dimension
1
which we denote by
\langle p_{U^{\prime}}\rangle
. We call any nonzero vector in it a
pure spinor
associated with
U^{\prime}
. For instance, we have
\langle p_{U}\rangle=\langle 1\rangle
. There are two equivalence classes of maximal totally singular subspaces of
V
by
[
11
, III.1.10]
, and
\Omega(V,Q)
is transitive on each equivalence class.

Lemma 2.20
.

Suppose that the maximal totally singular subspace
U^{\prime}
is the image of
U
under
g=r_{v_{s}}\cdots r_{v_{1}}
, where each
v_{i}
is nonsingular. Then
\langle p_{U^{\prime}}\rangle=\langle v_{1}\cdots v_{s}\rangle
.

Proof.

For an element
x\in U
, we have
xg=(-1)^{s}v_{1}\cdots v_{s}xv_{s}^{-1}\cdots v_{1}^{-1}
in
C(V)
. It follows that
(xg)v_{1}\cdots v_{s}=(-1)^{s}v_{1}\cdots v_{s}x=0
in
S_{U}^{+}
for each
x\in U
. The claim then follows from the fact
Ug=U^{\prime}
.
∎

First suppose that
m=4
. By
[
11
, p. 147]
, there is a hyperbolic quadratic form
\kappa
on
S_{U}^{+}
such that
\kappa(zx)=N(z)\kappa(x)
for
z
in the even Clifford group and
x\in S_{U}^{+}
. The nonzero singular vectors of
(S_{U}^{+},\kappa)
are exactly the pure spinors by
[
11
, IV.1.1]
. Set
w=e_{4}-f_{4}
,
w^{\prime}=e_{4}+f_{4}
and
V^{\prime}=w^{\perp,B}
. We have natural embeddings of
C(V^{\prime})
,
\textup{Spin}(V^{\prime})
in
C(V)
and
\textup{Spin}(V)
respectively. The group
\textup{Spin}(V^{\prime})
is
\textup{Spin}_{7}(q)
or
\textup{Sp}_{6}(q)
according as
q
is odd or even, and we call
S_{U}^{+}
its spin module.

Proposition 2.21
.

The group
\textup{Spin}(V^{\prime})
is transitive on both the set of singular points and each isometry class of nonsingular points of
S_{U}^{+}
. For odd
q
, a subgroup of the even Clifford group of
V^{\prime}
that contains
\textup{Spin}(V^{\prime})
is transitive on the set of nonsingular points if and only if it contains an element whose spin norm is nonsquare.

Proof.

The first claim follows from
[
28
, Theorem 8.4]
. The second claim follows from the fact that
\kappa(zx)=N(z)\kappa(x)
for
z
in the even Clifford group and
x\in S_{U}^{+}
.
∎

We further assume that
q=q_{0}^{2}
, and take an element
\delta\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\setminus{\mathbb{F}}_{q_{0}}
. We define

(2.5)

V_{0}:=\langle e_{1},f_{1},e_{2},f_{2},e_{3},f_{3},e_{4}+f_{4},\delta e_{4}+\delta^{q}f_{4}\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q_{0}}},

and let
Q_{0}:=Q|_{V_{0}}
be the restriction of
Q
to
V_{0}
. Then
(V_{0},Q_{0})
is an elliptic quadratic space, and
V=V_{0}\otimes{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. The Clifford algebra
C(V_{0})
embeds in
C(V)
naturally, and we have
\textup{Spin}(V_{0})=\textup{Spin}(V)\cap C(V_{0})
. The group
\textup{Spin}(V_{0})
is a covering group of
\Omega_{8}^{-}(q_{0})
, and we call
S_{U}^{+}
its spin module.

Theorem 2.22
.

Suppose that
m=4
and
q=q_{0}^{2}
, and let
V_{0}
be defined as in (
2.5
). Then
\textup{Spin}(V_{0})
has exactly two orbits on the singular points of
S_{U}^{+}
which are
(q_{0}^{3}+1)
- and
(q^{3}-q_{0}^{3})
-tight sets of
Q^{+}(7,q)
respectively.

Proof.

Let
X
be the set of maximal totally singular subspace of
(V,Q)
in the same equivalence class as
U
that intersects
V_{0}
in a
3
-dimensional
{\mathbb{F}}_{q_{0}}
-subspace, and set
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}:=\{\langle p_{U^{\prime}}\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}\mid U^{\prime}\in X\}
. The quadratic space
(V_{0},Q_{0})
has
(q_{0}^{3}+1)\frac{q^{4}-1}{q-1}
totaly singular
3
-subspaces, and each is contained in exactly one element of
X
. It follows that
|{\mathcal{N}}_{1}|=(q_{0}^{3}+1)\frac{q^{4}-1}{q-1}
. Since
\Omega_{8}^{-}(q_{0})
acts transitively on the totaly singular
3
-subspaces of
V_{0}
, we deduce that it is transitive on the set
X
. It follows that
\textup{Spin}(V_{0})
is transitive on
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
.

We claim that
\Omega_{8}^{-}(q_{0})
is transitive on the set
Y
of maximal totally singular subspaces of
V
in the same equivalence class as
U
that intersect
V_{0}
in a
1
-dimensional
{\mathbb{F}}_{q_{0}}
-subspace. We define
Y_{1}:=\{U^{\prime}\in Y\mid U^{\prime}\cap V_{0}=\langle f_{1}\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q_{0}}}\}
. Since
\Omega_{8}^{-}(q_{0})
is transitive on the singular points of
V_{0}
, it suffices to show that
\Omega_{8}^{-}(q_{0})_{\langle f_{1}\rangle}
is transitive on
Y_{1}
. Set
V_{1}^{\prime}=f_{1}^{\perp,B}/\langle f_{1}\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}
, and let
V_{0}^{\prime}
be the quotient image of
V_{0}
in
V_{1}^{\prime}
. Let
\kappa_{1}^{\prime}
be the induced quadratic form on
V_{1}^{\prime}
, and identify the quadric defined by
(V_{1}^{\prime},\kappa_{1}^{\prime})
as the Klein quadric
Q^{+}(5,q)
. Then
\textup{PG}(V_{0}^{\prime})
is a Baer subgeometry that meets the quadric in a
Q^{-}(5,q_{0})
. Since the elements in
X_{1}
intersect
V_{0}
in
\langle f_{1}\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q_{0}}}
, their quotients in
V_{1}^{\prime}
are totally singular
3
-subspaces that intersects
V_{0}^{\prime}
trivially. There is an Hermitian surface
H(3,q)
in
\textup{PG}(3,q)
such that the Klein correspondence maps totally singular lines of
H(3,q)
to the singular points of
Q^{-}(5,q_{0})
, cf.
[
26
, Section 15.4]
. Under such a correspondence the
q_{0}^{3}(q_{0}-1)(q+1)
nonsingular points of
\textup{PG}(3,q)
are mapped to the totally singular
3
-subspaces of one equivalence class in
V_{1}^{\prime}
that intersect
V_{0}^{\prime}
trivially, and the same is true for the
q_{0}^{3}(q_{0}-1)(q+1)
non-tangent planes of
\textup{PG}(3,q)
. By considering the full preimages of those totally singular
3
-subspaces in
V
, we deduce that
|Y_{1}|=q_{0}^{3}(q_{0}-1)(q+1)
. Since
\textup{SU}_{4}(q_{0})
is transitive on both the set of nonsingular points and the set of non-tangent planes of
H(3,q)
, we deduce that
\Omega_{6}^{-}(q_{0})\cong\textup{SU}_{4}(q_{0})
is transitive on
Y_{1}
. Since
\Omega_{6}^{-}(q_{0})\leq\Omega_{8}^{-}(q_{0})_{\langle f_{1}\rangle}
, this establishes the claim.

We have
|Y|=|Q^{-}(7,q_{0})|\cdot|Y_{1}|=(q^{3}-q_{0}^{3})\frac{q^{4}-1}{q-1}
by the arguments in the last paragraph. Since
\Omega_{8}^{-}(q_{0})
is transitive on
Y
, we deduce that
\textup{Spin}(V_{0})
is transitive on
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}:=\{\langle p_{U^{\prime}}\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}\mid U^{\prime}\in Y\}
. Since
|{\mathcal{N}}_{1}|+|{\mathcal{N}}_{2}|=|Q^{+}(7,q)|
, it follows that they are the only two
\textup{Spin}(V_{0})
-orbits on the singular points of
S_{U}^{+}
. By Section
2.1
,
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
are both intriguing sets. Since their sizes are not divisible by the ovoid number
q^{3}+1
, they are tight sets rather than
m
-ovoids. This completes the proof.
∎

Suppose that
m=5
in the sequel, and define

(2.6)

V_{1}:=z^{\perp,B},\quad\textup{where }z:=e_{5}-f_{5}.

The restriction of
Q
to
V_{1}
is parabolic, and
\textup{Spin}(V_{1})
embeds in
\textup{Spin}(V)
as
\textup{Spin}(V_{1})=\{x\in\textup{Spin}(V)\mid xzx^{-1}=z\}
. The group
\textup{Spin}(V_{1})
is absolutely irreducible on
S_{U}^{+}
, and we call
S_{U}^{+}
its spin module. The group
\Omega(V_{1})
is transitive on each equivalence class of maximal totally singular subspaces of
V
by
[
28
, Theorem 8.4]
, so
\textup{Spin}(V_{1})
is transitive on the set
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
of projective points defined by pure spinors in
S_{U}^{+}
. We have
|{\mathcal{N}}_{1}|=(q^{3}+1)\frac{q^{8}-1}{q-1}
, which is the number of maximal totally singular subspaces of
V
in one equivalence class. We define

(2.7)

\beta^{\prime}:\,S_{U}^{+}\times S_{U}^{+}\rightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q},\;(x,y)\mapsto f_{1}\cdots f_{5}\bar{x}zy.

Here,
\bar{x}
is the image of any preimage of
x
in
C_{+}(V)
under the anti-isomorphism
\bar{\cdot}
of
C(V)
. It is routine to check that
\beta^{\prime}
is well-defined, indeed takes value in
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
and is
\textup{Spin}(V_{1})
-invariant. Moreover, for even subsets
I,J
of
\{1,2,\ldots,5\}
we have
\beta^{\prime}(e_{I},e_{J})\neq 0
if and only if their symmetric difference is
\{1,\ldots,4\}
. It follows that
\beta^{\prime}
is a nondegenerate bilinear form on
S_{U}^{+}
.

Lemma 2.23
.

Suppose that
m=5
, and let
V_{1}
be as in (
2.6
). There is a nondegenerate
\textup{Spin}(V_{1})
-invariant quadratic form
\kappa^{\prime}
of plus sign on
S_{U}^{+}
that vanishes on the pure spinors in
S_{U}^{+}
and has
\beta^{\prime}
in (
2.7
) as its associated bilinear form. Moreover, the even Clifford group of
V_{1}
is in its similarity group.

Proof.

By
[
43
, Proposition 5.4.9]
,
S_{U}^{+}
has a
\textup{Spin}(V_{1})
-invariant quadratic form
\kappa^{\prime}
of plus sign.
Since
\textup{Spin}(V_{1})
is absolutely irreducible on
S_{U}^{+}
and
\beta^{\prime}
is a nondegenerate
\textup{Spin}(V_{1})
-invariant bilinear form, we deduce that
\beta^{\prime}
is the associated bilinear form of
\kappa^{\prime}
after properly rescaling
\kappa^{\prime}
by
[
7
, Lemma 1.8.8]
. We set
V_{2}=\langle e_{1},f_{1},\ldots,e_{4},f_{4}\rangle
and
U_{2}:=U\cap V_{2}
. There is a natural embedding of the half spin module
S_{U_{2}}^{+}
of
\textup{Spin}(V_{2})
in
S_{U}^{+}
, and
S_{U_{2}}^{+}
has a basis consisting of the
e_{I}
’s with
I
ranging over even subsets of
\{1,\ldots,4\}
. The restriction of
\beta^{\prime}
to
S_{U_{2}}^{+}
is nondegenerate upon direct check. Since
S_{U_{2}}^{+}
is an absolutely irreducible
\textup{Spin}(V_{2})
-module, we deduce that the restriction of
\kappa^{\prime}
is the unique
\textup{Spin}(V_{2})
-invariant form on
S_{U_{2}}^{+}
up to a scalar. It follows that
\kappa^{\prime}
vanishes on the pure spinor
1
in
S_{U_{2}}^{+}
by
[
11
, IV.1.1]
. Since
\textup{Spin}(V_{1})
is transitive on the set
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
of projective points defined by pure spinors in
S_{U}^{+}
,
\kappa^{\prime}
vanishes on all pure spinors in
S_{U}^{+}
. This establishes the first claim.

By
[
43
, p. 30]
the product of even number of reflections preserves each equivalence class of maximal totally singular subspaces of
V
, so we deduce that the even Clifford group of
V
preserves the set
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
. Take an element
g
in the even Clifford group of
V_{1}
. Then
g=v_{1}\cdots v_{2s}
for some nonsingular vector
v_{i}
’s of
V_{1}
. We have
\beta^{\prime}(xg,yg)=\lambda\beta^{\prime}(x,y)
, where
\lambda=\prod_{i=1}^{2s}Q(v_{i})=N(g)
. Since
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
consists of singular points and is
g
-invariant, we deduce that
g
is a similarity of
\kappa^{\prime}
as desired. This completes the proof of the second claim.
∎

Theorem 2.24
.

Suppose that
m=5
, let
V_{1}
be as in (
2.6
) and
\kappa^{\prime}
be as in Lemma
2.23
, and let
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(15,q)
be the associated polar space of
(S_{U}^{+},\kappa^{\prime})
. Let
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
be the set of projective points defined by pure spinors in
S_{U}^{+}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
be its complement in
{\mathcal{P}}
. Then
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
are the only
\textup{Spin}(V_{1})
-orbits on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
, and they are
(q^{3}+1)
- and
(q^{7}-q^{3})
-tight sets of
{\mathcal{P}}
respectively.

The remaining part of this subsection is devoted to the proof of Theorem
2.24
. Take the same notation as in Lemma
2.23
. We have
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(15,q)
, and the set
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
consists of singular points of
\kappa^{\prime}
by Lemma
2.23
. For each nonzero singular vector
v\in V
, we define the subspace
T_{\langle v\rangle}:=\{x\in S_{U}^{+}\mid vx=0\}
.

Lemma 2.25
.

Let
Y
be the set of singular points of
Q
in
V_{1}
, and suppose that
q>2
. Then
\textup{Spin}(V_{1})
is transitive on the set
\mathcal{T}:=\{T_{\langle v\rangle}\mid\langle v\rangle\in Y\}
, and the union of elements in
\mathcal{T}
is
Q^{+}(15,q)
.

Proof.

The subspace
T_{\langle f_{1}\rangle}
has dimension
8
, and it has a basis consisting of the
e_{I}
’s with
I
ranging over the even subsets of
\{2,\ldots,5\}
upon direct check. It is totally isotropic with respect to the form
\beta^{\prime}
by the paragraph following (
2.7
). For any nonzero elements
a_{2},\ldots,a_{4}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
,
e_{2}\cdots e_{5}=(e_{2}+a_{2}f_{2})\cdots(e_{4}+a_{4}f_{4})(e_{5}+f_{5})
is a pure spinor of
S_{U}^{+}
that lies in
T_{\langle f_{1}\rangle}
. Similarly, we deduce that the specified basis of
T_{\langle f_{1}\rangle}
are all pure spinors. Therefore,
T_{\langle f_{1}\rangle}
is a maximal totally singular subspace of
S_{U}^{+}
by Lemma
2.23
.

For nonsingular vectors
w_{1},w_{2}
of
V
, we have
T_{\langle vr_{w_{2}}r_{w_{1}}\rangle}=w_{1}w_{2}\cdot T_{\langle v\rangle}
. Since
\Omega(V_{1})
is transitive on
Y
, we deduce that
\textup{Spin}(V_{1})
is transitive on the set
\mathcal{T}
. The subspace
T_{\langle f_{1}\rangle}
is totally singular, so is each subspace in
\mathcal{T}
. Fix a nonzero singular vector
x
of
S_{U}^{+}
. It remains to show that there is a nonzero singular vector
v
in
V_{1}
such that
x\in T_{\langle v\rangle}
, so that the last claim in the lemma follows.

We claim that
S_{x}:=\{z^{\prime}ux\mid u\in V_{1}\}
is a totally singular subspace of
S_{U}^{+}
, where
z^{\prime}:=e_{5}+f_{5}
. Take a vector
u
in
V_{1}
. If
u
is nonsingular, then
z^{\prime}u
is in the even Clifford group of
V_{1}
. By Lemma
2.23
,
z^{\prime}ux
is also singular. If
u
is singular, then take a nonsingular vector
v
in
\langle z,u\rangle^{\perp,B}
. The vector
v+\lambda u
is nonsingular, so that
z^{\prime}(v+\lambda u)x
is singular for any
\lambda\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. Since
q>2
, we expand
\kappa^{\prime}(z^{\prime}(v+\lambda u)x)=0
and compare the coefficients of
1,\lambda,\lambda^{2}
to deduce that
z^{\prime}ux
is singular. This establishes the claim.

Since
S_{x}
is totally singular, it has dimension at most
8
. Since
V_{1}
has dimension
9
, there is a nonzero vector
v\in V_{1}
such that
z^{\prime}vx=0
by the definition of
S_{x}
. Since
z^{\prime}
is invertible, we have
vx=0
, i.e.,
x\in T_{\langle v\rangle}
. It follows that
Q(v)x=vvx=0
, so
Q(v)=0
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.24
.

We verify the claim for
q=2
by using the Atlas data
[
13
]
, so assume that
q>2
in the sequel. We set
V_{3}=\langle e_{1},f_{1},z\rangle^{\perp,B}
. Then
V_{3}
is spanned by
\{e_{2},f_{2},\ldots,e_{4},f_{4},z^{\prime}\}
, where
z^{\prime}=e_{5}+f_{5}
. Let
Y
be the set of singular points of
Q
in
V_{1}
, and set
\mathcal{T}:=\{T_{\langle v\rangle}\mid\langle v\rangle\in Y\}
which consists of maximal totally singular subspaces of
S_{U}^{+}
. By Lemma
2.25
,
\textup{Spin}(V_{1})
is transitive on
\mathcal{T}
and the union of subspaces in
\mathcal{T}
is
Q^{+}(15,q)
. There is a natural embedding of the half spin module
S_{U_{3}}^{+}
of
\textup{Spin}(V_{3})
inside
S_{U}^{+}
, which identifies
S_{U_{3}}^{+}
with
T_{\langle f_{1}\rangle}
. The even Clifford group of
V_{3}
has two orbits on the points of
T_{\langle f_{1}\rangle}
by Proposition
2.21
. For nonsingular vectors
v_{1},\ldots,v_{2s}
in
V_{3}
, set
\lambda=\prod_{i}Q(v_{i})^{-1}
and
g=v_{1}\cdots v_{2s}
. The spin norm of
g^{\prime}=g(e_{1}+f_{1})(e_{1}+\lambda f_{1})
is
1
and it commutes with
z
, so
g^{\prime}
is in
\textup{Spin}(V_{1})
. For
x\in T_{\langle f_{1}\rangle}
, we have
gx=g^{\prime}x
. In other words, there is a subset of
\textup{Spin}(V_{1})
that induces the same action as the even Clifford group of
V_{3}
on
T_{\langle f_{1}\rangle}
. We thus conclude that there are at most two
\textup{Spin}(V_{1})
-orbits on the singular points of
S_{U}^{+}
. Since
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
is a
\textup{Spin}(V_{1})
-orbit, it follows that its complement
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
is the other
\textup{Spin}(V_{1})
-orbit. By Section
2.1
they are both intriguing sets, and they are tight sets since their sizes are not divisible by the ovoid number
q^{7}+1
. This completes the proof.∎

3.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

In this section we present the proof of Theorem
1.1
. Let
V
be a vector space over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
equipped with a nondegenerate quadratic, unitary or symplectic form
\kappa
, and write
d=\dim(V)
. Let
{\mathcal{P}}
be the associated polar space, and assume that it has rank
r\geq 2
and is not
Q^{+}(3,q)
. Let
\theta_{r}
be its ovoid number, cf. Table
2.1
. Suppose that
H
is a reducible subgroup of
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
that has two orbits
O_{1}
,
O_{2}
on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
. Let
H_{0}
be the quotient group of
H
in
\textup{P}\Gamma(V,\kappa)
, and write
Z
for the center of
\textup{GL}(V)
. By Section
2.1
the two
H
-orbits are either
i_{1}
- and
i_{2}
-tight sets of
{\mathcal{P}}
or
m_{1}
- and
m_{2}
-ovoids of
{\mathcal{P}}
respectively, where
i_{1}+i_{2}=\theta_{r}
and
m_{1}+m_{2}=\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
.

Let
W
be an
H
-submodule of
V
of the lowest dimension. Let
N_{\Gamma}(W)
be the stabilizer of
W
in
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
, so that
H\leq N_{\Gamma}(W)
. Let
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
be the set of singular points in
W^{\perp}
, and let
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
be its complement in
{\mathcal{P}}
. Then
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
are both
N_{\Gamma}(W)
-invariant, and they are the two
H
-orbits when they are both nonempty. Let
H|_{W^{\perp}}
be the restriction of
H
to
W^{\perp}
, and let
\kappa|_{W}
be the restriction of
\kappa
to
W
. Since the subspace
W\cap W^{\perp}
is also
H
-invariant, we deduce that either
W\cap W^{\perp}=0
, or
W\cap W^{\perp}=W
, i.e.,
W\leq W^{\perp}
. If
W\cap W^{\perp}=0
, then
W
is nondegenerate and
V=W\oplus W^{\perp}
. Suppose that
W\leq W^{\perp}
and
W
is not totally singular or isotropic. This occurs only if
\kappa
is orthogonal and
q
is even. Since
\kappa
is nondegenerate,
d
is also even. The mapping
\kappa|_{W}:\,W\rightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
is
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-semilinear, i.e.,
\kappa(\lambda x+y)=\lambda^{2}\kappa(x)+\kappa(y)
for
x,y\in W
and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. Since
W
is not totally singular,
\kappa|_{W}
is surjective. We deduce that
\ker(\kappa|_{W})
is an
H
-invariant
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-subspace of codimension
1
in
W
, and it is trivial by the choice of
W
. It follows that
\dim(W)=1
. To summarize, we have one of the following cases:

(1A)

W
is nondegenerate, and
V=W\oplus W^{\perp}
,

(1B)

W
is totally singular or isotropic,

(1C)

\kappa
is orthogonal with
q
,
d
even, and
W=\langle v\rangle
for some nonsingular vector
v
.

Proposition 3.1
.

If either
\kappa
is symplectic or we are in case (1B), then
W
is a maximal totally singular or isotropic subspace of
V
.

Proof.

We only gives details for the case where
\kappa
is symplectic, since the other case is similar. Suppose that
\kappa
is symplectic, so that each nonzero vector is isotropic. We can not be in case (1A), since otherwise
N_{\Gamma}(W)
stabilizes both
W
and
W^{\perp}
and it has at least three orbits on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
. Suppose that we are in case (1B), i.e.,
W
is totally isotropic. Let
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
be the projective points of
\textup{PG}(W)
, which is nonempty and
N_{\Gamma}(W)
-invariant. Then
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
is one of the two
H
-orbits, and so it is either a tight set or
m
-ovoid. Let
U
be a maximal totally isotropic subspace that contains
W
, so that the points of
\textup{PG}(U)
form a
1
-tight set. Since an
m
-ovoid and a
1
-tight set intersects in
m
points by
[
3
, Theorem 4]
, we deduce that
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
is a
1
-tight set, i.e.,
W=U
. This completes the proof.
∎

We assume that
\kappa
is orthogonal or unitary in the sequel in view of Proposition
3.1
. The case (1B) has also been covered in the same proposition, and it remains to consider (1A), (1C).

Proposition 3.2
.

Suppose that we are in case (1A) with
\kappa
orthogonal or unitary. Then
W
contains no nonzero singular vectors, and
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
are the two
H
-orbits. Moreover,

(1)

If
W=\langle u\rangle
, then
H|_{W^{\perp}}
is transitive on both
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
and the set
\mathcal{T}_{2}
of nonsingular points
\langle v\rangle
in
W^{\perp}
such that
\kappa(v,v)=-\kappa(u,u)
or
\kappa(v)=-\kappa(u)
. If
q
is odd and
\kappa
is orthogonal, then
|\mathcal{T}_{2}|=\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{N}}_{2}|
.

(2)

If
\dim(W)=2
, then
d
is even,
\kappa
is orthogonal, and
H|_{W^{\perp}}
is transitive on both
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
and the set of all nonsingular points in
W^{\perp}
.

Proof.

If
W
has a nonzero singular vector
u_{1}
, then
W^{\perp}
also has a nonzero singular vector
u_{2}
since
\dim(W^{\perp})\geq\dim(W)
. The singular points
\langle u_{1}\rangle
,
\langle u_{2}\rangle
and
\langle u_{1}+u_{2}\rangle
are in distinct
N_{\Gamma}(W)
-orbits: a contradiction. Therefore,
W
has no singular points. It follows that
\dim(W)\leq 1
or
2
according as
\kappa
is unitary or orthogonal, and
\dim(W)=2
only if
W
is elliptic. Since
{\mathcal{P}}
has rank
r\geq 2
and is not
Q^{+}(3,q)
, we deduce that
W^{\perp}
has a nonzero singular points by considering dimensions, i.e.,
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
is nonempty. The two sets
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
are
N_{\Gamma}(W)
-invariant and so are the two
H
-orbits as desired.

We first suppose that
\dim(W)=1
and establish (1). Suppose that
W=\langle u\rangle
. We only give details for the case
\kappa
is orthogonal, since the unitary case is similar. We have
\kappa(u)\neq 0
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}=\{\langle v+u\rangle\mid v\in W^{\perp},\,\kappa(v)=-\kappa(u)\}
. Take
\langle v_{1}\rangle
,
\langle v_{2}\rangle
from the set
\mathcal{T}_{2}
. For
i=1,2
, there is
\lambda_{i}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
such that
\kappa(\lambda_{i}v_{i})=-\kappa(u)
. Then
\langle\lambda_{i}v_{i}+u\rangle
,
i=1,2
, are singular points in
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
. Since
H
is transitive on
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
, there is
h\in H
and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
such that
\lambda_{1}v_{1}h+uh=\lambda(\lambda_{2}v_{2}+u)
. It follows that
uh=\lambda u
and
v_{1}h=\lambda\lambda_{1}^{-1}\lambda_{2}v_{2}
. This shows the transitivity of
H|_{W^{\perp}}
on
\mathcal{T}_{2}
as desired. Further suppose that
q
is odd. We define
S_{1}:=\{v\in W^{\perp}\mid\kappa(v)=-\kappa(u)\}
. Then
|{\mathcal{N}}_{2}|=|S_{1}|=2\cdot|\mathcal{T}_{2}|
, which gives the second claim. This concludes the proof of (1).

We next suppose that
\dim(W)=2
and establish (2). Since
W
has no singular points, we see that
\kappa
is orthogonal and its restriction to
W
is elliptic. We have
\{\kappa(v)\mid v\in W\setminus\{0\}\}={\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
. For each nonsingular vector
v
in
W^{\perp}
, there is an element
u\in W
such that
\kappa(u)=-\kappa(v)
and so
u+v
is a singular vector. We have
|(v+u)^{\perp}\cap{\mathcal{N}}_{1}|=|u^{\perp}\cap{\mathcal{N}}_{1}|
. By the same arguments as in the
\dim(W)=1
case, we deduce that
H|_{W^{\perp}}
is transitive on the set of nonsingular points in
\textup{PG}(W^{\perp})
. If
d=2r+1
is odd and
q
is odd, then
|P^{\perp}\cap{\mathcal{N}}_{1}|
takes the two values
|Q^{+}(2r-3,q)|
,
|Q^{-}(2r-3,q)|
as
P
varies in
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
. It follows that
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
is not an intriguing set for
d=2r+1
. We conclude that
d
is even. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 3.3
.

Suppose that we are in case (1C) and
d=2m+2\geq 6
. Then either
\textup{Sp}_{2a}(q^{b})^{\prime}\unlhd H
with
m=ab
, or
G_{2}(q^{b})^{\prime}\unlhd H
with
m=3b
.

The remaining part of this subsection is devoted to the proof of Proposition
3.3
. We first introduce some notation. We assume without loss of generality that
W=\langle v\rangle
with
\kappa(v)=1
. Take
\delta\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
such that
\textup{Tr}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}/{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}(\delta)=1
. Let
\langle u,v\rangle
be an elliptic
2
-subspace such that
\kappa(x_{1}u+x_{2}v)=\delta x_{1}^{2}+x_{1}x_{2}+x_{2}^{2}
, and set
U:=\langle u,v\rangle^{\perp}
. Let
f
be the associated bilinear form of
(V,\kappa)
, and let
r_{v}
be the reflection such that
xr_{v}=x+\frac{f(x,v)}{\kappa(v)}v
for
x\in V
.
Let
f_{1}
be the induced symplectic form on
W^{\perp}/W
.

By considering the induced action on the symplectic space
(W^{\perp}/W,f_{1})
, we obtain a natural group homomorphism
\psi:\,N_{\Gamma}(W)\rightarrow\Gamma(W^{\perp}/W,f_{1})
. It is routine to show that
\ker(\psi)=\langle r_{v}\rangle
and
\psi
is surjective by using
[
29
, Theorem 14.2]
and Witt’s lemma. Since
V=U\perp\langle u,v\rangle
, we have a symplectic space isomorphism between
(U,f|_{U})
and
(W^{\perp}/W,f_{1})
via
x\mapsto\bar{x}
for
x\in U
. By composition we obtain a group homomorphism
\psi:\,N_{\Gamma}(W)\rightarrow\Gamma(U,f|_{U})
, which we still denote by
\psi
by abuse of notation.

Lemma 3.4
.

Take notation as above, and suppose that
g\in N_{I(V)}(W)
. Then there is an isometry
h
of the symplectic space
(U,f|_{U})
, an element
w\in U
and an element
\eta\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
such that

(3.1)

\eta+\eta^{2}=\kappa(w)
, and
f(w,zh)^{2}=\kappa(z)+\kappa(zh)
for
z\in U

and

(3.2)

g:\,(u,v,z)\mapsto(u+\eta v+w,v,zh+f(w,zh)v),\quad\textup{ for }z\in U.

Conversely, each triple
(h,w,\eta)
that satisfies (
3.1
) defines an isometry
g\in N_{I(V)}(W)
as in (
3.2
).

Proof.

The homomorphism
\psi
maps
N_{I(V)}(W)
to
I(U,f|_{U})
surjectively with kernel
\langle r_{v}\rangle
, so
h:=\psi(g)
is a isometry of
(U,f|_{U})
. Since
g
is an isometry and stabilizes
W
, we have
vg=v
. For
z\in U
, we have
zg-zh=\beta(z)v
for some
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear function
\beta:\,U\rightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. Since
f|_{U}
is nondegenerate, there is a unique element
w\in U
such that
\beta(z)=f(w,zh)
for
z\in U
. We have
f(zg,zh)=f(zh+\beta(z)v,zh)=0
and so

\displaystyle\beta(z)^{2}=\kappa(zg-zh)=\kappa(zg)+\kappa(zh)+f(zg,zh)=\kappa(z)+\kappa(zh).

We deduce that
w\in U
is uniquely determined by the property
f(w,zh)^{2}=\kappa(z)+\kappa(zh)
for
z\in U
. Write
ug=au+\eta v+w^{\prime}
, where
a,\eta\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
and
w^{\prime}\in U
. We deduce from
f(ug,vg)=1
that
a=1
. For
z\in U
, we deduce from
f(ug,zg)=0
that
0=f(u+bv+w,zh+\beta(z)v)=f(w+w^{\prime},zh)
, so
w^{\prime}=w
. From
\kappa(ug)=\delta
we deduce that
\kappa(w)=\eta+\eta^{2}
. This establishes (
3.1
) and (
3.2
).

Conversely, if a triple
(h,w,\eta)
satisfies (
3.1
), then we defines a linear transformation
g
as in (
3.2
). It is clear that
g
stabilizes
W
. The fact that
g
is an isometry follows by direct check. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

Choose
z_{0}\in U
such that
\kappa(z_{0})=\delta
. Let
\kappa_{1}
be the restriction of
\kappa
to
U
, and define
\kappa_{0}(z)=\kappa_{1}(z)+f(z_{0},z)^{2}
for
z\in U
