. Then the quadratic space
(U,\kappa_{0})
has the same sign as
(V,\kappa)
.

Proof.

Since
\langle u,v\rangle
is elliptic,
(U,\kappa_{1})
has an opposite sign to that of
(V,\kappa)
. Since
U
is nondegenerate and
\dim(U)\geq 4
, there is an elliptic line
\langle z_{0},z_{1}\rangle
in
U
such that
\kappa_{1}(x_{0}z_{0}+x_{1}z_{1})=x_{1}^{2}+x_{1}x_{0}+\delta x_{0}^{2}
. For
z=x_{0}z_{0}+x_{1}z_{1}
, we have
f(z_{0},z)=x_{1}
and
\kappa_{0}(z)=x_{1}(x_{0}+\delta x_{1})
, so
\langle z_{0},z_{1}\rangle
is a hyperbolic line for
\kappa_{0}
. Since
\kappa_{0}
and
\kappa_{1}
agrees on the orthogonal complement of
\langle z_{0},z_{1}\rangle
in
U
, we deduce that
(U,\kappa_{0})
has an opposite sign to that of
(U,\kappa_{1})
. This establishes the claim.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

Take the same notation as introduced following this proposition. Each element of
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
is of the form
\langle\kappa(z)^{q/2}v+z\rangle
with
z\in U\setminus\{0\}
, which is in bijection with the points of
\textup{PG}(U)
. Since
H
is transitive on
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
, we deduce that
\psi(H)
is transitive on the points of
\textup{PG}(U)
. By
[
28
, Theorem 5.2(a)]
we have
K_{1}\unlhd\psi(H)
(
m=ab
) or
K_{2}\unlhd\psi(H)
(
m=3b
), where
K_{1}=\textup{Sp}_{2a}(q^{b})^{\prime}
and
K_{2}=G_{2}(q^{b})^{\prime}
. Let
H_{i}
be the full preimage of
K_{i}
under
\psi
for
i=1,2
. Since
\ker(\psi)
has order
2
and
q
is even, we deduce that
H_{1}^{\infty}\cong\textup{Sp}_{2a}(q^{b})^{\prime}
(
m=ab
) and
H_{2}^{\infty}\cong G_{2}(q^{b})^{\prime}
(
m=3b
). Both
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
lie in
N_{I(V)}(W)
, and they are transitive on
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
by the bijection between
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
and points of
\textup{PG}(U)
. It remains to show that
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are both transitive on
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
.

Fix an vector
z_{0}\in U
such that
\kappa(z_{0})=\delta
, so that
\langle u+z_{0}\rangle
is a singular point in
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
. Take
g
in
N_{I(V)}(W)
that stabilizes
\langle u+z_{0}\rangle
, and let
h,w,\eta
be as in Lemma
3.4
. Since
g
is an isometry, we deduce that
u+z_{0}=ug+z_{0}g
, i.e.,
(f(w,z_{0}h)+\eta)v=z_{0}h+z_{0}+w
by (1) of Lemma
3.4
. The right hand side is in
U
while
v
is not, so
w=z_{0}h+z_{0}
and
\eta=f(w,z_{0}h)=f(z_{0},z_{0}h)
. It follows that
w
and
\eta
are uniquely determined by
h
. We plug them into the second equation in (2) of Lemma (
3.4
) to deduce that
f(z_{0},z)^{2}+\kappa(z)=f(z_{0},zh)^{2}+\kappa(zh)
for
z\in U
. By specifying
z=z_{0}
we obtain
f(z_{0},z_{0}h)^{2}+\kappa(z_{0}h)+\kappa(z_{0})=0
, i.e.,
\eta+\eta^{2}=\kappa(w)
. We conclude that
g
stabilizes
\langle u+z_{0}\rangle
if and only if
h
stabilizes the quadratic form
Q_{0}(z):=f(z_{0},z)^{2}+\kappa(z)
on
U
.

We now determine the stabilizer of
Q_{0}
in the corresponding
K_{i}
for
i=1,2
. By Lemma
3.5
,
Q_{0}
has the same sign as
(V,\kappa)
. There is an embedding of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}
in
\textup{End}(U)
such that
U
becomes a vector space
U^{\prime}
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}
equipped with a
\psi(K_{i})
-invariant symplectic form
f^{\prime}
such that
f|_{U}=\textup{Tr}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}/{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}\circ f^{\prime}
. Let
\kappa^{\prime}:\,U^{\prime}\rightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}
be any quadratic form such that
\kappa|_{U}=\textup{Tr}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}/{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}\circ\kappa^{\prime}
, and set
Q^{\prime}(z):=f^{\prime}(z_{0},z)^{2}+\kappa^{\prime}(z)
. Then
Q_{0}=\textup{Tr}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}/{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}\circ Q^{\prime}
, and
(U^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
has the same sign as
(V,\kappa)
by Table
2.2
and Lemma
3.5
. An element
h\in K_{i}
is an isometry of
(U,Q_{0})
if and only if it is an isometry of
(U^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
. The stabilizer of
(U^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
in
\textup{Sp}_{2a}(q^{b})
is
\Omega(U^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, and the stabilizer in
G_{2}(q^{b})
is
\textup{SU}_{3}(q^{b}).2
or
\textup{SL}_{3}(q^{b}).2
according as
(V,\kappa)
is elliptic or hyperbolic by
[
16
, Propositions 2.1, 6.1]
. We deduce that the orbit of
\langle u+z_{0}\rangle
under
H_{i}
has the same size as
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
for
i=1,2
. It follows that
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are both transitive on
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Let us summarize what we have done so far in this section. Let
W
be an
H
-submodule of
V
of the lowest dimension, so that we have either case (1A), (1B) or (1C). If
\kappa
is symplectic or we are in case (1B), then we have (R1) of Theorem
1.1
by Proposition
3.1
. Suppose that
\kappa
is orthogonal or unitary. For case (1A) we obtain (R2) and (R3) of Theorem
1.1
by Proposition
3.2
, and for case (1C) we obtain (R4) of Theorem
1.1
by Proposition
3.3
. This completes the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

4.
Proof of Theorem
1.3
: the non-geometric subgroups

Write
q=p^{f}
with
p
prime. Let
V
be a
d
-dimensional vector space over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
that is equipped with a nondegenerate quadratic, unitary or symplectic form
\kappa
. Suppose that the associated polar space
{\mathcal{P}}
has rank
r\geq 2
and
{\mathcal{P}}\neq Q^{+}(3,q)
. We assume that
H
is a subgroup of
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
of Aschbacher class
{\mathcal{S}}
that has two orbits
O_{1}
,
O_{2}
on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
which are
i_{1}
- and
i_{2}
-tight sets respectively. We refer the reader to
[
7
, Definition 2.1.3]
for the exact definition of the Aschbacher class
{\mathcal{S}}
. We write
H_{0}
for the quotient group of
H
in
\textup{P}\Gamma(V,\kappa)
, and write
Z
for the center of
\textup{GL}(V)
. There is a simple group
S
such that
S\leq H_{0}\leq M
, where
M
is the normalizer of
S
in
\textup{P}\Gamma(V,\kappa)
. We write
L:=H^{\infty}
and
K:=N_{\Gamma(V)}(L)
, so that
L\unlhd H\unlhd K
. If
S
is a simple group of Lie type in the characteristic
p
, then we write
\tilde{S}
for the covering group of
S
by the
p^{\prime}
-part of its Schur multiplier; otherwise, we write
\tilde{S}
for the full covering group of
S
.

We specify a basis of
V
such that
\kappa
takes the standard form as in
[
7
, Table 1.1]
. We regard elements of
V
as row vectors and identify
\Delta(V)
as a matrix group with respect to the specified basis. The
L
-module
V
is naturally a
\tilde{S}
-module, and let
\rho:\,\tilde{S}\rightarrow\textup{GL}_{d}(q)
be the corresponding representation afforded by
V
. We then have
L=\tilde{S}\rho
. Since
H
is not in the Aschbacher class
{\mathcal{C}}_{5}
,
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
is the smallest field over which
V
can be realized. By
[
18
, Theorem 74.9]
,
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
is the smallest extension field of
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
that contains the traces of
g\rho
,
g\in\tilde{S}
.

Proposition 4.1
.

If
{\mathcal{P}}=W(d-1,q)
, then we have one of the symplectic examples in Table
1.6
.

Proof.

As
H
has two orbits on the singular points, the group
HZ
has two orbits on the nonzero vectors of
V
. First consider the case where
S
is a simple group of Lie type in the defining characteristic
p
. By
[
47
, Section 2]
, we have one of the cases (A8)-(A11) in the main theorem therein. In each case, it is routine to determine the smallest field of realization of the respective module by using
[
7
, Theorem 5.1.13]
. For (A8), we have
H^{\infty}=\textup{SL}_{5}(q)
and
V=\wedge^{2}(W)
which has no form by
[
7
, Proposition 5.4.3]
, where
W
is the natural module of
\textup{SL}_{5}(q)
. For (A9),
V
is the spin module for
\tilde{S}=\textup{Spin}_{7}(q)
which has a nondegenerate quadratic form by
[
7
, Proposition 5.4.9]
. For (A10),
V
is the spin module for
\tilde{S}=\textup{Spin}^{+}_{10}(q)
which is not self-dual by
[
43
, Proposition 5.4.9]
. It follows that
V
does not have a symplectic form by
[
43
, Lemma 2.10.15]
. For (A11), we have
\tilde{S}=\textup{Sz}(q)
and one of the two orbits is an ovoid of
W(3,q)
.

Next consider the case where
S
is an alternating group, a simple group of Lie type in characteristic different from
p
or a sporadic simple group. We have one of the examples in
[
47
, Table 2]
by
[
47
, Sections 2-4]
, and we list those with a symplectic form in Table
1.6
by
[
9
, Table 11.6]
. In the row about
W(3,5)
(in Table 1.6), the normalizer
M
of
2.A_{6}
has two orbits
O_{1}
,
O_{2}
on the singular points while
2.A_{6}
has three orbits by
[
47
, Lemma 4.3]
. In all the other examples,
S
has exactly two orbits on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
. We remark that the
A_{9}
row of
[
47
, Table 2]
is not in Table
1.6
since it arises from the embedding
A_{9}<\Omega_{8}^{+}(2)
which is in Aschbacher class
{\mathcal{C}}_{8}
. This completes the proof.
∎

In view of Proposition
4.1
, we suppose that
\kappa
is orthogonal or unitary for the rest of this section. We shall make use of the numerical conditions in Lemma
2.1
.
Since
2r\leq d\leq 2r+2
, we deduce that
\lceil\frac{d}{2}\rceil-1\leq r\leq\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\rfloor
. Since
|H_{0}|
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
, we deduce that
d<2+\log_{q}(2\cdot|\textup{Aut}(S)|)
by Lemma
2.1
. Together with the bound
2r\leq d
, we deduce that
r<1+\frac{1}{2}\log_{q}(2\cdot|\textup{Aut}(S)|)
.

Remark 4.2
.

We describe a general strategy to handle a specific simple group
S
. Let
R(S)
be a lower bound on the dimensions of absolutely irreducible representations of
V
in characteristic
p
. Then
R(S)\leq d
and so
\frac{R(S)}{2}-1\leq r
. There are finitely many
(q,r)
pairs such that
\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
and

\frac{1}{2}R(S)-1\leq r<1+\frac{1}{2}\log_{q}(2\cdot|\textup{Aut}(S)|).

If
q
is not a square, then we expect
{\mathcal{P}}
to be orthogonal and so the ovoid number is
\theta_{r}=q^{d-1-r}+1
. In such a case, we check whether there is an integer
i
such that
\textup{lcm}(i,\theta_{r}-i)\cdot\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
. We then look up
[
50
]
and
[
32
]
for candidate representations with Frobenius-Schur indicators
+
or
\circ
whose dimensions are in the interval
[\max\{2r,R(S)\},2r+2]
. By Section
2.1
, it suffices to consider one representative from each weak equivalence class. Their matrix representations are usually available via the online Atlas database
[
13
]
or the Magma Command AbsolutelyIrreducibleModules, and the Magma command ClassicalForms returns the form
\kappa
. Then we can determine the number of their orbits on the singular points by computer.

4.1.
The alternating group
A_{n}
,
n\geq 5

Suppose that
S
is an alternating group
A_{n}
,
n\geq 5
. We first recall the definition of fully deleted modules for
A_{n}
. The group
A_{n}
acts on the vector space
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{n}
by permuting the coordinates. Set
U=\left\{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\mid\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=0\right\}
,
W=\{(a,\ldots,a)\mid a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\}
, and define the fully deleted module for
A_{n}
as
V_{0}:=U/(U\cap W)
. We have
\dim(V_{0})=n-1-[\![p\mid n]\!]
. For a nonzero vector
v\in U
, we write
c_{i}
for the number of occurrence of
i
among its coordinates for
i\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
. If
\{i\mid c_{i}>0\}=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}\}
, then we say that
v
is of the shape
i_{1}^{c_{i_{1}}}\cdots i_{s}^{c_{i_{s}}}
. Also, we define the Hamming weight of
v
as
\omega_{H}(v)=n-c_{0}
. If
p
is odd, then
A_{n}
preserves the quadratic form
Q(a)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{2}
on
U
. If
p=2
and
n\not\equiv 2\pmod{4}
, then
A_{n}
preserves the quadratic form
Q(a)=(-1)^{\omega_{H}(a)/2}
on
U
. In both cases
U\cap W
is the radical of
Q
, and so there is an induced nondegenerate quadratic form on
V_{0}
. In the remaining cases,
V_{0}
does not have an
A_{n}
-invariant quadratic or unitary form, cf.
[
43
, p. 187]
. This leads to the following embeddings of
A_{n}
in orthogonal groups:

(1)

If
p
is odd,
n=2m+1
and
p\nmid n
, then
A_{n}\leq\Omega_{2m}^{\epsilon}(p)
, where
\epsilon=+
if and only if
(-1)^{m}\cdot n
is a square of
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{*}
;

(2)

If
pn
is odd and
p\mid n
, then
A_{n}\leq\Omega_{n-2}(p)
;

(3)

If
p
is odd,
n=2m+2
and
p\nmid n
, then
A_{n}\leq\Omega_{2m+1}(p)
;

(4)

If
p
is odd,
n=2m+2
and
p\mid n
, then
A_{n}\leq\Omega_{2m}^{\epsilon}(p)
, where
\epsilon=-
if and only if
m
is even and
p\equiv 3\pmod{4}
;

(5)

If
p=2
and
n=2m+1
, then
A_{n}\leq\Omega_{2m}^{\epsilon}(2)
, where
\epsilon=+
if and only if
m\pmod{4}\in\{0,3\}
;

(6)

If
p=2
and
n=2m+2
(
m
odd), then
A_{n}\leq\Omega_{2m}^{\epsilon}(2)
, where
\epsilon=+
if and only if
m\equiv 3\pmod{4}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

If
p
is odd and
p\equiv 2\pmod{3}
, then there are nonzero elements of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
such that
1^{j}+a_{1}^{j}+a_{2}^{j}+a_{3}^{j}=0
for
j=1,2
. Moreover,
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\neq(1,1,1)
for each such triple.

Proof.

Consider the quadric
{\mathcal{Q}}
defined by
x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{4}^{2}=0
in
\textup{PG}(3,q)
. The vector
w=(1,1,1,1)
is not singular, so the tangent hyperplane
x_{1}+\cdots+x_{4}=0
at
\langle w\rangle
intersects
{\mathcal{Q}}
in a conic. Let
\langle(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\rangle
be a point on the conic, and assume that
a_{0}=1
without loss of generality. We claim that the
a_{i}
’s are nonzero. Suppose to the contrary that one of them, say
a_{3}
, is zero. Then
a_{2}=-1-a_{1}
, and
1+a_{1}^{2}+(-1-a_{1})^{2}=0
which simplifies to
a_{1}^{2}+a_{1}+1=0
. It follows that
a_{1}
has multiplicative order
3
, which contradicts the condition that
p\equiv 2\pmod{3}
. The triple
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
has all the desired properties in the lemma. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 4.4
.

If
V
is the fully deleted module for
A_{n}
,
n\geq 5
, then
p=2
and
n\in\{11,20\}
. The two
A_{n}
-orbits are respectively
11
,
22
-tight sets of
Q^{-}(9,2)
for
n=11
and
19
,
494
-tight sets of
Q^{-}(17,2)
for
n=20
.

Proof.

Take the same notation as in the preceding Lemma
4.3
, and suppose that
V
is the fully deleted module
V_{0}
. We have
q=p
, and the rank
r
is uniquely determined by the
(p,n)
pair in each case. If
p
divides
n
, we define
\omega_{H}(\bar{u}):=\min\{\omega_{H}(x)\mid x\in u+W\}
for
\bar{u}=u+W\in V
. It is preserved by the induced action of
S_{n}
on
V
.

We claim that the rank
r\leq 10
in each case (1)-(6). Suppose to the contrary that this is not the case. Then we have
d\geq 22
and correspondingly
n\geq 23
. If
p\equiv 0,1\pmod{3}
, then take an element
a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{*}
such that
a^{2}+a+1=0
and set
b:=-1-a
which is nonzero. If
p
is odd and
p\equiv 2\pmod{3}
, then take such a triple
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
as in Lemma
4.3
. If
p=2
, take singular vectors
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}
of the shapes
0^{n-4}1^{4}
,
0^{n-8}1^{8}
,
0^{n-12}1^{12}
respectively. If
p
is odd and
p\equiv 2\pmod{3}
, then take singular vectors
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}
of the shapes
0^{n-4}1a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}
,
0^{n-8}1^{2}a_{1}^{2}a_{2}^{2}a_{3}^{2}
,
0^{n-12}1^{3}a_{1}^{3}a_{2}^{3}a_{3}^{3}
respectively; if
p\equiv 0,1\pmod{3}
, take singular vectors
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}
of the shapes
0^{n-3}1^{1}a^{1}b^{1}
,
0^{n-6}1^{2}a^{2}b^{2}
,
0^{n-9}1^{3}a^{3}b^{3}
respectively. Here, we slightly abuse the notation since the
a_{i}
’s as well as
1,a,b
may not be distinct. If
p\nmid n
, then the
u_{i}
’s are in distinct
S_{n}
-orbits. If
p\mid n
, then it is routine to check that
\omega_{H}(\overline{u_{i}})=\omega_{H}(u_{i})
for
i=1,2,3
and the
\overline{u_{i}}
’s are in distinct
S_{n}
-orbits. This establishes the claim.

We first consider the case
p=2
. Suppose that we are in case (5). If
n\geq 13
, there are singular vectors of the shapes
0^{n-4}1^{4}
,
0^{n-4}1^{8}
,
0^{n-4}1^{12}
which are in distinct
S_{n}
-orbits. If
n\in\{9,11\}
, the nonzero singular vectors all have Hamming weights
4
or
8
, and
A_{n}
acts transitively on those of each weight. For
n=9
, the two orbits are respectively
1
- and
14
-ovoids of
Q^{+}(7,2)
, and for
n=11
the two orbits are respectively
11
- and
22
-tight sets of
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(9,2)
. If
n=7
, then all the nonzero singular vectors have weights
4
and
A_{7}
is transitive on them. Suppose that we are in case (6), and write
n=2m+2
. Since
r\leq 10
, we have
n\leq 16
if
m\equiv 3\pmod{4}
and
n\leq 20
if
m\equiv 1\pmod{4}
. Therefore,
n
is one of
8
,
12
,
16
,
20
. If
n\in\{8,12\}
, then all the nonzero singular vectors have Hamming weights
4
and
A_{12}
is transitive on them. If
n\in\{16,20\}
, then the nonzero singular vectors have weights
4
or
8
and corresponding
A_{20}
has two orbits. For
n=16
,
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(13,2)
and the orbit lengths are not divisible by
2^{7}-1
, so they are not tight sets. For
n=20
,
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(17,2)
and the orbit lengths are
\binom{20}{4}=4845
and
\binom{20}{8}=125970
which are not divisible by the ovoid number
2^{9}+1
. It follows that the two orbits are both tight sets.

We next consider the case where
p
is odd and
n\geq 8
. Since
r\leq 10
, we deduce that
d\leq 22
and
n\leq d+2\leq 24
. If
8\leq n\leq 24
, then the
(p,n)
pairs such that
\frac{p^{r}-1}{p-1}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
are
(3,10)
,
(3,13)
,
(3,14)
,
(3,15)
,
(7,10)
,
(7,11)
. For the pairs with
p=3
and
p\nmid n
, the singular vectors of the shapes
0^{n-3}1^{3}
,
0^{n-6}1^{6}
,
0^{n-6}1^{3}2^{3}
are not in the same
S_{n}
-orbits. For
(p,n)=(3,15)
, the images of the singular vectors
0^{12}1^{3}
,
0^{9}1^{6}
,
0^{9}1^{3}2^{3}
of
U
in
V
are in distinct
S_{15}
-orbits. For the pairs with
p=7
and
p\nmid n
, the singular vectors
0^{n-3}1^{1}2^{1}4^{1}
,
0^{n-6}1^{2}2^{2}4^{2}
,
0^{n-9}1^{3}2^{3}4^{3}
are singular vectors in distinct
S_{n}
-orbits.

It remains to consider the case where
p
is odd and
n\leq 7
. Since the rank
r\geq 2
and we exclude
Q^{+}(3,q)
from consideration, we deduce that
n
is at least
7
,
7
,
6
,
8
,
7
,
8
in the cases (1)-(6). If
n=6
, then we are in case (3),
p\neq 3
and
{\mathcal{P}}=Q(4,p)
. The ovoid number is
\theta_{2}=p^{2}+1
and the rank
r=2
. For the primes
p
such that
p+1
divides
|\textup{Aut}(A_{6})|
, there is an integer
i
such that
\textup{lcm}(i,p^{2}+1-i)\cdot(p+1)
divides
|\textup{Aut}(A_{6})|
only if
p\in\{5,7,11\}
. For those three
p
’s, there are nonzero singular vectors of three distinct weights by computer check, so
N_{\textup{PGO}(5,p)}(A_{6})=S_{6}
has more than two orbits on the singular points. If
n=7
, then we are in case (1) or (2), and
d\leq n-1=6
,
r\leq 3
. There is no prime
p
such that
\frac{p^{3}-1}{p-1}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(A_{7})|
, so we have
r=2
. It follows that the sign is
-
and so
(-1)^{3}\cdot 7\pmod{p}
is a nonsquare of
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{*}
for
p\neq 7
. If
p=7
, then the images of the singular vectors of the shapes
0^{4}1^{1}2^{1}3^{1}
,
0^{3}1^{2}4^{1}6^{1}
in
V
are in distinct
S_{7}
-orbits. If
-7
is nonsquare in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{*}
, i.e.,
p\mod{7}\in\{3,5,6\}
, then
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(5,p)
and the ovoid number is
p^{3}+1
. For such primes that
p+1
divides
|\textup{Aut}(A_{7})|
, we have
|\textup{Aut}(A_{7})|\geq\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{P}}|
only if
p\in\{3,5\}
. There are respectively three and four
S_{7}
-orbits for
p=3,5
by a computer check. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

If
V
is not the fully deleted module of
A_{n}
(
n\geq 5
) up to weak equivalence, then we have
n\leq 14
.

Proof.

Suppose to the contrary that
n\geq 15
. Let
\tilde{A}_{n}
be the full covering group of
A_{n}
whose center has order
2
, and recall that
k
is the algebraic closure of
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
. Since
V
is not the fully deleted module, we have
d\geq n+1
by
[
63
,
64
]
. It follows that
d\geq 16
and so
r\geq 7
. Let
p^{\prime}
be a primitive prime divisor of
p^{fr}-1
. We have
\textup{ord}_{p^{\prime}}(p)=fr
, so
p^{\prime}\geq 1+fr
. Since
\frac{p^{fr}-1}{p-1}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(A_{n})|=n!
, we deduce that
p^{\prime}\leq n
. It follows that
d\leq 2(r+1)\leq 2p^{\prime}\leq 2n
. If
V
is not a faithful
\tilde{A}_{n}
-module, i.e., the center of
\tilde{A}_{n}
acts trivially on
V
, then we have
d\geq\frac{1}{4}n(n-5)
by
[
37
, Theorems 6, 7]
which contradicts
d\leq 2n
for
n\geq 15
. If
V
is a faithful
\tilde{A}_{n}
-module, then
p\neq 2
and
V\otimes k
is a faithful irreducible representation of
\tilde{A}_{n}
. By
[
66
,
36
]
,
d
is divisible by
2^{\lfloor(n-s-1)/2\rfloor}
and is at least
2^{\lfloor(n-2-[\![p|n]\!])/2\rfloor}
. Here,
s
is the
2
-adic weight of
n=2^{i_{1}}+\cdots+2^{i_{s}}
with
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{s}
. This is incompatible with the bound
d\leq 2n
for
n\geq 15
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 4.6
.

If
V
is not the fully deleted module of
A_{n}
(
n\geq 5
) up to weak equivalence, then
L=2.A_{7}
,
{\mathcal{P}}=H(3,3^{2})
and the two
A_{7}
-orbits on the singular points are
7
- and
21
-tight sets respectively.

Proof.

Suppose that
V
is not the fully deleted module of
A_{n}
up to weak equivalence. We have
n\leq 14
by Lemma
4.5
. There is a lower bound
R(A_{n})
on
d
by
[
43
, Proposition 5.3.7]
, so that we have
r\geq\lceil\frac{R(A_{n})}{2}\rceil-1
. For instance, we have
R(A_{n})=n-2
for
n\geq 9
. We follow the approach described in Remark
4.2
. We call a pair
(q,r)
feasible if
r\geq\lceil\frac{R(A_{n})}{2}\rceil-1
and
\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(A_{n})|
. For
n=10
, the feasible
(q,r)
pairs are
(2,3)
,
(4,3)
,
(2,4)
,
(3,4)
,
(7,4)
and
(2,6)
. For
n=11,12
, the feasible
(q,r)
pairs are
(2,4)
,
(3,4)
,
(7,4)
and
(2,6)
. For
n=13,14
, the feasible
(q,r)
pairs are
(2,6)
,
(3,6)
,
(4,6)
and
(2,12)
. There is no candidate representation with Frobenius-Schur indicator
+
or
\circ
whose dimension is in the interval
[2r,2r+2]
in each case by
[
32
]
, so we assume that
n\leq 9
in the sequel.

For
n=9
, the feasible
(q,r)
pairs are
(2,3)
,
(4,3)
,
(2,4)
,
(3,4)
and
(2,6)
. For the three absolutely irreducible representations with
(q,d)=(2,8)
in the online Atlas
[
13
]
, we all have
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(7,2)
. Both (8b) and (8c) have a transitive
A_{9}
-action on the singular points, and (8a) corresponds to the fully deleted module. For the absolutely irreducible representation of
2.A_{9}
with
(q,d)=(3,8)
, we have
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(7,3)
and
2.A_{9}
is transitive on the singular points. There is no further candidate representations.

For
n=8
, we have
A_{8}\cong\textup{PSL}_{4}(2)
. We have
R(A_{8})=4
, but the representation that attains this bound has no form. Since
V
is not the fully deleted module, we have
d\geq 14
if
p=2
and
d\geq 8
if
p\neq 2
. It follows that
r\geq 3
. A feasible
(q,r)
pair is one of
(3,4)
and
(2,6)
. For
(q,r)=(3,4)
,
V
is a faithful representation of
2.A_{8}
of dimension
8
. We have
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(7,3)
with a transitive
A_{8}
-action on the singular points. For
(q,r)=(2,6)
,
V
is a
14
-dimensional representation of
\textup{SL}_{4}(2)
such that
V\otimes k
has highest weight
\omega_{1}+\omega_{3}
up to weak equivalence. We have
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(13,2)
and
S_{8}=N_{\textup{PGO}^{-}_{14}(2)}(A_{8})
has six orbits on the singular points.

For
n=7
, a feasible
(q,r)
pair either has
r=2
or is one of
(2,3)
,
(4,3)
,
(2,4)
,
(3,4)
and
(2,6)
. There is no candidate representation for
(q,r)\in\{(2,4),(3,4)\}
. For
(q,r)=(2,6)
, there is exactly one
14
-dimensional candidate representation in characteristic
2
that can be realized over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
. We have
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(13,2)
and
S_{7}=N_{\textup{PGO}^{-}_{14}(2)}
has
12
orbits on the singular points. For
(q,r)=(4,3)
, there is a candidate representation of dimension
6
with Frobenius-Schur indicator
\circ
which is a faithful representation of
3.A_{7}
. It has a unitary form and the normalizer of
3.A_{7}
has more than two orbits on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
. For
(q,r)=(2,3)
, there is exactly one candidate representation of dimension
6
with Frobenius-Schur indicator
+
. It turns out that
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(5,2)
and
A_{7}
is transitive on it. We next consider the case where
r=2
. There is no candidate representation with dimension in the interval
[4,6]
with a Frobenius-Schur indicator
+
, so we expect
{\mathcal{P}}=H(3,q)
with
q
a square. If
p=2
, the
4
-dimensional candidate representations with Frobenius-Schur indicators
+
can all be realized over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
and so can not be unitary. The only odd prime power
q
such that
q
is a square and
q+1
divides
|\textup{Aut}(A_{7})|
is
q=3^{2}
. We use the online data in
[
13
]
to check that there are two
A_{7}
-orbits of sizes
70
,
210
respectively for both representations (8a) and (8b). The two orbits are
7
- and
21
-tight sets of
H(3,9)
respectively, and we have
L=2.A_{7}
in this case.

For
n=6
, we have
A_{6}=\textup{PSp}_{4}(2)^{\prime}=\textup{PSL}_{2}(9)
and
|\textup{Aut}(A_{6})|=2\cdot 6!
. Its Schur multiplier is cyclic of order
6
, so its projective representation in characteristic
3
is also a representation of
\textup{SL}_{2}(9)
. A feasible
(q,r)
pair either has
r=2
or is one of
(2,4)
and
(3,4)
. For
(q,r)=(3,4)
, we deduce that
d
is a square by
[
7
, Corollary 5.3.3]
. It follows that
d=9
. It turns out that
{\mathcal{P}}=Q(8,2)
and the normalizer of
\textup{SL}_{2}(9)
has more than two orbits. For
(q,r)=(2,4)
, there is no candidate representations with Frobenius-Schur indicator
+
that can be realized over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
. We then consider the case where
r=2
. We have either
{\mathcal{P}}=Q(4,q)
,
Q^{-}(5,q)
or
H(3,q)
. If
q
is a square and
q+1
divides
|\textup{Aut}(A_{6})|
, then
q=4
or
9
. The
4
-dimensional candidate representations in characteristic
p\in\{2,3\}
can all be realized over
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
, so
{\mathcal{P}}
is not unitary. For
Q(4,q)
, there is an integer
i
such that
\textup{lcm}(q^{2}+1-i,i)\cdot(q+1)
divides
|\textup{Aut}(A_{6})|
only when
q\in\{3,5,7,11\}
; for
Q^{-}(5,q)
, we deduce that
q\in\{2,3,5\}
similarly. There are candidate representations only for
Q(4,q)
with
q\in\{5,7,11\}
, which are
p
-modular reductions of two irreducible representations over the complex numbers. We check with computer that the normalizer of
A_{6}
has more than two orbits on the singular points in each case.

For
n=5
, we have
A_{5}=\textup{PSL}_{2}(2^{2})=\textup{PSL}_{2}(5)
. Similar to the
A_{6}
case, its representations in characteristic
2
,
5
are representations over
\textup{SL}_{2}(4)
,
\textup{SL}_{2}(5)
respectively. A feasible
(q,r)
pair either has
r=2
or is one of
(2,4)
and
(3,4)
. For
(q,r)=(3,4)
, there is no candidate representation. For
(q,r)=(2,4)
, we deduce that
d
is a power of
2
as well as a square by
[
7
, Section 5.3]
. There is no such integer
d
in the interval
[2r,2r+2]=[8,10]
. We then consider the case where
r=2
. We proceed as in the case of
A_{6}
to show that
{\mathcal{P}}
is not unitary and it can only be
Q(4,3)
or
Q^{-}(5,2)
. There is no such representation for
2.A_{5}
in either case by
[
32
]
. This completes the proof.
∎

To summarize, we have handled the fully deleted modules in Proposition
4.4
and the remaining modules in Proposition
4.6
in this subsection. We obtain three rows in Table
1.6
that correspond to
H(3,9)
,
Q^{-}(9,2)
and
Q^{-}(17,2)
respectively. This completes the analysis of the alternating groups.

4.2.
The groups of Lie type in defining characteristic

Suppose that
S
is a simple group of Lie type
{}^{t}X_{\ell}(p^{e})
defined over
{\mathbb{F}}_{p^{e}}
. Let
\tilde{S}={}^{t}\hat{X}_{\ell}(p^{e})
be the covering group of
S
by the
p^{\prime}
-part of its Schur multiplier. Write
G={}^{t}X_{\ell}(k)
, where
k
is the algebraic closure of
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
. There is a certain endomorphism
F
of the algebraic group
G
such that
\tilde{S}
consists of elements of
G
fixed by
F
. We regard
G
as a matrix group, and let
\phi
be the field automorphism
x\mapsto x^{p}
applied to the entries of
G
. If
t=1
, then
F=\phi^{e}
. If
t\geq 2
, then
F^{t}
is a power of
\phi^{e}
. In particular, if
X_{\ell}
is one of
A_{\ell}
,
D_{\ell}
,
E_{6}
and
D_{4}
, the group
G
has a graph automorphism
\tau_{o}
of order
2
,
2
,
2
and
3
respectively such that
F=\tau_{o}^{-1}\phi^{e}
. In those cases, we also write
\tau_{o}
for its restriction to
\tilde{S}
which agrees with
\phi^{e}
. The finite dimensional irreducible
k\tilde{S}
-modules are restrictions of irreducible rational
kG
-modules to the action of
k\tilde{S}
, and the irreducible rational
kG
-modules are characterized by their highest weights, cf.
[
57
]
. We take the same notation as in
[
50
]
, which will be our main reference in this subsection. When
X_{\ell}
is one of
A_{\ell}
,
D_{\ell}
and
E_{6}
, we write
\tau
for the symmetry induced on the Dynkin diagram by the graph automorphism
\tau_{o}
. For an irreducible
kG
-module
M
, we write
M^{(i)}
for
{}^{\phi^{i}}M
for brevity. If
M=L(\omega)
is an irreducible module with highest weight
\omega
, then
M^{(i)}=L(p^{i}\omega)
. If we write
M^{*}
for its dual module, then by
[
35
, 3.1.6]
we have

(4.1)

L(\omega)^{*}\cong\begin{cases}L(\tau(\omega)),\quad&\textup{for types $A_{\ell}$, $D_{\ell}$ ($\ell$ odd), $E_{6}$,}\\
L(\omega),\quad&\textup{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Suppose that
V\otimes k
is the restriction of the irreducible
kG
-module
L(\omega)
. If either
t=1
or
{}^{t}X_{\ell}
is one of
{}^{2}A_{\ell}
,
{}^{2}D_{\ell}
or
{}^{2}E_{6}
, then we can apply
[
7
, Theorem 5.1.13]
to determine
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. We shall make frequent use of the following result which combines
[
43
, Proposition 5.4.6]
and the remark following it.

Theorem 4.7
.

Take notation as above, and write
q=p^{f}
with
p
prime. Let
k
be the algebraic closure of
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
, and let
\theta
be the field automorphism
x\mapsto x^{p}
of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
.

(i)

If
t=1
, then
f\mid e
and there is an irreducible
k\tilde{S}
-module
M
such that

(4.2)

V\otimes k\cong M\otimes M^{(f)}\otimes\cdots\otimes M^{(e-f)}.

In particular,
\dim(V)=(\dim_{k}M)^{e/f}
.

(ii)

If
\tilde{S}
is one of
{}^{2}A_{\ell}(p^{e})
,
{}^{2}D_{\ell}(p^{e})
,
{}^{2}E_{6}(p^{e})
,
{}^{3}D_{4}(p^{e})
, then one of the following occurs: (a)
f\mid e
,
V\cong{}^{\tau_{o}}V
, there is an irreducible
k\tilde{S}
-module
M
such that
M\cong{}^{\tau_{o}}M
and
\dim(V)=(\dim_{k}M)^{e/f}
; (b)
f\mid te
but
f
does not divide
e
, and there is irreducible
k\tilde{S}
-module
M
such that
M\not\cong{}^{\tau_{o}}M
and
\dim(V)=\dim(M)^{te/f}
.

(iii)

If
\tilde{S}
is one of
{}^{2}B_{2}(p^{e})
,
{}^{2}G_{2}(p^{e})
and
{}^{2}F_{4}
, where
e
is odd and
p=2,3,2
respectively, then
f\mid e
and there is irreducible
k\tilde{S}
-module
M
such that
\dim(V)=(\dim_{k}M)^{e/f}
. Moreover, we have
\dim_{k}M\geq R_{p}(L)
with
R_{p}(L)=4,7,26
respectively.

Remark 4.8
.

We now describe how to determine whether
V
has a nondegenerate form. For some specific modules of dimensions up to
17
, we refer the reader to
[
7
]
,
[
56
]
and
[
54
]
for this information. Let
V^{\ast}
be the dual module of
V
, and let
\theta
be the field automorphism
x\mapsto x^{p}
of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. By
[
43
, Lemma 2.10.15]
,
V
has a nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form only if
V^{*}\cong V
, and
V
has a nondegenerate unitary form if and only if
f
is even and
V^{*}\cong V^{\theta^{f/2}}
. Suppose that
V\otimes k
is the restriction of the irreducible
kG
-module
L(\omega)
. If
X_{\ell}
is one of
B_{\ell}
,
C_{\ell}
,
D_{\ell}
(
\ell
even),
G_{2}
,
F_{4}
,
E_{7}
and
E_{8}
, then
V
has a nondegenerate symmetric or alternating bilinear form by (
4.1
). Suppose that
X_{\ell}
is one of
A_{\ell}
,
D_{\ell}
(
\ell
odd) and
E_{6}
. Then
V^{*}\otimes k
is the restriction of
L(\tau(\omega))
and
V^{\theta^{f/2}}\otimes k
is the restriction of
L(\omega)^{\theta^{f/2}}=L(p^{f/2}\omega)
. Therefore, to decide whether
V
has a nondegenerate bilinear or unitary form it suffices to examine the restrictions of the
kG
-modules
L(\omega)
,
L(\tau(\omega))
and
L(p^{f/2}\omega)
to
\tilde{S}
. By
[
58
, § 13]
, the
p^{e}
-restricted
kG
-modules remain irreducible and inequivalent upon restriction to
\tilde{S}
for those three types.

We assume that
\kappa
is orthogonal or unitary in view of Proposition
4.1
. Recall that
q=p^{f}
with
p
prime,
d=\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(V)
,
{\mathcal{P}}
is the associated polar space,
L=H^{\infty}
and
K
is its normalizer in
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
. We have
d\geq 4
by the hypothesis
r\geq 2
. We suppose that
S
is not one of
\textup{PSL}_{2}(4)
,
\textup{PSL}_{2}(5)
,
\textup{PSL}_{2}(9)
,
\textup{PSL}_{4}(2)
,
\textup{PSp}_{4}(2)^{\prime}
which are isomorphic to alternating groups. We suppose that
S
is not one of
\textup{PSU}_{2}(p^{e})
,
\textup{PSp}_{2}(p^{e})
,
\textup{P}\Omega_{3}(p^{e})
(
p
odd),
\textup{P}\Omega_{4}^{-}(p^{e})
,
\textup{P}\Omega_{5}(p^{e})
(
p
odd),
\textup{P}\Omega_{6}^{\pm}(p^{e})
,
{}^{2}G_{2}(3)^{\prime}
which are isomorphic to classical groups of types
A_{\ell}
or
{}^{2}A_{\ell}
.

Proposition 4.9
.

If
S=\textup{PSL}_{d^{\prime}}(p^{e})
with
(d^{\prime},p^{e})\not\in\{(2,4),(2,5),(2,9),(4,2)\}
, then
(d^{\prime},q)=(3,p^{e})
,
V
is the adjoint module of
\textup{SL}_{3}(q)
, and the two
S
-orbits are
(q+1)
- and
(q^{3}-q)
-tight sets of
{\mathcal{P}}=Q(6,q)
respectively.

Proof.

We have
\tilde{S}=\textup{SL}_{d^{\prime}}(p^{e})
and
G=\textup{SL}_{d^{\prime}}(k)
in this case, and we write
W
for the natural
\tilde{S}
-module over
{\mathbb{F}}_{p^{e}}
. We first show that
V
is not weakly equivalent to
W
,
S^{2}(W)
or
\wedge^{2}(W)
if
q=p^{e}
. Suppose that
q=p^{e}
. Since
W
has no form,
V
is not weakly equivalent to it. We argue as in Remark
4.8
to deduce that
S^{2}(W)
(
p
odd) has a nondegenerate form only if
d^{\prime}=2
in which case it has dimension
3
: a contradiction to
d\geq 4
. Similarly,
\wedge^{2}(W)
has a nondegenerate form only if
d^{\prime}=4
in which case it has dimension
6
and is the natural module of
\Omega_{6}^{+}(q)\cong\textup{SL}_{4}(q)
. The group
\Omega_{6}^{+}(q)
is transitive on the points of
Q^{+}(5,q)
. This establishes the claim.

By Theorem
4.7
there is an irreducible
k\tilde{S}
-module
M
such that (
4.2
) holds and
d=(\dim_{k}M)^{s}
, where
s:=e/f
is an integer. We have
\dim_{k}M\geq d^{\prime}
by
[
7
, Theorem 1.11.5]
. Also, we have
|\textup{Aut}(S)|=\epsilon sfq^{sd^{\prime}(d^{\prime}-1)/2}\prod_{i=2}^{d^{\prime}}(q^{si}-1)
, where
\epsilon=1
or
2
according as
d^{\prime}=2
or not. It follows that
|\textup{Aut}(S)|<\epsilon sfq^{s(d^{\prime 2}-1)}
. By Lemma
2.1
we deduce that

(4.3)

d^{\prime s}\leq d<2+(d^{\prime 2}-1)s+\log_{q}(2\epsilon sf).

We first suppose that
d^{\prime}=2
. All the absolutely irreducible
\textup{SL}_{2}(q)
-modules are self-dual, so we expect
{\mathcal{P}}
to be orthogonal. Since we do not consider
Q^{+}(3,q)
, we have
d\geq 5
. It follows from (
4.3
) that
s\leq 3
or
(p,f,s)=(2,1,4)
. We divide into four subcases according to the value of
s
. (i) If
s=1
, then we have
d\leq 6
by (
4.3
). It follows that
{\mathcal{P}}
is one of
Q(4,q)
(
q
odd) and
Q^{\pm}(5,q)
. If
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{\pm}(5,q)
, then the condition
|\textup{Aut}(S)|\geq\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{P}}|
is violated upon routine check. In the case
{\mathcal{P}}=Q(4,q)
, we have
d=5
. By
[
7
, Section 5.3]
, we have
p\geq 5
and
V=S^{4}(W)
up to weak equivalence. We take the model of
S^{4}(W)
as in
[
7
, p. 281]
and use Lemma 5.3.4 therein to deduce that the
\textup{SL}_{2}(q)
-orbit that contains
\langle e_{0}\rangle
has length
q+1
and is
\Gamma\textup{L}_{2}(q)
-invariant. However,
\textup{lcm}(1,q^{2}+1-1)\cdot(q+1)=q^{2}(q+1)
does not divide
|\textup{Aut}(S)|=fq(q^{2}-1)
, so there are more than two
\Gamma\textup{L}_{2}(q)
-orbits on the singular points. (ii) If
s=2
, then we have
d=(\dim_{k}M)^{2}\leq 10
by (
4.3
). Since
d\geq 5
, it follows that
\dim_{k}M=3
and
d=9
. Therefore,
q
is odd and we expect
{\mathcal{P}}=Q(8,q)
whose rank is
r=4
. We deduce from the second inequality in (
4.3
) that
p^{f}\leq 4f
. It holds only for
(p,f)=(3,1)
, but then we have
|\textup{Aut}(S)|<\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{P}}|
by direct check: a contradiction. (iii) If
s=3
, then we have
d\leq 13
by (
4.3
). It follows that
\dim_{k}M=2
and
d=8
. We have
{\mathcal{P}}=W(7,q)
or
Q^{+}(7,q)
according as
q
is odd or even by
[
59
, Proposition 2.6]
, so
r=4
and
q
is even. The condition that
\frac{q^{4}-1}{q-1}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
reduces to
q^{2}+1\mid 3f(q-1)
which does not hold: a contradiction. (iv) If
(p,f,s)=(2,1,4)
, then we have
16\leq d\leq 17
by (
4.3
). It follows that
\dim_{k}M=2
and
d=16
, and so we expect
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{\pm}(15,2)
. If
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(15,2)
, then
|\textup{Aut}(S)|=16320<\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{P}}|=16447.5
: a contradiction. If
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(15,2)
, then
r=7
and we derive the contradiction that
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
is not divisible by
2^{7}-1
. To summarize, we obtain no examples for
d^{\prime}=2
.

We then suppose that
d^{\prime}=3
. It follows from (
4.3
) that
s\leq 3
. We also split into three cases according to the value of
s
. (i) If
s=1
, then
d\leq 12
by (
4.3
). We deduce from
[
50
, A. 6]
that either
V
is the adjoint module up to weak equivalence or
\dim(V)=10
. The former case has been handled in Section
2.3
. There are more than two
K
-orbits on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
for
p\neq 3
, and there are two
S
-orbits that are
(q+1)
- and
(q^{3}-q)
-tight sets of
{\mathcal{P}}=Q(6,q)
respectively for
p=3
. In the case
\dim(V)=10
, we have
r=4
or
5
. We derive the contradiction that
\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
does not divide
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
in either case upon direct check. (ii) If
s=2
, then
9\leq d\leq 21
by (
4.3
). It follows that
\dim_{k}M=3
or
4
. By
[
50
, A. 6]

M
is the restriction of
L(\omega_{1})
or
L(\omega_{2})
up to weak equivalence, and correspondingly
V\otimes k
is the restriction of
L((1+q)\omega_{1})
or
L((1+q)\omega_{2})
by (
4.2
). We deduce that
V
does not have a nondegenerate form by Remark
4.8
. (iii) If
s=3
, then
3^{3}\leq d\leq 29
by (
4.3
). It follows that
\dim_{k}M=3
, and we deduce that
V
has no form as in the case
s=2
. To summarize, we obtain the example in the statement of this proposition for
d^{\prime}=3
.

Finally, we suppose that
d^{\prime}\geq 4
. We first show that
p^{fr}-1
has a primitive prime divisor. If
s\geq 2
then
d\geq 16
and so the rank
r\geq 7
. If
s=1
, then
V
is not
W
,
S^{2}(W)
or
\wedge^{2}(W)
by the first paragraph of the proof. It follows from
[
47
, Theorem 2.2]
that
d\geq 15
unless
(d^{\prime},p)=(4,2)
and
V
is the adjoint module of dimension
14
. In the latter case,
V
has a quadratic form whose type is plus or minus according as
f
is even or odd by
[
56
, Corollary 8.4.6]
. The normalizer of
\textup{PSL}_{4}(2)
in
\textup{GL}_{14}(2)
has
6
orbits on
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(13,2)
, so we have
q=2^{f}>2
. We conclude that
fr\geq 7
in all cases, and so
p^{fr}-1
has a primitive prime divisor
p^{\prime}
by
[
68
]
. Since
\textup{ord}_{p^{\prime}}(p)=fr
, we have
\gcd(p^{\prime},fr)=1
. Also,
p^{\prime}\geq 1+fr\geq\frac{1}{2}d\geq\frac{1}{2}d^{\prime s}>s
, so
\gcd(p^{\prime},s)=1
. Since
p^{\prime}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
, we deduce that
fr
divides one of
\{2sf,\ldots,d^{\prime}sf\}
. In particular,
r\leq d^{\prime}s
. It follows that
d^{\prime s}\leq d\leq 2d^{\prime}s+2
. For
s\geq 2
, it holds only if
(d^{\prime},s)=(4,2)
in which case
16\leq d\leq 18
. It follows that
\dim_{k}M=4
and
d=16
. Then
M
is the restriction of
L(p^{i}\omega_{1})
or
L(p^{i}\omega_{3})
for some
i
and correspondingly
V\otimes k
is the restriction of
L(p^{i}(1+q)\omega_{1})
or
L(p^{i}(1+q)\omega_{3})
by (
4.2
). We deduce that
V
has no form by Remark
4.8
. For
s=1
, we have
d^{\prime}\leq d\leq 2d^{\prime}+2\leq\binom{d^{\prime}+1}{2}
. By
[
43
, Theorem 5.11.5]
, we have excluded the candidate representations in the beginning of the proof. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 4.10
.

If
S=\textup{PSp}_{d^{\prime}}(p^{e})
with
d^{\prime}\geq 4
and
(d,q)\neq(4,2)
, then
p^{e}=q
and we have one of the following:

(1)

(d^{\prime},p)=(6,3)
,
V
is a section of
\wedge^{2}(W)
and the two
S
-orbits are respectively
(q^{2}+1)
- and
(q^{6}-q^{2})
-tight sets of
{\mathcal{P}}=Q(12,q)
, where
W
is the natural
\textup{Sp}_{6}(q)
-module,

(2)

(d^{\prime},p)=(8,2)
,
V
is the spin module and the two
S
-orbits are respectively
(q^{3}+1)
- and
(q^{7}-q^{3})
-tight sets of
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(15,q)
.

Proof.

We have
\tilde{S}=\textup{Sp}_{d^{\prime}}(p^{e})
and
G=\textup{Sp}_{d^{\prime}}(k)
in this case, and we write
W
for the natural
\tilde{S}
-module over
{\mathbb{F}}_{p^{e}}
. The absolutely irreducible module
V
is self-dual by (
4.1
), so we expect
{\mathcal{P}}
to be orthogonal. It follows that
V
is not weakly equivalent to
W
when
q=p^{e}
. By Theorem
4.7
there is an irreducible
k\tilde{S}
-module
M
such that (
4.2
) holds and
d=(\dim_{k}M)^{s}
, where
s:=e/f
is an integer. We have
\dim_{k}M\geq d^{\prime}
by
[
7
, Theorem 1.11.5]
. Also, we have
|\textup{Aut}(S)|=\epsilon sfq^{sd^{\prime 2}/4}\prod_{i=1}^{d^{\prime}/2}(q^{2si}-1)
, where
\epsilon=\frac{2}{\gcd(2,q^{s}-1)}
or
1
according as
d^{\prime}=4
or not. It follows that
|\textup{Aut}(S)|<\epsilon sfq^{d^{\prime}(d^{\prime}+1)s/2}
. By Lemma
2.1
we deduce that

(4.4)

d^{\prime s}\leq d<2+\frac{1}{2}d^{\prime}(d^{\prime}+1)s+\log_{q}(4sf).

We first consider the case
d^{\prime}=4
. We deduce from (
4.4
) that
s\leq 2
and we have
16\leq d=(\dim_{k}M)^{2}<25
for
s=2
. If
s=2
, then we deduce that
(\dim_{k}M,d)=(4,16)
which has been excluded in Section
2.6
. If
s=1
, then
d<14
if
p\in\{2,3\}
and
d\leq 12
otherwise by (
4.4
). If
d=4
or
5
, then
V
is either the natural module of
\textup{Sp}_{4}(q)
or
\Omega_{5}(q)
(
p
odd). In both cases
S
is transitive on the singular points, so we have
d\geq 6
. By
[
50
, A. 22]
, we deduce that either
V=S^{2}(W)
(
p
odd) up to weak equivalence, or
(p,d)=(5,12)
and
V\otimes k
is the restriction of
L(5^{i}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}))
for some
i
. The former case has been excluded in Section
2.4
, and the latter case is symplectic by
[
7
, Table 5.6]
. To summarize, there is no example for
d^{\prime}=4
.

We suppose that
d^{\prime}\geq 6
in the sequel. We claim that
p^{fr}-1
has a primitive prime divisor. By
[
68
]
the claim is true provided one of the following holds: (1)
d\geq 15
, so that
r\geq 7
, (2)
p
is odd and
d\geq 7
, so that
r\geq 3
, (3)
V
is the spin module, so that
r=2^{d^{\prime}/2-1}\geq 4
and is a power of
2
. By
[
43
, Proposition 5.4.11]
, it remains to consider the case where
(d^{\prime},p)=(6,2)
and
V
is a section of
\wedge^{2}(W)
of dimension
14
.
We have
r\geq 6
, and so
p^{fr}=2^{6}
if and only if
(p^{f},r)=(2,6)
. The normalizer of
\textup{Sp}_{6}(2)
in
\textup{GL}_{14}(2)
has three orbits on
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(13,2)
, so
p^{fr}\neq 2^{6}
. We thus have
fr\geq 12
and so
p^{fr}-1
has a primitive prime divisor by
[
68
]
.

Let
p^{\prime}
be a primitive prime divisor of
p^{fr}-1
. It is routine to show that
\gcd(p,2fs)=1
as in the proof of Proposition
4.9
, and we deduce from the fact
p^{\prime}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
that
r\leq d^{\prime}s
. It follows that
d^{\prime s}\leq d\leq 2r+2\leq 2d^{\prime}s+2
. It follows that
s=1
, and by
[
43
, Proposition 5.4.11]
we deduce that up to weak equivalence
V
is one of the following modules: (a) a section of
\wedge^{2}(W)
with
(d^{\prime},d)=(6,14-[\![p=3]\!])
, (b) a spin module of dimension
2^{d^{\prime}/2}
with
d^{\prime}\in\{6,8\}
and
p
odd, (c) a section of
\wedge^{3}(W)
with
(d^{\prime},d)=(6,14)
and
p=2
. The case (a) has been considered in Section
2.4
: there are two
S
-orbits which are
(q^{2}+1)
- and
(q^{6}-q^{2})
-tight sets of
{\mathcal{P}}=Q(12,q)
for
p=3
and more than three
K
-orbits for
p\neq 3
. For (b), the group
S
is transitive on
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(7,q)
by
[
28
, Theorem 8.4]
if
d^{\prime}=6
, and
S
has exactly two orbits on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
which are respectively
(q^{3}+1)
- and
(q^{7}-q^{3})
-tight sets if
d^{\prime}=8
by Section
2.7
. For (c),
{\mathcal{P}}
is symplectic by
[
56
, Proposition 9.3.5]
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 4.11
.

If
S=\textup{P}\Omega_{d^{\prime}}(p^{e})
with
pd^{\prime}
odd and
d^{\prime}\geq 7
, then
(d^{\prime},p^{e})=(9,q)
,
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(15,q)
, and the two
S
-orbits are
(q^{3}+1)
- and
(q^{7}-q^{3})
-tight sets respectively.

Proof.

Write
\ell=\frac{d^{\prime}-1}{2}
, and let
W
be the natural
\Omega_{d^{\prime}}(p^{e})
-module over
{\mathbb{F}}_{p^{e}}
. We have
\tilde{S}=\textup{Spin}_{d^{\prime}}(p^{e})
and
G=\textup{Spin}_{d^{\prime}}(k)
. If
q=p^{e}
, then
V
is not weakly equivalent to
W
since
\Omega_{d^{\prime}}(q)
is transitive on the singular points of
Q(8,q)
(
p
odd). By Theorem
4.7
there is an irreducible
k\tilde{S}
-module
M
such that (
4.2
) holds and
d=(\dim_{k}M)^{s}
, where
s:=e/f
is an integer. We have
\dim_{k}M\geq d^{\prime}
by
[
7
, Theorem 1.11.5]
and the assumption
d^{\prime}\geq 7
. Also, we have
|\textup{Aut}(S)|=sfq^{s\ell^{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}(q^{2si}-1)
. The module
V
is self dual by
4.1
, so we expect
{\mathcal{P}}
to be orthogonal. For
s\geq 2
, we have
d\geq 49
. For
s=1
, we have
d\geq 15
unless
V
is weakly equivalent to the spin module of dimension
2^{3}
with
d^{\prime}=7
by
[
43
, Proposition 5.4.11]
. In the latter case,
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(7,q)
and the rank
r=4
by
[
43
, Proposition 5.4.9]
. We deduce that
p^{fr}-1
has a primitive prime divisor
p^{\prime}
in all cases by
[
68
]
. We have
\gcd(p^{\prime},2fs)=1
as usual and deduce from
p^{\prime}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
that
r\leq(d^{\prime}-1)s
. It follows that
d^{\prime s}\leq d\leq 2r+2\leq 2(d^{\prime}-1)s+2
, which holds only if
s=1
. Then
d\leq 2d^{\prime}
, and we deduce that
V
is weakly equivalent to the spin module with
d^{\prime}\in\{7,9\}
by
[
43
, Proposition 5.4.11]
. By Section
2.7
,
\textup{P}\Omega_{7}(q)
is transitive on
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(7,q)
and
\textup{P}\Omega_{9}(q)
has two orbits on
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(15,q)
which are
(q^{3}+1)
- and
(q^{7}-q^{3})
-tight sets respectively. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 4.12
.

We can not have
S=\textup{P}\Omega^{+}_{d^{\prime}}(p^{e})
with
d^{\prime}
even and
d^{\prime}\geq 8
.

Proof.

Suppose to the contrary that
S=\textup{P}\Omega^{+}_{d^{\prime}}(p^{e})
with
d^{\prime}
even and
d^{\prime}\geq 8
. Write
\ell=\frac{1}{2}d^{\prime}
, and let
W
be the natural
\Omega^{+}_{d^{\prime}}(p^{e})
-module over
{\mathbb{F}}_{p^{e}}
. We have
\tilde{S}=\textup{Spin}_{d^{\prime}}^{+}(p^{e})
and
G=\textup{Spin}(k)
. If
q=p^{e}
then
V
is not weakly equivalent to
W
since
\Omega^{+}_{d^{\prime}}(q)
is transitive on
Q^{+}(d^{\prime}-1,q)
. By Theorem
4.7
there is an irreducible
k\tilde{S}
-module
M
such that (
4.2
) holds and
d=(\dim_{k}M)^{s}
, where
s:=e/f
is an integer. We have
\dim_{k}M\geq d^{\prime}
by
[
7
, Theorem 1.11.5]
. Also, we have
|\textup{Aut}(S)|=2f\epsilon q^{s\ell(\ell-1)}(q^{s\ell}-1)\prod_{i=1}^{\ell-1}(q^{2si}-1)
, where
\epsilon=3
or
1
according as
d^{\prime}=8
or not. For
s\geq 2
, we have
d\geq 64
. For
s=1
, by
[
43
, Proposition 5.4.11]
we have
d\geq 15
unless
\ell=4
and
V=W
up to weak equivalence which was excluded. We thus always have
r\geq 7
.
It follows that
p^{fr}-1
has a primitive prime divisor
p^{\prime}
in all cases by
[
68
]
. It is routine to show that
\gcd(p^{\prime},6fs)=1
as usual and we deduce from
p^{\prime}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
that
r\leq(d^{\prime}-2)s
. We thus have
d^{\prime s}\leq d\leq(2d^{\prime}-4)s+2
, which holds only if
s=1
. Since
d\leq 2d^{\prime}-2
, we deduce that
d^{\prime}\in\{8,10\}
and
V
is the half spin module up to weak equivalence by
[
43
, Proposition 5.4.11]
. If
d^{\prime}=10
, then
V
has no form by
[
54
, Table 4.1]
. If
d^{\prime}=8
, then
V
is weakly equivalent to
W
which was excluded. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.13
.

We can not have
S=\textup{PSU}_{d^{\prime}}(p^{e})
with
d^{\prime}\geq 3
.

Proof.

Suppose to the contrary that
S=\textup{PSU}_{d^{\prime}}(p^{e})
with
d^{\prime}\geq 3
. We have
\tilde{S}=\textup{SU}_{d^{\prime}}(p^{e})
,
G=\textup{SL}_{d^{\prime}}(k)
, and
|\textup{Aut}(S)|=2ep^{ed^{\prime}(d^{\prime}-1)}\prod_{i=2}^{d^{\prime}}(p^{ei}-(-1)^{i})
. Let
W
be the natural
\textup{SU}_{d^{\prime}}(p^{e})
-module over
{\mathbb{F}}_{p^{2e}}
, and set
W_{0}=W\otimes k
. We take an orthonormal basis
e_{1},\ldots,e_{d^{\prime}}
of
W
with respect to its unitary form. We can not have
V\otimes k=W_{0}
, since otherwise
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}={\mathbb{F}}_{p^{2e}}
,
V=W
and
S
is transitive on the singular points of
H(d-1,q)
. If
d^{\prime}=6
and
V\otimes k
is the restriction of the
kG
-module
L(p^{i}\omega_{3})
for some integer
i
, then we have
q=p^{e}
by
[
7
, Theorem 5.1.13]
and
V=\wedge^{3}(W)
up to weak equivalence. Here, the restriction of
{}^{\phi^{e}}L(p^{i}\omega_{3})
as a
k\tilde{S}
-module is the same as the restriction of
L(p^{i}\tau(\omega_{3}))=L(p^{i}\omega_{3})
. The space
V
has a symplectic form if
p
is odd by
[
50
, A. 3]
and has a quadratic form if
p=2
, and the latter has been excluded in Section
2.5
.

We claim that
V\otimes k
is not
\wedge^{2}(W_{0})
up to weak equivalence. Suppose to the contrary that
V\otimes k=\wedge^{2}(W_{0})
, so that
V\otimes k
is the restriction of the
kG
-module
L(\omega_{d^{\prime}-1})
. We have
d^{\prime}\geq 4
by the fact
d=\binom{d^{\prime}}{2}\geq 5
. We use
[
7
, Theorem 5.1.13]
to determine
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, the smallest field over which
\wedge^{2}(W_{0})
can be realized. For
d^{\prime}=4
we have
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}={\mathbb{F}}_{p^{e}}
,
V
is the natural module of
\Omega_{6}^{-}(p^{e})
and
S
is transitive on the singular points of
Q^{-}(5,q)
. For
d^{\prime}\geq 5
we have
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}={\mathbb{F}}_{p^{2e}}
and so
V=\wedge^{2}(W)
. The space
V
has a nondegenerate unitary form
\kappa
by
[
7
, Proposition 5.2.4]
. We have
d=\binom{d^{\prime}}{2}
, and
r=\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\rfloor\geq 5
. Since
f=2e
and
r\geq 5
,
p^{fr}-1
has a primitive prime divisor
p^{\prime}
. We have
p^{\prime}\geq 1+fr>2e
, so
\gcd(p^{\prime},2e)=1
. Since
p^{\prime}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
, we deduce that
fr\leq 2ed^{\prime}
, i.e.,
r\leq d^{\prime}
. It follows from
d\leq 2r+2
that
\binom{d^{\prime}}{2}\leq 2d^{\prime}+2
which holds only if
d^{\prime}=5
. For
d^{\prime}=5
, we have
d=10
and
r=5
. The condition that
\frac{q^{5}-1}{q-1}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
simplifies to
(p^{4e}+\cdots+p^{e}+1)\mid 2e(4p^{3e}+p^{2e}+p^{e}+4)
which never holds: a contradiction. This establishes the claim.

We claim that
V\otimes k
is not
S^{2}(W_{0})
up to weak equivalence for odd
p
. Suppose to the contrary that
V\otimes k=S^{2}(W_{0})
, so that it is the restriction of the
kG
-module
L(2\omega_{d^{\prime}})
. Similar to the
\wedge^{2}(W_{0})
case, we deduce that
f=2e
and so
V=S^{2}(W)
. The space
V
has a nondegenerate unitary form
\kappa
by
[
7
, Proposition 5.2.4]
. We have
d=\binom{d^{\prime}+1}{2}
, and
r=\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\rfloor\geq 3
. For
d^{\prime}=3
, we have
(d,r)=(6,3)
, and the condition that
\frac{q^{3}-1}{q-1}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
simplifies to
(p^{2e}+p^{e}+1)\mid 2e(2p^{e}+1)
which never holds. For
d^{\prime}=4
or
5
, we have
(d,r)=(10,5)
or
(15,7)
respectively and similarly
\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
does not divide
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
. Therefore, we have
d^{\prime}\geq 6
. It follows that
d\geq 21
and so
r\geq 10
. We deduce from
[
68
]
that
p^{fr}-1
has a primitive prime divisor
p^{\prime}
, and we have
p^{\prime}\geq 1+fr>2e
. Since
p^{\prime}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
, we deduce that
r\leq d^{\prime}-[\![2\mid d^{\prime}]\!]
. It follows from
d\leq 2r+2
that
\binom{d^{\prime}+1}{2}\leq 2d^{\prime}+2-2\cdot[\![2\mid d^{\prime}]\!]
. It does not hold for
d^{\prime}\geq 6
: a contradiction. This establishes the claim.

By Theorem
4.7
there is an integer
s
and an irreducible
k\tilde{S}
-module
M
such that
d=(\dim_{k}M)^{s}
. Moreover,
e\in\{fs,\frac{1}{2}fs\}
, and if
e=\frac{1}{2}fs
then
s
is odd. We have
\dim_{k}M\geq d^{\prime}
by
[
7
, Theorem 1.11.5]
, and
d\geq\binom{d^{\prime}+1}{2}+1
by
[
43
, Proposition 5.4.11]
and the preceding arguments. In particular, we have
d\geq 7
and so
r\geq 3
. We claim that
p^{fr}-1
has a primitive prime divisor. It suffices to show that
p^{fr}\neq 2^{6}
by
[
68
]
. For
s\geq 2
, we have either
d=9
or
d\geq 16
. It follows that
r=4
or
r\geq 7
, and so the claim holds for
s\geq 2
. Suppose that
s=1
and
p^{fr}=2^{6}
. Since
r\geq 3
, we have
(p^{f},r)=(2^{2},3)
or
(2,6)
. For
(p^{f},r)=(2^{2},3)
, we deduce from

\binom{d^{\prime}+1}{2}+1\leq d\leq 2r+2
that
d^{\prime}=3
in which case
7\leq d\leq 8
. We deduce that
e=2
, since
e\in\{1,2\}
and
\textup{PSU}_{3}(2)
is soluble. Then by
[
50
, A. 6]

V
is the adjoint module of
\textup{SU}_{3}(4)
of dimension
8
which was excluded in Section
2.3
. For
(p^{f},r)=(2,6)
we have
e=1
. The condition
\binom{d^{\prime}+1}{2}+1\leq d\leq 2r+2
implies
d^{\prime}=4
, and correspondingly
11\leq d\leq 14
. We deduce that
V
is the adjoint module of dimension
14
from
[
50
, A. 7]
which was excluded in Section
2.3
. Therefore,
p^{fr}\neq 2^{6}
and the claim also holds for
s=1
.

Let
p^{\prime}
be a primitive prime divisor of
p^{fr}-1
. We have
\gcd(p^{\prime},2fs)=1
as in the proof of Proposition
4.9
, and we deduce from
p^{\prime}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
that
fr\leq 2e(d^{\prime}-[\![2\mid d^{\prime}]\!])
. Since
d\leq 2r+2
, we have

(4.5)

\max\left\{d^{\prime s},\binom{d^{\prime}+1}{2}+1\right\}\leq d\leq 2+\frac{4e}{f}\cdot(d^{\prime}-[\![2\mid d^{\prime}]\!]).

If
e=\frac{1}{2}fs
with
s
odd, then (
4.5
) holds only for
(d^{\prime},s)=(3,1)
and correspondingly
d\in\{7,8\}
. We deduce from
[
50
, A. 6]
that
V\otimes k
is the restriction of
L(p^{i}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}))
for some
i
, and it can be realized over
{\mathbb{F}}_{p^{e}}
by
[
7
, Theorem 5.1.13]
: a contradiction to
e=\frac{1}{2}f
. If
e=fs
, then (
4.5
) holds only if
(d^{\prime},s)=(3,3)
, or
s\leq 2
and
d^{\prime}\leq 7
. By Theorem
4.7
(ii), we have
M={}^{\tau_{o}}M
and so
\dim_{k}M\geq d^{\prime 2}-1-[\![p\mid d^{\prime}]\!]
for
3\leq d^{\prime}\leq 7
by
[
50
]
. Therefore, we improve (
4.5
) to
(d^{\prime 2}-2)^{s}\leq d\leq 4(d^{\prime}-[\![2\mid d^{\prime}]\!])s+2
which holds only if
(d^{\prime},s)=(3,1)
or
(4,1)
. In both cases, we have
d\leq 14
. We deduce from
[
50
]
that up to weak equivalence
V
is the adjoint module, which was excluded in Section
2.3
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.14
.

If
S=\textup{P}\Omega_{d^{\prime}}^{-}(p^{e})
with
d^{\prime}\geq 8
, then
(d^{\prime},p^{e})=(8,q^{1/2})
,
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(7,q)
and the two
S
-orbits are respectively
(q^{3/2}+1)
- and
(q^{3}-q^{3/2})
-tight sets.

Proof.

Write
\ell=d^{\prime}/2\geq 4
, and let
W
be the natural module of
\Omega_{d^{\prime}}(p^{e})
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{p^{e}}
. We have
\tilde{S}=\textup{Spin}^{-}_{d^{\prime}}(p^{e})
,
G=\textup{Spin}_{d^{\prime}}(k)
, and
|\textup{Aut}(S)|=2ep^{e\ell(\ell-1)}(p^{e\ell}+1)\prod_{i=1}^{\ell-1}(p^{2ei}-1)
. If
V=W
, then
q=p^{e}
and
S
is transitive on the singular points of
Q^{-}(7,q)
. If
V
is the spin module of dimension
2^{\ell-1}
for
\tilde{S}
, then we have
q=p^{2e}
and
r=2^{\ell-2}
by
[
43
, Proposition 5.4.9 (iii)]
. Since
r
is a power of
2
and
r\geq 4
,
p^{fr}-1
has a primitive prime divisor
p^{\prime}
. We have
\gcd(p^{\prime},2e)=1
as usual and deduce from
p^{\prime}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
that
fr\leq 2e\ell
. It follows that
r=2^{\ell-2}\leq\ell
which holds only if
\ell=4
. The latter case was handled in Section
2.7
: there are two
S
-orbits which are respectively
(q^{3/2}+1)
- and
(q^{3}-q^{3/2})
-tight sets of
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(7,q)
.

We assume that
V
is neither the natural module for
\Omega_{d^{\prime}}(p^{e})
nor the spin module of dimension
2^{\ell-1}
for
\tilde{S}
in the sequel. We deduce that
d\geq\binom{d^{\prime}}{2}-1
by
[
43
, Proposition 5.4.11]
. In particular,
d\geq 27
and so the rank
r\geq 13
. Let
p^{\prime}
be a primitive prime divisor of
p^{fr}-1
, cf.
[
68
]
. By Theorem
4.7
there is an integer
s
and an irreducible
k\tilde{S}
-module
M
such that
d=(\dim_{k}M)^{s}
. Moreover,
e\in\{fs,\frac{1}{2}fs\}
, and if
e=\frac{1}{2}fs
then
s
is odd. We have
d=(\dim_{k}M)^{s}\geq d^{\prime s}
by
[
7
, Theorem 1.11.5]
. We have
\gcd(p^{\prime},2fs)=1
as in the proof of Proposition
4.9
and deduce from
p^{\prime}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
that
fr\leq 2e\ell
. Since
e\leq fs
, it follows that

\max\left\{d^{\prime s},\frac{1}{2}d^{\prime}(d^{\prime}-1)-1\right\}\leq d\leq 2+2d^{\prime}s
. It holds for no
(d^{\prime},s)
pairs with
d^{\prime}
even and
d^{\prime}\geq 8
: a contradiction. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.15
.

The group
S
is not one of
{}^{2}B_{2}(p^{2a+1})
with
2a+1>1
,
{}^{2}G_{2}(p^{2a+1})
with
2a+1>1
or
{}^{2}F_{4}(p^{2a+1})^{\prime}
, where
p=2,3,2
respectively.

Proof.

Suppose to the contrary that
S
is one of the three groups. By Theorem
4.7
there is an irreducible
k\tilde{S}
-module
M
such that
d=(\dim_{k}M)^{s}
, where
s:=\frac{2a+1}{f}
is an odd integer. For the type
{}^{2}B_{2}
, we have
\dim_{k}M=4^{a}
for an integer
a
by
[
57
, Theorem 12.2]
and an irreducible
4
-dimensional module has a nondegenerate alternating form.
It follows that
d\geq 16
and so
r\geq 7
for
{}^{2}B_{2}
. For the type
{}^{2}G_{2}
, there are nonnegative integers
b,c
such that
\dim_{k}M=7^{b}27^{c}
respectively. For the type
{}^{2}F_{4}
, we have
\dim_{k}M\geq 26
. We thus have
d\geq 7
,
26
and correspondingly
r\geq 3
,
12
for
{}^{2}G_{2}
and
{}^{2}F_{4}
respectively. We deduce that
p^{fr}-1
has a primitive prime divisor
p^{\prime}
in all three cases by
[
68
]
. We have
\gcd(p^{\prime},fs)=1
as usual and we deduce from
p^{\prime}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
that
fr
is upper bounded by
4fs
,
6fs
,
6fs
for the types
{}^{2}B_{2}
,
{}^{2}G_{2}
and
{}^{2}F_{4}
respectively. For
S={}^{2}B_{2}(p^{2a+1})
, we
deduce from
4^{as}\leq d\leq 2+2r\leq 2+8s
that
a=s=1
which contradicts
d\geq 16
. For
S={}^{2}G_{2}(p^{2a+1})
, we similarly deduce that
\dim_{k}M=7
and
s=1
, i.e.,
f=2a+1
. It follows that
d=7
and so
r=3
. The condition that
\frac{q^{3}-1}{q-1}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
simplifies to
(3^{2f}+3^{f}+1)\mid f(3^{f}-1)
which does not hold: a contradiction. For
S={}^{2}F_{4}(p^{2a+1})^{\prime}
, we have
26^{s}\leq d\leq 2+12s
which never holds: a contradiction. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.16
.

The group
S
is not one of
E_{6}(p^{e})
,
E_{7}(p^{e})
,
E_{8}(p^{e})
,
{}^{2}E_{6}(p^{e})
or
{}^{3}D_{4}(p^{e})
.

Proof.

First suppose that
S
is one of
E_{6}(p^{e})
,
E_{7}(p^{e})
and
E_{8}(p^{e})
. By Theorem
4.7
there is an irreducible
k\tilde{S}
-module
M
such that
d=(\dim_{k}M)^{s}
, where
s:=\frac{e}{f}
is an integer. We have
\dim_{k}M\geq R_{p}(S)
by
[
50
]
, where
R_{p}(S)=27
,
56
,
248
for
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
and
E_{8}
respectively. It follows that
d\geq 27
and so
r\geq 13
in each case. Then
p^{fr}-1
has a primitive prime divisor
p^{\prime}
by
[
68
]
. We have
\gcd(p^{\prime},2fs)=1
as in the proof of Proposition
4.9
, and we deduce from
p^{\prime}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
that
r\leq 12s
,
18s
,
30s
for
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
and
E_{8}
respectively. For
E_{6}
we deduce from
d\leq 2r+2
that
R_{p}(S)^{s}\leq 24s+2
which never holds. We derive similar contradictions for
E_{7}
and
E_{8}
.

Next suppose that
S={}^{t}X_{\ell}(p^{e})
with
{}^{t}X_{\ell}={}^{2}E_{6}
or
{}^{3}D_{4}
. By Theorem
4.7
, there is an integer
s
and an irreducible
k\tilde{S}
-module
M
such that
d=(\dim_{k}M)^{s}
. Moreover,
e\in\{fs,\frac{1}{t}fs\}
, and if
e=\frac{1}{t}fs
then the prime
t
divides
f
but not
s
. For
{}^{2}E_{6}
we have
\dim_{k}M\geq 27
and so
r\geq 13
. For
{}^{3}D_{4}
we have either
\dim_{k}M=8
or
\dim_{k}M\geq 26
by
[
50
, A. 41]
. If
S={}^{3}D_{4}(p^{e})
and
d=8
, then
q=p^{3e}
and
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(7,p^{3e})
by
[
7
, Theorem 5.6.1]
. We thus have either
r=4
or
r\geq\frac{d}{2}-1\geq 12
for
{}^{3}D_{4}
. Therefore,
p^{fr}-1
has a primitive prime divisor
p^{\prime}
in all cases by
[
68
]
. We have
\gcd(p^{\prime},tfs)=1
as usual and deduce from
p^{\prime}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
that:
fr
divides one of
\{8e,10e,12e,18e\}
if
S={}^{2}E_{6}(p^{e})
, and
fr
divides
12e
if
S={}^{3}D_{4}(p^{e})
. For
S={}^{2}E_{6}(p^{e})
, we have
27^{s}\leq d\leq 2+2r\leq 2+\frac{36e}{f}
. Since
e\in\{fs,\frac{1}{2}fs\}
, we deduce that
e=f
and
s=1
. It follows that
27\leq d\leq 38
, and we have
d=27
by
[
50
, A. 51]
. Then
r=13
which divides neither of
\{8,10,12,18\}
: a contradiction. For
S={}^{3}D_{4}(p^{e})
, we have
8^{s}\leq d\leq 2+\frac{24e}{f}
. Since
e\in\{fs,\frac{1}{3}fs\}
, we deduce that
s=1
. If
e=\frac{1}{3}f
, then
r\mid 4
and
8\leq d\leq 10
. It follows that
r=4
. The condition that
\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
simplifies to
(p^{2e}+1)\mid 6e(p^{e}+1)
which never holds. If
e=f
, then
r\mid 12
and
8\leq d\leq 26
. We have
M={}^{\tau_{o}}M
by Theorem
4.7
and so
\dim_{k}M\geq 26
by
[
50
, A. 41]
. It follows that
d=26
and
r=12
. The condition that
\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
simplifies to
(p^{2f}+1)(p^{f}+1)\mid 6f
which never holds. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.17
.

If
S=F_{4}(p^{e})
, then
q=3^{e}
,
{\mathcal{P}}=Q(24,q)
and the two
S
-orbits are respectively
(q^{4}+1)
- and
(q^{12}-q^{4})
-tight sets.

Proof.

We refer the reader to
[
12
]
for the case
p=3
and
[
24
, Remark 3.1]
for the case
p\neq 3
.
∎

Lemma 4.18
.

The group
S
can not be
G_{2}(p^{e})^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.7
there is an irreducible
k\tilde{S}
-module
M
such that
d=(\dim_{k}M)^{s}
, where
s:=\frac{e}{f}
is an integer. We have
\dim_{k}M\geq 7-[\![p=2]\!]
by
[
50
, A. 49]
. We first show that
p^{fr}-1
has a primitive prime divisor. For
s\geq 2
we have
d\geq 6^{2}
and so
r\geq 17
, so the claim holds in this case. Suppose that
s=1
. If
d=7-[\![p=2]\!]
, then there is exactly one such candidate module up to weak equivalence. We have
q=p^{e}
,
{\mathcal{P}}=W(6,q)
for
p=2
and
{\mathcal{P}}=Q(6,q)
for odd
p
by
[
7
, Propositions 5.7.1, 5.7.2]
. The group
S
is transitive on
{\mathcal{P}}=Q(6,q)
if
p
is odd by
[
67
, p. 125]
. Therefore, we have
d>7-[\![p=2]\!]
. It follows that
d=\dim_{k}M\geq 14
for
p\neq 3
and
d=\dim_{k}M\geq 27
for
p=3
by
[
50
, A. 49]
. We have
r\geq 6
in both cases. If
p^{fr}=2^{6}
, then we deduce that
(p^{f},r)=(2,6)
. It follows that
(p^{e},d)=(2,14)
, and we use the Atlas data
[
13
]
to check that there are more than two
K
-orbits on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(13,2)
. Therefore,
p^{fr}-1
also has a primitive prime divisor for
s=1
by
[
68
]
. This establishes the claim.

Let
p^{\prime}
be a primitive prime divisor of
p^{fr}-1
. We have
|\textup{Aut}(S)|=\epsilon eq^{6}(q^{2s}-1)(q^{6s}-1)
, where
\epsilon=2
or
1
according as
p=3
or not.
As usual, we have
\gcd(p^{\prime},2fs)=1
and deduce from the condition
p^{\prime}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
that
fr\mid 6fs
, i.e.,
r\mid 6s
. Since
d\leq 2r+2
, we have
\max\{6^{s},14\}\leq d\leq 2+12s
. It follows that
(s,d)=(1,14)
, and so
p\neq 3
. By
[
56
, Proposition 9.3.7]
, we have
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{\pm}(13,q)
where the sign
\pm
depends on
q
. Since
r\mid 6
, we deduce that
r=6
and so we expect
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(13,q)
. We claim that there is no integer
i
such that
\textup{lcm}(i,q^{7}+1-i)\cdot\frac{q^{6}-1}{q-1}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
, i.e.,
\textup{lcm}(i,q^{7}+1-i)
divides
fq^{6}(q^{2}-1)(q-1)
. Suppose to the contrary that there is such an integer
i
. It is clear that
i\neq 1,q^{7}
. If neither of
i,q^{7}+1-i
is a multiple of
p
, then
\textup{lcm}(i,q^{7}+1-i)
divides
f(q^{2}-1)(q-1)
. We deduce that
f(q^{2}-1)(q-1)\geq\frac{1}{2}(q^{7}+1)
which never holds. We assume without loss of generality that
i=up^{t}
with
p\nmid u
and
t>0
. It follows that
\textup{lcm}(u,q^{7}+1-up^{t})
divides
f(q^{2}-1)(q-1)
. We have
p^{t}\leq q^{7}-up^{t}\leq f(q^{2}-1)(q-1)-1<fq^{3}
, i.e.,
1+p^{t}\leq fq^{3}
. Since both
u
and
q^{7}+1-up^{t}
are at most
f(q^{2}-1)(q-1)
, the sum of
up^{t}
and
q^{7}+1-up^{t}
, namely
q^{7}+1
, is upper bounded by
(1+p^{t})f(q^{2}-1)(q-1)
. Since
1+p^{t}\leq fq^{3}
, we deduce that
q^{7}+1\leq f^{2}q^{3}(q^{2}-1)(q-1)
which never holds. This completes the proof.
∎

To summarize, we have handled the simple groups of Lie type in the defining characteristic in this subsection. We obtain the five infinite families of tight sets in Table
1.5
.

4.3.
The groups of Lie type in cross characteristic and sporadic groups

We first suppose that
S
is a simple group of Lie type
{}^{t}X_{\ell}({\mathbb{F}}_{q_{1}})
, where
q_{1}=p_{1}^{e}
with
p_{1}
a prime distinct from
p
. We assume that
S
is not one of
\textup{PSL}_{2}(4)=\textup{PSL}_{2}(5)
,
\textup{PSL}_{2}(9)
,
\textup{PSL}_{4}(2)
,
\textup{PSp}_{4}(2)^{\prime}
(these groups are isomorphic to alternating groups, which were handled previously). If
S=\textup{PSL}_{2}(7)=\textup{PSL}_{3}(2)
, then we assume that
p_{1}\neq 2,7
; if
S=\textup{PSp}_{4}(3)=\textup{PSU}_{4}(2)
, then we assume that
p_{1}\neq 2,3
; if
S=\textup{PSU}_{3}(3)=G_{2}(2)^{\prime}
, then we assume that
p_{1}\neq 2,3
. We follow the strategy as outlined in Remark
4.2
. By Lemma
2.1
, the dimension
d
of
V
is upper bounded by

(4.6)

d_{\max}:=2+\left\lfloor\log_{p_{2}}(2\cdot|\textup{Aut}(S)|)\right\rfloor,

where
p_{2}
is the smallest prime distinct from
p_{1}
. The value
d_{\max}
is roughly polynomial in
\ell
and linear in
\ln(q_{1})
. There is a lower bound
e(S)
on
d
by
[
45
]
which is roughly polynomial in
q_{1}^{\ell}
, see also
[
43
, Theorem 5.3.9]
. There has been extensive work that improves the bound in the literature, cf.
[
33
]
and the references therein, but the bound
e(S)
in
[
45
]
suffices for our purpose. It is straightforward to check that the condition
d_{\max}\geq e(S)
holds only in the following cases: (A)
S=\textup{PSL}_{2}(q_{1})
,
q_{1}\in\{7,11,13\}
, (B)
S=\textup{PSL}_{n}(q_{1})
,
(n,q_{1})\in\{(3,3),(3,4)\}
, (C)
S=\textup{PSp}_{2m}(q_{1})
,
(m,q_{1})\in\{(2,3),(3,2),(3,3)\}
, (D)
S=\textup{PSU}_{n}(q_{1})
,
(n,q_{1})\in\{(3,3),(3,4),(3,5),(4,3),(5,2),(6,2)\}
, (E)
S=\textup{P}\Omega_{8}^{+}(2)
or
\textup{P}\Omega_{7}(3)
, (F)
S=G_{2}(q_{1})^{\prime}
,
q_{1}\in\{3,4\}
. In particular,
S
is not one of
\textup{P}\Omega^{-}_{2m}(q_{1})
(
m\geq 4
),
E_{6}(q_{1})
,
E_{7}(q_{1})
,
E_{8}(q_{1})
,
F_{4}(q_{1})
,
{}^{2}E_{6}(q_{1})
,
{}^{3}D_{4}(q_{1})
,
{}^{2}F_{4}(q_{1})
,
{}^{2}B_{2}(q_{1})
or
{}^{2}G_{2}(q_{1})
. For the surviving cases, there are finitely many prime power
q
’s such that
d_{\max}^{\prime}\geq e(S)
, where

(4.7)

d_{\max}^{\prime}:=2+\left\lfloor\log_{q}(2\cdot|\textup{Aut}(S)|)\right\rfloor.

Recall that
L=H^{\infty}
and
K
is its normalizer in
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
.

Proposition 4.19
.

If
S
is a simple group of Lie type in characteristic distinct from
p
which is not isomorphic to an alternating group, then we have one of the following:

(1)

L=\textup{PSL}_{2}(11)
,
{\mathcal{P}}=H(4,4)
, and the two
S
-orbits are
11
- and
22
-tight sets respectively,

(2)

L=\textup{PSp}_{4}(3)
,
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(5,5)
, and the two
S
-orbits are
36
- and
90
-tight sets respectively,

(3)

L=3.\textup{PSU}_{4}(3)
,
{\mathcal{P}}=H(5,4)
, and the two
S
-orbits are
6
- and
27
-tight sets respectively,

(4)

L=G_{2}(3)
,
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(13,2)
, and the two
S
-orbits are
12
- and
117
-tight sets respectively.

Proof.

We continue with the arguments preceding the statement of this proposition. We call a
(q,r)
pair feasible for
S
if it satisfies that
\max(\{2,\lceil\frac{1}{2}e(S)\rceil-1\})\leq r\leq\lfloor\frac{1}{2}d_{\max}^{\prime}\rfloor
and
\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
. For (D) and (E), we exclude
\textup{PSU}_{3}(4)
,
\textup{PSU}_{3}(5)
,
\textup{PSU}_{6}(2)
,
\textup{P}\Omega_{7}(3)
by the fact that there is no feasible pair. The following three cases are excluded due to the fact that there is no candidate representation with Frobenius-Schur indicator
+
or
\circ
whose dimension is in the interval
[2r,2r+2]
:
S=\textup{PSU}_{5}(2)
, with
(3,4)
as the only feasible
(q,r)
pair;
S=\textup{PSL}_{3}(3)
, with
(5,4)
as the only feasible
(q,r)
pair;
S=G_{2}(4)
, with
(3,6)
as the only feasible
(q,r)
pair. For
S=\textup{PSU}_{3}(3)=G_{2}(2)^{\prime}
, we have
e(S)=6
and an irreducible representation of dimension
6
has Frobenius-Schur indicator
-
by
[
32
]
. Hence we have
d\geq 7
and so
r\geq 3
. There is no feasible
(q,r)
pair with
r\geq 3
,
p\neq 2,3
for such a
S
.

Suppose that
S=\textup{PSL}_{2}(7)=\textup{PSL}_{3}(2)
. For a feasible
(q,r)
pair we have
r=2
, and
q
is one of
3
,
5
,
11
,
13
,
23
,
27
,
41
,
47
,
83
and
167
. It follows that
d\leq 6
. Since each
q
is not a square, we expect
{\mathcal{P}}
to be orthogonal. By
[
32
]
, there is exactly one candidate representation of dimension
d=6
with Frobenius-Schur indicator
+
. The condition
d\leq d_{\max}^{\prime}
holds only for
q=3,5
, where
d_{\max}^{\prime}
is as in (
4.7
). The condition
|\textup{Aut}(S)|\geq\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{P}}|
does not hold for
q=5
, and the normalizer
K
has three orbits on
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(5,3)
for
q=3
.

Suppose that
S=\textup{PSL}_{2}(11)
. For a feasible
(q,r)
pair, either it is one of
\{(2,4),(3,4)\}
, or
r=2
and
q
is one of
18
integers; moreover, if
q
is a square, then
(q,r)\in\{(4,2),(9,2)\}
. It follows that
d\leq 2r+2\leq 10
, and if
{\mathcal{P}}
is unitary then
q\in\{4,9\}
and
d\in\{4,5\}
. By
[
32
]
, there are two candidate representations up to weak equivalence: (i)
d=5
, with Frobenius-Schur indicator
\circ
and quadratic irrationality
\sqrt{-11}
, (ii)
d=10
, with Frobenius-Schur indicator
+
. For (i),
X^{2}+X+3=0
has roots in
{\mathbb{F}}_{3}
and
V
does not have a unitary form for
q=3^{2}
by
[
7
, Corollary 4.4.2]
. If
q=4
, then
S
has two orbits of sizes
55
,
110
respectively on
{\mathcal{P}}=H(4,4)
by a computer check. For (ii), we examine the list of feasible pairs to see that
r=4
and
q\in\{2,3\}
. Since
d=2r+2
, we expect
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(9,q)
. The condition
|\textup{Aut}(S)|\geq\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{P}}|
does not hold for
q=3
, and there is no integer
i
such that
\textup{lcm}(i,2^{4}+1-i)\cdot(2^{4}-1)
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
for
q=2
.

Suppose that
S=\textup{PSL}_{2}(13)
. For a feasible
(q,r)
pair, either it is one of
(2,3)
,
(3,3)
,
(4,3)
,
(9,3)
,
(16,3)
,
(5,4)
and
(3,6)
, or
r=2
and
q
is one of
19
integers. The pairs
(9,3)
,
(16,3)
,
(5,4)
and
(3,6)
are excluded by the condition
d_{\max}^{\prime}\geq d\geq 2r
, where
d_{\max}^{\prime}
is as in (
4.7
). It follows that
r\leq 3
, and so
d\leq 8
. By
[
32
]
up to weak equivalence there is exactly one candidate representation of dimension
d=7
whose Frobenius-Schur indicator is
+
for odd
p
, and
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
is the splitting field of
X^{2}-13
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
. We then have

, so

for
(q,r)
to be a feasible pair. It turns out that the normalizer
K
has three orbits on
{\mathcal{P}}=Q(6,3)
.

Suppose that
S=\textup{PSL}_{3}(4)
. For a feasible
(q,r)
pair, either it is
(3,4)
, or
r=2
and
q
is one of
57
integers; moreover, if

is a square then

. We have

, and so

. If
{\mathcal{P}}
is unitary, then
(q,r)=(9,2)
and

. There is exactly one such candidate representation of dimension

in which case

up to weak equivalence, and

is transitive on
{\mathcal{P}}=H(3,9)
by direct check. If

is orthogonal, then there is exactly one such candidate representation whose dimension is in the range
[4,8]
and Frobenius-Schur indicator is
+
up to weak equivalence by
[

]
. For such a representation we have

, and

is transitive on

by direct check.

Suppose that
S=\textup{PSp}_{4}(3)=\textup{PSU}_{4}(2)
. For a feasible
(q,r)
pair with
p\neq 2,3
, we have
r=2
and
q=p
is one of
33
primes. It follows that
{\mathcal{P}}
is orthogonal and

. There is exactly one candidate representation up to weak equivalence whose dimension is in the range
[4,6]
and Frobenius-Schur indicator is
+
by
[
32
]
, and such a representation has dimension
d
. Since

, we expect
{\mathcal{P}}
to be
Q^{-}(5,p)
. We deduce from
|\textup{Aut}(S)|\geq\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{P}}|
that
p\leq 17
. There is an integer
i
such that
(p+1)\cdot\textup{lcm}(i,p^{3}+1-i)
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
only if

or
7
. For

, we have
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(5,5)
and there are exactly two
S
-orbits of sizes

. Their sizes are not divisible by the ovoid number

, so the two orbits are both tight sets. For

, we have

and so

: a contradiction.

Suppose that
S=\textup{PSp}_{6}(2)
. Then
(e(S),d_{\max})=(7,24)
, so
3\leq r\leq 12
. The only feasible
(q,r)
pair is
(3,4)
. It follows that
8\leq d\leq 10
, and
{\mathcal{P}}
is orthogonal since
q
is not a square. There is exactly one candidate representation which has dimension
8
up to weak equivalence, for which we have
L=2.\textup{PSp}_{6}(2)
. The group

is transitive on
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(7,3)
by direct check.

Suppose that
S=\textup{PSp}_{6}(3)
. Then
e(S)=13
, and so
r\geq 6
. We deduce from
d_{\max}^{\prime}\geq d\geq e(S)
that
q\leq 8
, where
d_{\max}^{\prime}
is as in (

). A feasible
(q,r)
pair with

is one of
(2,6)
,
(4,6)
and
(2,12)
. If
{\mathcal{P}}
is unitary, then
(q,r)=(4,6)
and

. There is exactly one such candidate representation up to weak equivalence which has dimension
13
by
[
32
]
. It turns out that
{\mathcal{P}}=H(12,4)
and there are four
K
-orbits of different sizes on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
. There is no candidate representation whose dimension is in the ranges
[13,14]
and
[24,26]
with a Frobenius-Schur indicator
+
by
[
32
]
.

Suppose that
S=\textup{PSU}_{4}(3)
. Then
e(S)=6
and a candidate representation of dimension
6
has Frobenius-Schur indicator
\circ
by
[
32
]
. We deduce that

in all cases. A feasible
(q,r)
pair with

is one of
(2,3)
,
(4,3)
,
(2,4)
and
(2,6)
. It follows that

and
6\leq d\leq 12
. The only candidate representation has dimension
d=6
and Frobenius-Schur indicator
\circ
by
[
32
]
. We thus expect
{\mathcal{P}}=H(5,q)
with
q
square, so that
