. There is exactly one conjugacy class of
\textup{PSU}_{4}(3)
in
\textup{PGU}_{6}(2)
, and it has two orbits of sizes
126
,
567
on
{\mathcal{P}}=H(5,4)
which are
6
- and
27
-tight sets respectively.

Suppose that
S=\textup{P}\Omega^{+}_{8}(2)
. We have
e(S)=8
, so the rank
r\geq 3
. A feasible
(q,r)
pair with
r\geq 3
is either
(3,4)
or
(7,4)
. It follows that
8\leq d\leq 10
. There is exactly one candidate representation of dimension
8
whose Frobenius-Schur indicator is
+
up to weak equivalence by
[
32
]
, and the corresponding covering group is
L=2.\textup{P}\Omega^{+}_{8}(2)
. We first realize
2.\textup{P}\Omega^{+}_{8}(2)
as the Weyl group of the
E_{8}
-root lattice in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
generated by the
120
reflections in the
240
root vectors and then take tensor with
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
to get the representation in Magma, cf.
[
13
]
. The group
S
is transitive on
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(7,3)
for
q=3
, and there are three
K
-orbits on
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(7,7)
for
p=7
.

Suppose that
S=G_{2}(3)
. Then
e(S)=14
, and so
r\geq 6
. The only feasible
(q,r)
pair is
(2,6)
. Since
e(S)\leq d\leq 2r+2
, we deduce that
d=14
. There is exactly one such candidate representation whose Frobenius-Schur indicator is
+
up to weak equivalence by
[
32
]
. We check by Magma
[
6
]
that
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(13,2)
and
S
has two orbits on the singular points which are
12
- and
117
-tight sets respectively. This completes the proof.
∎

We next consider the case where
S
is a sporadic group. We also take the same approach as outlined in Remark
4.2
. By
[
48
, Theorem 2.3.2]
there is a lower bound
R(S)
on the dimension
d
, see also
[
43
, p. 187]
. We define
d_{\max}^{\prime}
as in (
4.7
) which is also an upper bound on
d
by Lemma
2.1
.

Proposition 4.20
.

If
S
is a sporadic simple group, then we have one of the following:

(1)

L=M_{11}
,
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(9,2)
and
S
has two orbits on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
which are
11
- and
22
-tight sets;

(2)

L=3.M_{22}
,
{\mathcal{P}}=H(5,4)
and
S
has two orbits on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
which are
11
- and
22
-tight sets;

(3)

L=Co_{1}
,
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(23,2)
and
S
has two orbits on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
which are
24
- and
2025
-tight sets.

Proof.

We call a
(q,r)
pair feasible for
S
if
\max(\{2,\lceil\frac{1}{2}R(S)\rceil\}-1)\leq r\leq\lfloor\frac{1}{2}d_{\max}^{\prime}\rfloor
and
\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
divides
|\textup{Aut}(S)|
. For
S=Fi_{23}
,
Fi_{24}^{\prime}
,
HN
,
Ly
,
BM
,
F_{1}
or
He
, the condition
d_{\max}^{\prime}\geq R(S)
does not hold for any
q
. For
S=M_{23}
or
M_{24}
,
R(S)=11
and the only feasible
(q,r)
pair is
(2,6)
. For
S=J_{2}
,
R(S)=6
and we have
2\leq r\leq 6
for all feasible
(q,r)
pairs. There is no candidate representation with Frobenius-Schur indicator
+
or
\circ
whose dimension is the interval
[2r,2r+2]
in each case by
[
32
]
.

For
S=M_{11}
,
R(S)=5
and a feasible
(q,r)
pair either has
r=2
or is one of
(2,4)
and
(3,4)
. It follows that
5\leq d\leq 10
. There are two candidate representations which have dimension
10
and Frobenius-Schur indicator
+
up to weak equivalence by
[
32
]
, one defined over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
and the other over
{\mathbb{F}}_{3}
. For
q=2
, we have
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(9,2)
and
S
has two orbits on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
which are
11
- and
22
-tight sets respectively. For
q=3
, it turns out that
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(9,3)
and so
r=5
: a contradiction to
r=4
.

For
S=M_{12}
,
R(S)=6
and a feasible
(q,r)
pair either has
r=2
or is one of
(2,4)
,
(3,4)
. It follows that
6\leq d\leq 10
. There are two candidate representations which have dimension
10
and Frobenius-Schur indicator
+
up to weak equivalence by
[
32
]
, one defined over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
and the other over
{\mathbb{F}}_{3}
. For
q=2
, we have
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(9,2)
and
S
is transitive on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
. For
q=3
, we have
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(9,3)
and so
r=5
: a contradiction to
r=4
.

For
S=M_{22}
,
R(S)=6
and a feasible
(q,r)
pair either has
r=2
or is one of
(2,3)
,
(2,4)
,
(2,6)
,
(4,3)
and
(3,4)
. It follows that
6\leq d\leq 12
. There is exactly one candidate representation of dimension
6
defined over
{\mathbb{F}}_{4}
with Frobenius-Schur indicator
\circ
up to weak equivalence by
[
32
]
. It turns out that
{\mathcal{P}}=H(5,4)
,
L=3.M_{22}
and
S
has two orbits on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
which are
11
- and
22
-tight sets respectively.

For
S=J_{1}
,
R(S)=7
and a feasible
(q,r)
pair is one of
(2,3)
,
(4,3)
,
(7,3)
,
(11,3)
,
(2,4)
and
(3,4)
. It follows that
7\leq d\leq 10
. There is exactly one candidate representation of dimension
7
defined over
{\mathbb{F}}_{11}
with Frobenius-Schur indicator
+
up to weak equivalence by
[
32
]
. It turns out that
{\mathcal{P}}=Q(6,11)
and the normalizer
K
has more that two orbits on the singular points.

For
S=J_{3}
,
R(S)=9
and a feasible
(q,r)
pair is one of
(2,4)
,
(2,8)
,
(3,4)
and
(4,4)
. It follows that
9\leq d\leq 10
. There is exactly one candidate representation of dimension
9
defined over
{\mathbb{F}}_{4}
with Frobenius-Schur indicator
\circ
up to weak equivalence by
[
32
]
. It turns out that
{\mathcal{P}}=H(8,4)
and
S
is transitive on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
.

For
S=Suz
,
R(S)=12
and a feasible
(q,r)
pair is one of
(2,6)
,
(2,12)
,
(3,6)
and
(4,6)
. It follows that
12\leq d\leq 26
. There is exactly one candidate representation of dimension
12
defined over
{\mathbb{F}}_{4}
with Frobenius-Schur indicator
\circ
up to weak equivalence by
[
32
]
. It turns out that
{\mathcal{P}}=H(11,4)
and the normalizer
K
has three orbits on the singular points.

For
S=Co_{1}
,
R(S)=24
and the only feasible pair is
(q,r)=(2,12)
. It follows that
24\leq d\leq 26
. There is exactly one candidate representation of dimension
24
whose Frobenius-Schur indicator is
+
up to weak equivalence by
[
32
]
. It turns out that
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(23,2)
,
L=Co_{1}
and
S
has exactly two orbits on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
which are
24
- and
2025
-tight sets respectively. This completes the proof.
∎

To summarize, we have established Theorem
1.3
for the case where
H
is a subgroup of Aschbacher class
{\mathcal{S}}
in this section. The resulting examples are collected in Tables
1.5
and
1.6
. In particular, the case where
\kappa
is symplectic is handled in Proposition
4.1
based on the main theorem in
[
47
]
.

5.
Proof of Theorem
1.3
: the geometric subgroups

In this section, we present the proof of Theorem
1.3
for the case of geometric subgroups. Suppose that
V
is a
d
-dimensional vector space over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
that is equipped with a nondegenerate quadratic, unitary or symplectic form
\kappa
. Let
{\mathcal{P}}
be the associated polar space, and suppose that it has rank
r\geq 2
and is not
Q^{+}(3,q)
. Let
H
be an irreducible group of semisimilarities that has two orbits
O_{1}
,
O_{2}
on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
which are respectively
i_{1}
- and
i_{2}
-tight sets, and assume that
H
is of Aschbacher class
{\mathcal{C}}_{2},\ldots,{\mathcal{C}}_{7}
or
{\mathcal{C}}_{8}
. Here, we say that
H
is of Aschbacher class
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
if it is contained in a maximal subgroup of Aschbacher class
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
in
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
, cf.
[
1
]
. Let
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}
be the largest extension field of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
such that
H
is a subgroup of
\Gamma\textup{L}_{d/b}(q^{b})
. Let
E
be the corresponding copy of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}
in
\textup{End}(V)
, and regard
V
as a vector space
V^{\prime}
of dimension
d/b
over
E
. There is a nondegenerate reflexive sesquilinear form or quadratic form
\kappa^{\prime}
on
V^{\prime}
such that
(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
,
(V,\kappa)
are associated as in Table
2.2
. Let
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
be the polar space associated with
(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
. The group
H
is a subgroup of
\Gamma^{\#}(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
, where
\Gamma^{\#}(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
is defined in (
2.2
). We write
\Gamma^{\#}
for
\Gamma^{\#}(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
and
\Delta^{\#}
for its intersection with
\Delta(V,\kappa)
for short.

Lemma 5.1
.

If
b>1
, then
V^{\prime}
has a nonzero singular or isotropic vector.

Proof.

Suppose to the contrary that
b>1
and
V^{\prime}
has no nonzero singular or isotropic vector. This happens exactly when
d=b
for the rows 4, 5, 7, 9, 12 and when
d=2b
for the row 3 by Table
2.2
. We observe that the case
d=2b
for the row 3 reduces to the case
d=b
for the row 9, so we only need to consider the first five cases with
d=b
. It is routine to check that the conditions
\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
divides
\textup{P}\Gamma^{\#}(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
and
\textup{P}\Gamma^{\#}(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})\geq\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{P}}|
exclude rows 4, 5, 7, 12. For row 9, we have
d=2r+2
,
|\textup{P}\Gamma^{\#}(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})|=df(q^{r+1}+1)
, and
|{\mathcal{P}}|=(q^{r+1}+1)\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
. The two conditions simplify to
(q^{r}-1)\mid f(q^{2}-1)
,
2f(2r+2)(q-1)\geq q^{r}-1
, which hold only for a finite list of
(q,r)
pairs. For such a
(q,r)
pair, we examine whether there is an integer
i
such that
\textup{lcm}(i,q^{r+1}+1-i)\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
divides
f(2r+2)(q^{r+1}+1)
. A
(q,d)
pair that satisfies all the conditions either is
(2,10)
, or has
d=6
and
q\in\{7,3^{2},11,2^{4},3^{3},2^{5},2^{6}\}
. We use Magma to check that
\Gamma^{\#}(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
has at least three orbits on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
in each case. This completes the proof.
∎

We first consider the case where
b>1
. Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
be as defined in (
2.3
), i.e.,
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}=\{\langle\eta v\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}\mid\eta\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}^{*},\,\langle v\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}\}
. Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
be its complement in
{\mathcal{P}}
. The set
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
is nonempty by Lemma
5.1
, and the set
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
is empty only for row 1, rows 9 (with
b=2
) and row 10 (with
b=2
) upon inspection of Table
2.2
. We define

(5.1)

O_{i}^{\prime}=\{\langle v\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}}\mid\langle v\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}\in O_{i}\}\quad\textup{ for }i=1,2.

Proposition 5.2
.

If
{\mathcal{M}}_{1},{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
are proper subsets of
{\mathcal{P}}
, then we have one of the cases in Table
1.2
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
are the two
H
-orbits.

Proof.

Write
q=p^{f}
with
p
prime, and set
b^{\prime}=b
or
b^{\prime}=b/2
according as
\kappa^{\prime}
is orthogonal or unitary. Let
\alpha
be as in Table
2.2
for rows 8, 12, and set
\alpha=1
for the remaining rows. Since
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
is nonempty, we can not have row 1, row 9 (with
b=2
) or row 10 (with
b=2
) of Table
2.2
. Since the set of singular points of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
is a trivial intriguing set, the set
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
is a tight set for rows 2, 6, 8, 10 of Table
2.2
and is an
m
-ovoid for rows 3, 5, 7, 9 by
[
41
]
. It is routine to check that the condition
\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
divides
|{\mathcal{M}}_{1}|
does not hold for rows 4, 11, and it holds for row 12 only if
b=2
. Therefore, we only need to consider rows 2, 6, 8, 10 (with
b>2
), 12 (with
b=2
) of Table
2.2
. In all those cases
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
is orthogonal or unitary. Since
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
is nonempty by Lemma
5.1
, we deduce that
\{\kappa^{\prime}(x)\textup{ or }\kappa^{\prime}(x,x)\mid x\in V^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}\}={\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b^{\prime}}}
. We set
X:=\{\kappa^{\prime}(x)\textup{ or }\kappa^{\prime}(x,x)\mid\langle x\rangle\in{\mathcal{M}}_{2}\}
, and write
\lambda(\Delta^{\#}):=\{\lambda(g)\mid g\in\Delta^{\#}\}
, where
\lambda(g)
is as in (
2.1
). We have
\kappa=\textup{Tr}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q_{0}^{b}}/{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}\circ\alpha\kappa^{\prime}
with
q_{0}\in\{q,q^{1/2}\}
by Table
2.2
, so
X=\{a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b^{\prime}}}^{*}\mid\textup{Tr}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q_{0}^{b}}/{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(\alpha a)=0\}
correspondingly.

Since
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
are
\Gamma^{\#}
-invariant, they are the two
H
-orbits by our assumption. It follows that
\Gamma^{\#}
should be transitive on both
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
. The group
\Gamma^{\#}
is transitive on
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
by Witt’s Lemma. We now determine when
\Gamma^{\#}
is transitive on
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
. Take a point
\langle v\rangle
in
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
, and write
\eta:=\kappa^{\prime}(v)
or
\kappa^{\prime}(v,v)
. Since
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
is the
\Gamma^{\#}
-orbit of
\langle v\rangle
, we deduce that
X=X^{\prime}
with
X^{\prime}=\{\lambda(g)\eta^{\sigma(g)}\mid g\in\Gamma^{\#}\}
. Conversely, if
X^{\prime}=X
then we deduce from Witt’s Lemma that
\Gamma^{\#}
is transitive on
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
.

(1)

For row 2 of Table
2.2
we have
\lambda(\Delta^{\#})={\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
and
\alpha=1
, so
X^{\prime}=\{a\eta^{p^{i}}\mid i\geq 0,a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}\}
. We also have
X=\{a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}^{*}\mid\textup{Tr}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}/{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(a)=0\}
. It follows from
X=X^{\prime}
that
bf(q-1)
is divisible by
q^{b-1}-1
. This holds only if
b=2
or
(p^{f},b)\in\{(2,3),(8,3)\}
, and we have
X=X^{\prime}
in those cases.

(2)

For row 6 of Table
2.2
we have
\lambda(\Delta^{\#})={\mathbb{F}}_{q^{1/2}}^{*}
and
\alpha=1
, so
X^{\prime}=\{a\eta^{p^{i}}\mid i\geq 0,a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{1/2}}^{*}\}
. We also have
X=\{a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b/2}}^{*}\mid\textup{Tr}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b/2}}/{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{1/2}}}(a)=0\}
. It follows from
X=X^{\prime}
that
q^{(b-1)/2}-1
divides
(q^{1/2}-1)bf/2
. This holds only if
(p^{f},b)\in\{(2^{2},3),(2^{6},3)\}
, in which cases we do have
X=X^{\prime}
.

(3)

For row 8 of Table
2.2
we have
X={\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b/2}}^{*}
. We choose
v
properly so that
\eta=1\in X
. In this case
\lambda(\Delta^{\#})={\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b/2}}^{*}\cap{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}={\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
, since
b
is even. It follows that
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}\subseteq X^{\prime}
. We have
\kappa^{\prime}(vg,vg)=\lambda(g)\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}\cdot\alpha^{\sigma(g)-1}
for
g\in\Gamma^{\#}
by (
2.2
). It follows that
X
is a subset of
X^{\prime\prime}=\{\alpha^{p^{i}-1}a\mid a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*},i\geq 0\}
. Since
\alpha^{q^{b/2}}+\alpha=0
, the latter set has size at most
bf(q-1)/2
. We deduce from
X\subseteq X^{\prime\prime}
that
q^{b/2}-1\leq bf(q-1)/2
, which holds only if
b=2
. When
b=2
, we have
X=X^{\prime}={\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
by comparing sizes.

(4)

For row 10 of Table
2.2
with
b>2
, we have
\lambda(\Delta^{\#})={\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
and
\alpha=1
, so
X^{\prime}=\{a\eta^{p^{i}}\mid i\geq 0,a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}\}
. We also have
X=\{a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b/2}}^{*}\mid\textup{Tr}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b/2}}/{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(a)=0\}
. Since
\alpha=1
, we deduce from
X=X^{\prime}
that
q^{b/2-1}-1
divides
(q-1)bf/2
. It holds only if
b=4
or
(p^{f},b)\in\{(2,6),(2^{3},6)\}
, and
X=X^{\prime}
holds in each case.

(5)

For row 12 of Table
2.2
with
b=2
, we have
X=\{u\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}^{*}\mid\textup{Tr}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}/{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(\alpha u)=0\}
. Since
\eta
is in
X
, we have
X={\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}\cdot\eta
. In this case
\lambda(\Delta^{\#})
is the intersection of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
with the set of nonzero squares of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}^{*}
, i.e.,
\lambda(\Gamma^{\#})={\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
. It follows that
X^{\prime}
contains
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}\cdot\eta
. We deduce that
X=X^{\prime}
by comparing sizes.

We list the above cases where
\Gamma^{\#}
is transitive on both
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
in Table
1.2
. It remains to the determine the subgroups of
\Gamma^{\#}
that are transitive on both
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
.

(i)

For row 2 of Table
2.2
, we have
d\geq 6
since
\kappa
has plus sign and we do not consider the case
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(3,q)
. Since
H
is transitive on both
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
, we deduce that it is transitive on both the set of singular points and the isometry class of nonsingular points of
(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
that contains
\langle v\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}}
. We consider only the case
d/b\geq 6
. By Lemmas
2.6
and the fact
q^{b}
is a square or cube, we deduce that either
\Omega_{d/2}^{+}(q^{b})\unlhd H
, or
\textup{SU}_{d/(2b)}(q^{b})\unlhd H
with
d/(2b)
even, or
\textup{Spin}_{7}(q^{b})\unlhd H
with
d/b=8
.

(ii)

For row 6 of Table
2.2
, we have
b=3
and
q\in\{2^{2},2^{6}\}
. Since
H
is transitive on the singular points of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
, we deduce that either
\textup{SU}_{d/3}(q^{3/2})\unlhd H
or
(d/b,q)=(3,4)
by
[
28
, Theorem 4.1]
.

(iii)

For row 8 of Table
2.2
, we have
b=2
and
d/2
is even by Table
2.2
. Since
H
is transitive on both
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
, we deduce that it is transitive on both the singular points and the nonsingular points of
(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
. By Lemma
2.3
, we see that either
\Omega(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})\unlhd H
or
(q,d)=(2,4)
.

(iv)

For row 10 of Table
2.2
,
d/b
is even. Since
H
is transitive on both
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
, we deduce that it is transitive on both the set of singular points and the set of nonsingular points of
(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
. Since
q^{b}
is a
b
-th power with
b\in\{4,6\}
, we deduce that
\textup{SU}_{d/b}(q^{b/2})\unlhd H
by Lemma
2.3
.

(v)

For row 12 of Table
2.2
, we have
b=2
. Since
H
is transitive on both
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
, we deduce that it is transitive on both the set of singular points and one isometry class of nonsingular points of
(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
. By Lemma
2.4
, we have either
\Omega_{d/2}(q^{2})\unlhd H
, or
G_{2}(q^{2})\unlhd H
with
d=14
.

This completes the proof.
∎

By Lemma
5.1
and Proposition
5.2
, it remains to consider the case where
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}={\mathcal{P}}
for
b>1
. In such a case,
(V,\kappa)
and
(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
have the same set of nonzero isotropic or singular vectors. Since
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}={\mathcal{P}}
, we are in rows 1, 9 (with
b=2
) and 10 (with
b=2
) of Table
2.2
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Suppose that
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}={\mathcal{P}}
and
b>1
, and let
O_{1}^{\prime}
,
O_{2}^{\prime}
be as in (
5.1
). If one of
O_{1}^{\prime}
,
O_{2}^{\prime}
consists of all points of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
, then we have the two rows in the first block of Table
1.3
.

Proof.

We assume without loss of generality that
O_{1}^{\prime}={\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
, and let
Y
be the set of nonzero isotropic or singular vectors of
(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
. Let
Z,~\tilde{Z}
be the center of
\textup{GL}(V)
and
\textup{GL}(V^{\prime})
respectively. Since
H
is transitive on
O_{1}
, it is transitive on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
. Therefore,
HZ
is not transitive on
Y
while
H\tilde{Z}
does. The
HZ
-orbits on
Y
are in bijection with the
H
-orbits on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
, so
HZ
has two orbits
Y_{1}
,
Y_{2}
on
Y
. Since
HZ
is normal in
H\tilde{Z}
, there is an element
\eta\in\tilde{Z}
such that
Y_{1}\eta=Y_{2}
. It follows that
[H\tilde{Z}:\,HZ]
is even. In particular,
[\tilde{Z}:\,Z]=\frac{q^{b}-1}{q-1}
is even which forces
q
to be odd and
b
to be even. Since
\Omega(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
is transitive on
Y
by Witt’s Lemma,
H
does not contain
\Omega(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
. By the choice of
b
,
H
is not a subgroup of
\Gamma(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{3}
.

Suppose that we are in the situation of row 1 of Table
2.2
, so that
{\mathcal{P}}=W(d-1,q)
and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}=W(d/b-1,q^{b})
with
d/b
even. Since
H
is transitive on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
and
q^{b}
is an odd square, we deduce that
(q,d/2,H^{\infty})=(3,2,\textup{SL}_{2}(5))
by
[
28
, Theorems 3.1, 5.2]
. We use the Atlas data
[
13
]
to verify that
\textup{SL}_{2}(5)
has exactly two orbits of sizes
40
on the nonzero vectors of
\kappa^{\prime}
. Both orbits are
{\mathbb{F}}_{3}^{*}
-invariant and they yield
5
-tight sets of
{\mathcal{P}}=W(3,3)
respectively.

Suppose that we are in the situation of row 9 or 10 of Table
2.2
with
b=2
, so that
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{\mp}(d-1,q)
and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}=H(d/2-1,q^{2})
. Since
{\mathcal{P}}
has rank
r\geq 2
and we do not consider
Q^{+}(3,q)
, we have
d\geq 6
. Since
H
is transitive on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
and
q
is odd, we deduce that
(d/2,q,H^{\infty})
is one of
(3,3,\textup{PSL}_{2}(7))
,
(3,5,3.A_{7})
,
(4,3,4.\textup{PSL}_{3}(4))
by
[
28
, Theorem 4.1]
. For the triple
(3,5,3.A_{7})
, the two
3.A_{7}
-orbits are both
3
-ovoids of
Q^{-}(5,5)
. For the triple
(3,3,\textup{PSL}_{2}(7))
,
\textup{PSL}_{2}(7)
has four orbits of sizes
56
which are
{\mathbb{F}}_{3}^{*}
-invariant and its normalizer in
\Delta^{\#}(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
has two orbits which are both
14
-tight sets of
Q^{-}(5,3)
. For the triple
(4,3,4.\textup{PSL}_{3}(4))
,
4.\textup{PSL}_{3}(4)
is transitive on the set
Y
of nonzero singular vectors of
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(7,3)
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 5.4
.

Suppose that
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}={\mathcal{P}}
and
b>1
, and write
r^{\prime}
for the rank of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
. Let
O_{1}^{\prime}
,
O_{2}^{\prime}
be as in (
5.1
), and assume that they are both proper subsets of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
. If
r^{\prime}=1
, then either
({\mathcal{P}},{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime})=(W(3,q),W(1,q^{2}))
and
H
occurs in (T1-a) of Theorem
1.3
, or it occurs in the second block of Table
1.3
.

Proof.

The set
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
is nonempty by Lemma
5.1
. There are two such cases that satisfy the hypothesis in the proposition: (1)
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}=W(1,q^{2})
and
{\mathcal{P}}=W(3,q)
, (2)
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}=H(2,q^{2})
and
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(5,q)
. Let
\tilde{Z}
be the center of
\textup{GL}(V^{\prime})
. Let
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
be the sizes of the two
H
-orbits on
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
. We have
s_{1}+s_{2}=|{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}|
,
\textup{lcm}(s_{1},s_{2})
divides
[H\tilde{Z}:\,\tilde{Z}]
, and so
[H\tilde{Z}:\,\tilde{Z}]\geq\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}|
. The group
H
is an irreducible subgroup of
\Gamma(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
which is not of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{3}
by the choice of
b
, and it is not of
{\mathcal{C}}_{4}
,
{\mathcal{C}}_{7}
by comparing dimensions. We consider the two cases separately.

We first consider the case where
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}=W(1,q^{2})
and
{\mathcal{P}}=W(3,q)
. Take a basis
e_{1},e_{2}
of
V^{\prime}
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}
, and let
\phi
be the semilinear mapping of
V^{\prime}
such that
x_{1}e_{1}+x_{2}e_{2}\mapsto x_{1}^{p}e_{1}+x_{2}^{p}e_{2}
. If
H
is of class
{\mathcal{S}}
, then we have
H^{\infty}=2.A_{5}
and
q=p\equiv 3\pmod{10}
by
[
7
, Table 8.2]
. We deduce from
[H\tilde{Z}:\,\tilde{Z}]\geq\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}|
that
q\in\{3,7\}
. The group
2.A_{5}
is transitive on
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
for
q=3
and has two orbits on
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
of sizes
20,~30
respectively for
q=7
. The latter yields
40
,
60
-tight sets of
{\mathcal{P}}=W(3,7)
. If
H
is of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
as a subgroup of
\Gamma(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
, then it is of the same class as a subgroup of
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
which is covered by (T1-a) of Theorem
1.3
. It is not of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{6}
as a subgroup of
\Gamma(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
by
[
43
, Table 4.6.B]
and the fact that
q^{2}
is not a prime. If it is of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{8}
, then the condition
\textup{P}\Gamma^{\#}|\geq\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{P}}|
is not satisfied.
If
H
is of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{5}
, then it stabilizes a subline of size
q_{0}+1
, where
q^{2}=q_{0}^{s}
with
s
prime. By
[
47
, Case (IIc)]
and the fact
\dim(V^{\prime})=2
, we have
s\in\{2,3\}
and
H^{\infty}=\textup{SL}_{2}(q^{2/s})
. If
s=2
, then the normalizer of
\textup{SL}_{2}(q)
in
\Delta^{\#}(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
is
\textup{GL}_{2}(q){\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}^{*}
which has two orbits on the nonzero vectors of
V^{\prime}
. If
s=3
, then
(q,q_{0})=(q_{1}^{3},q_{1}^{2})
for some integer
q_{1}
, and
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\cap{\mathbb{F}}_{q_{0}}={\mathbb{F}}_{q_{1}}
. We write
K_{1}
,
K
for the normalizers of
\textup{SL}_{2}(q_{1}^{2})
in
\Delta^{\#}(V^{\prime})
and
\Gamma^{\#}(V^{\prime})
respectively, so that
K=K_{1}:\langle\phi\rangle
. We have
K_{1}=\textup{GL}_{2}(q_{1}^{2}){\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}^{*}
or
K_{1}=\left(\frac{1}{2}\textup{GL}_{2}(q_{1}^{2})\right){\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}^{*}
according as
p
is even or odd. Here,
\frac{1}{2}\textup{GL}_{2}(q_{1}^{2})
is the unique subgroup of index
2
in
\textup{GL}_{2}(q_{1}^{2})
for odd
p
. For
q
even,
K_{1}
has two orbits on nonzero vectors by the arguments in
[
47
, Case (IIc)]
. For
q
odd, the group
K_{1}
has one orbit
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
of size
q_{1}^{2}+1
and two orbits
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
,
{\mathcal{N}}_{3}
of sizes
\frac{1}{2}(q_{1}^{6}-q_{1}^{2})
on the points of
\textup{PG}(V^{\prime})
by the same arguments as in
[
47
, Case (IIc)]
. Write
q_{1}=p^{2^{a}k}
with
k
odd, and take an element
\eta\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p^{3k}}\setminus{\mathbb{F}}_{q_{1}^{2}}
. Then
\langle e_{1}+\eta e_{2}\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}}
is stabilized by the Sylow
2
-subgroup of
\langle\phi\rangle
. We deduce that the
K
-orbit of
\langle e_{1}+\eta e_{2}\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}}
equals one of
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
,
{\mathcal{N}}_{3}
but not their union. We conclude that
H
has two orbits on
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
only if
q
is odd for
s=3
.

We suppose that
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}=H(2,q^{2})
and
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(5,q)
in the sequel. If
H
is of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
, then it preserves a decomposition
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}:\,V^{\prime}=\langle e_{1}\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}}\oplus\langle e_{2}\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}}\oplus\langle e_{3}\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}}
with each
e_{i}
nonsingular. Let
N_{\Gamma^{\prime}}({\mathcal{D}}^{\prime})
be the normalizer of
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
in
\Gamma(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
. It has no subgroup that has two orbits on the nonsingular points of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
for
q=2
, so assume that
q>2
. Let
{\mathcal{N}}_{i}^{\prime}
be the set of singular points
x_{1}e_{1}+x_{2}e_{2}+x_{3}e_{3}
with
i
nonzero coordinates for
i=2
,
3
, which are
N_{\Gamma^{\prime}}({\mathcal{D}}^{\prime})
-invariant. Then
|{\mathcal{N}}_{2}^{\prime}|=3(q+1)
,
|{\mathcal{N}}_{3}^{\prime}|=(q+1)^{2}(q-2)
, and they are the two
H
-orbits and thus also the two
N_{\Gamma^{\prime}}({\mathcal{D}}^{\prime})
-orbits. Since
|{\mathcal{N}}_{2}|
and
|{\mathcal{N}}_{3}|
both divide
|N_{\Gamma^{\prime}}({\mathcal{D}}^{\prime})|=6\cdot 2f\cdot(q-1)(q+1)^{3}
, we deduce that
\textup{lcm}(3,~(q+1)(q-2))
divides
12f(q+1)
. This holds only for
p^{f}\in\{3,5,2^{2},2^{3},2^{5}\}
, and the group
N_{\Gamma^{\prime}}({\mathcal{D}}^{\prime})
is transitive on both
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{3}^{\prime}
upon direct check. The sets
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}^{\prime}
,
{\mathcal{N}}_{3}^{\prime}
yield tight sets for each such
q
.

If
H
is of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{5}
, there are two cases: (i)
q=q_{0}^{2}
and
H
stabilizes a subplane
\pi^{\prime}
of order
q_{0}
that intersects
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
in a conic
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
, (ii)
q=q_{0}^{r}
for some odd prime
r
and
H
stabilizes a subplane
\pi^{\prime}
of order
q_{0}^{2}
that intersects
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
in a unital
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}=H(2,q_{0}^{2})
. For (i), the set of singular points outside
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
that lie on a secant line to
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
is
H
-invariant and has size
\binom{q+1}{2}(q-1)<|{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}|-|{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}|
, so there are at least two orbits outside
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
. For (ii) there are
n_{1}=q_{0}^{4}+q_{0}^{2}+1-(q_{0}^{3}+1)=q_{0}^{2}(q_{0}^{2}-q_{0}+1)
secant lines to
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
in
\pi^{\prime}
, and the set of singular points outside
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
that those lines cover is
H
-invariant and has size
n_{1}(q-q_{0})<|{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}|-|{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}|
. Therefore,
H
is not of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{5}
.

If
H
is of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{6}
, then we deduce from
d=3
that it normalizes an irreducible subgroup
R=3_{+}^{1+2}
. By
[
7
, Table 4.6B]
we have
q=p
and
p\equiv 2\pmod{3}
. The order of
H\tilde{Z}
divides
3^{2}\cdot|\textup{Sp}_{2}(3)|\cdot(q^{2}-1)
, so
216\geq\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}|=\frac{1}{2}(p^{3}+1)
. It holds only for
p\in\{2,5\}
. For
p=2
the group
R
is transitive on the nonzero singular vectors. For
p=5
, there is a unique conjugacy class of
R
in
\Gamma=\Gamma(V,\kappa)
and its normalizer
N_{\Gamma}(R)
has two orbits on
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(5,5)
that are respectively
54,~72
-tight sets.

If
H
is of class
{\mathcal{S}}
, then by
[
7
, Table 8.6]
we have one of the following cases: (i)
H^{\infty}\leq\textup{PSL}_{2}(7)
,
q=p\equiv 3,5,6\pmod{7}
, (ii)
H^{\infty}\leq 3.A_{6}
,
q=p\equiv 11,14\pmod{15}
or
q=5
, (iii)
H^{\infty}\leq 3.A_{7}
,
q=5
. For (i) we have
H^{\infty}=\textup{PSL}_{2}(7)
, and we deduce from
[H\tilde{Z}:\,\tilde{Z}]\geq\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}|
that
q\in\{3,5\}
. The group
\textup{PSL}_{2}(7)
is transitive on
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
for
q=3
and has two orbits of sizes
42,~84
on
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
for
q=5
. In the latter case we obtain
42,~84
-tight sets of
Q^{-}(5,5)
. For (ii) we deduce from
[H\tilde{Z}:\,\tilde{Z}]\geq\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}|
that
q\in\{5,11\}
. There is no integer
i
such that
\textup{lcm}(i,q^{3}+1-i)
divides
|\textup{Aut}(A_{6})|
for
q=11
. The group
3.A_{6}
has two orbits of sizes
36,~90
on
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
for
q=5
, and they yield
36,~90
-tight sets of
Q^{-}(5,5)
respectively. The only quasisimple subgroup of
3.A_{6}
whose normalizer is transitive on both orbits is
3.A_{6}
, so
H^{\infty}=3.A_{6}
. For (iii),
3.A_{7}
has two orbits on nonzero singular points that yield
3
-ovoids of
Q^{-}(5,5)
respectively. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 5.5
.

Suppose that
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}={\mathcal{P}}
and
b>1
, and write
r^{\prime}
for the rank of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
. Let
O_{1}^{\prime}
,
O_{2}^{\prime}
be as in (
5.1
), and assume that they are both proper subsets of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
. If
r^{\prime}\geq 2
, then
O_{1}^{\prime}
,
O_{2}^{\prime}
form the two
H
-orbits on
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
and are both tight sets with the same parameters as
O_{1}
,
O_{2}
.

Proof.

Since
H
is transitive on both
O_{1}
and
O_{2}
, we deduce that
O_{1}^{\prime}
,
O_{2}^{\prime}
are the two
H
-orbits on
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
. Moreover, we have
\{v\in V\setminus\{0\}\mid\langle v\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}\in O_{i}\}=\{v\in V^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}\mid\langle v\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}}\in O_{i}^{\prime}\}
for
i=1,2
. In particular, we have
O_{i}=\{\langle\lambda v\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}\mid\lambda\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}^{*},\langle v\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}}\in O_{i}^{\prime}\}
for
i=1,~2
. For row 1 of Table
2.2
where both
\kappa
and
\kappa^{\prime}
are symplectic,
O_{i}
and
O_{i}^{\prime}
are intriguing sets of the same type with the same parameters for
i=1,~2
by
[
41
, Table 1]
. For row 9 or 10 of Table
2.2
with
b=2
,
\kappa
is orthogonal and
\kappa^{\prime}
is unitary. We have
\kappa(v)=\kappa^{\prime}(v,v)
, and the associated bilinear form of
\kappa
is
\textup{Tr}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}/{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}\circ\kappa^{\prime}
up to a scalar. It is routine to deduce that

\#\{\langle\lambda w\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}\mid\lambda\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}^{*},\langle w\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}}\in O_{j}^{\prime},\textup{Tr}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}/{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(\kappa^{\prime}(\lambda w,v))=0\}=q\cdot\#\{\langle w\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}}\in O_{j}^{\prime}\mid\kappa^{\prime}(w,v)=0\}+|O_{j}^{\prime}|

for
i,~j\in\{1,2\}
and
\langle v\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}\in O_{i}
. It then readily follows that
O_{1}^{\prime},~O_{2}^{\prime}
are intriguing sets of the same type and have the same parameters as
O_{1},~O_{2}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

Suppose that
b>1
, and define
O_{1}^{\prime},~O_{2}^{\prime}
as in (
5.1
). Let
r,~r^{\prime}
be the rank of
{\mathcal{P}}
and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
respectively. By Lemma
5.1
, Propositions
5.2
-
5.4
, it remains to consider the cases where
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}={\mathcal{P}}
,
r^{\prime}\geq 2
and
O_{1}^{\prime},~O_{2}^{\prime}
are proper subsets of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
. Since
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}={\mathcal{P}}
, we are in rows 1, 9 (with
b=2
) and 10 (with
b=2
) of Table
2.2
.
By Proposition
5.5
, each
O_{i}^{\prime}
is a tight set of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
with the same parameter as
O_{i}
for
i=1,~2
.

We assume that
b=1
for now, and will return to the three remaining
{\mathcal{C}}_{3}
cases mentioned in Remark
5.6
after we handle the Aschbacher classes
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
,
{\mathcal{C}}_{4},\ldots,{\mathcal{C}}_{8}
. If
H
is of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{8}
, then
{\mathcal{P}}=W(d-1,q)
with
q
even and
H
stabilizes a nondegenerate quadric
{\mathcal{Q}}
. The quadric
{\mathcal{Q}}
is a tight set or an
m
-ovoid of
W(d-1,q)
according as it is hyperbolic or elliptic. This yields (T4) of Theorem
1.3
, and we refer the reader to Section
2.2
for the groups of semisimilarities of
Q^{+}(d-1,q)
that are transitive on both
{\mathcal{Q}}
and its complement.

Lemma 5.7
.

Suppose that
q
is a prime power.

(1)

If
q\geq 7
and
3\leq s\leq 5
, then there is a vector
u\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s}
with all coordinates nonzero such that
\sum_{i=1}^{s}u_{i}^{2}=0
.

(2)

If either
q\geq 3
and
s\in\{2,3,4\}
, or
q=2
and
s\in\{2,4,6\}
, then there is a vector
u\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}^{s}
with all coordinates nonzero such that
\sum_{i=1}^{s}u_{i}^{q+1}=0
.

Proof.

The proofs of (1) and (2) are similar, so we only give details for (1) here. The quadratic form
Q(x)=x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}
on
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{3}
is nondegenerate, and the corresponding quadric has
q+1
singular points. Its intersection with each of the three planes defined by
x_{i}=0
,
1\leq i\leq 3
, has at most two points respectively. We deduce that there are at least
q+1-6>0
singular points with all three coordinates nonzero. By a similar argument, we deduce that there are at least
q^{2}+1-4(q+1)>0
and
(q^{2}+1)(q+1)-5(q+1)^{2}>0
singular points with all coordinates nonzero for
s=4,~5
respectively. The claim then follows.
∎

Proposition 5.8
.

Suppose that
b=1
and
H
stabilizes a subspace decomposition
{\mathcal{D}}:\,V=V_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus V_{t}
, where
t\geq 2
and
\dim(V_{i})=m
for each
i
. Then we have one of the cases in Table
1.1
and (T1) of Theorem
1.3
.

Proof.

By
[
43
, Table 4.2.A]
we have one of the following cases:

(2A)

V_{i}
’s are isometric, nondegenerate and pairwise perpendicular,

(2B)

t=2
,
qm
is odd,
\kappa
is orthogonal, and
V_{1},V_{2}
are nondegenerate and non-isometric,

(2C)

t=2
, and
V_{1},~V_{2}
are totally singular or isotropic.

We write
N_{\Gamma}({\mathcal{D}})
for the stabilizer of the decomposition
{\mathcal{D}}
in
\Gamma(V)
. For a vector
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{t})
with
x_{i}\in V_{i}
, we define its
{\mathcal{D}}
-length as
\omega_{\mathcal{D}}(x):=\#\{1\leq i\leq t\mid x_{i}\neq 0\}
. The
{\mathcal{D}}
-length is preserved by the action of
N_{\Gamma}({\mathcal{D}})
. We define
\Lambda:=\{\omega_{\mathcal{D}}(x)\mid\langle x\rangle\in{\mathcal{P}}\}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{i}=\{\langle x\rangle\in{\mathcal{P}}\mid\omega_{{\mathcal{D}}}(x)=i\}
for
i\in\Lambda
. For (2B) we have
\{\kappa(x)\mid x\in V_{i}\setminus\{0\}\}={\mathbb{F}}_{q}
for
i=1,~2
by the fact
m\geq 3
, and so the sets
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
are nonempty. The set
\{\langle x_{1}+x_{2}\rangle\mid x_{i}\in V_{i}\setminus\{0\},\kappa(x_{1})=\kappa(x_{2})=0\}
is a proper subset of
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
, so there are at least three
N_{\Gamma}({\mathcal{D}})
-orbits. We conclude that (2B) does not occur. For (2C)
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
are also nonempty. If
\kappa
is symplectic or unitary, then the set
\{\langle x_{1}+x_{2}\rangle\mid x_{i}\in V_{i}\setminus\{0\},\kappa(x_{1},x_{2})=0\}
is a proper
N_{\Gamma}({\mathcal{D}})
-invariant subset of
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
. Therefore,
\kappa
is orthogonal for (2C) and we obtain (T1-b) of Theorem
1.3
.

It remains to consider (2A). If
\kappa
is symplectic, then
\Lambda=\{1,\ldots,t\}
and we deduce that
t=2
. This leads to (T1-a) of Theorem
1.3
. Suppose that
\kappa
is unitary or orthogonal. We use the same arguments for excluding (2B) to deduce that each
V_{i}
has no nonzero singular vector. If
\kappa
is unitary, then
m=1
and so
t=d\geq 4
. By Lemma
5.7
we have
\{2,3,4\}\subseteq\Lambda
for
q\geq 3^{2}
and
\{2,4,6\}\subseteq\Lambda
for
q=2^{2}
with
t\geq 6
. Therefore, we have
q=2^{2}
and
t\in\{4,~5\}
if
\kappa
is unitary. The two sets
{\mathcal{N}}_{2},~{\mathcal{N}}_{4}
are respectively
2,~3
-ovoids of
H(3,4)
for
t=4
and
6,~27
-tight sets of
H(4,4)
for
t=5
, and
N_{\Gamma}({\mathcal{D}})
is transitive on them in both cases. If
\kappa
is orthogonal, then either
m=1
or each
V_{i}
is elliptic of dimension
2
. For the latter case
H
lies in
\Gamma\textup{L}_{t}(q^{2})
which contradicts the assumption
b=1
, so we have
m=1
. We then have
d=t\geq 5
, and so
|\Lambda|\geq 3
for
q\geq 7
by Lemma
5.7
. There are singular vectors of
{\mathcal{D}}
-lengths
2
,
4
,
5
respectively for
q=5
. For
q=3
the set
\Lambda
consists of multiples of
3
and it has size at most
2
if and only if
5\leq t\leq 8
. The group
N_{\Gamma}({\mathcal{D}})
is transitive on the singular vectors of each given
{\mathcal{D}}
-length in those cases. For
t=5
, the nonzero singular vectors all have
{\mathcal{D}}
-length
3
, and we exhaust the subgroups of
\textup{GO}_{1}(3)\wr S_{5}
whose orders are multiples of
4
to obtain
4,~6
-tight sets of
Q(4,3)
when there are exactly two orbits. For
t=6
,
{\mathcal{N}}_{3}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{6}
are
8,~20
-tight sets of
Q^{-}(5,3)
respectively. For
t\in\{7,8\}
,
{\mathcal{N}}_{3}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{6}
are not tight sets. We summarize those examples in Table
1.1
. This conclude the analysis of (2A). This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 5.9
.

Take notation as in Proposition
5.8
, and assume that
t=2
and
m\geq 3
. Let
H_{V_{1}}^{V_{1}}
be the restriction of the stabilizer
H_{V_{1}}
of
V_{1}
in
H
to
V_{1}
. If
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(2m-1,q)
with
V_{1},~V_{2}
totally singular, then either
\textup{SL}_{m}(q)\unlhd H_{V_{1}}^{V_{1}}
, or
(q,m)=(2,4)
and
A_{7}\unlhd H_{V_{1}}^{V_{1}}
, or
(q,m)\in\{(2,3),(8,3)\}
and
H_{V_{1}}^{V_{1}}\unlhd\Gamma\textup{L}_{1}(q^{3})
.

Proof.

Let
B
be the associated bilinear form of
\kappa
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
be the set of singular points in
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
. Let
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
be its complement in
{\mathcal{P}}
, so that

{\mathcal{N}}_{2}=\{\langle x_{1}+x_{2}\rangle\mid x_{i}\in V_{i}\setminus\{0\},B(x_{1},x_{2})=0\}
. Let
N_{\Gamma}(V_{1},V_{2})
be the stabilizer of both
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
in
\Gamma(V)
, which is a subgroup of
N_{\Gamma}({\mathcal{D}})
. We define

(5.2)

\iota:\,N_{\Gamma}(V_{1},V_{2})\rightarrow\Gamma\textup{L}(V_{1}),\quad g\mapsto g|_{V_{1}},

which is the restriction to
V_{1}
. We have
\ker(\iota)\cong{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
, and its elements act as identity on
V_{1}
and as scalars on
V_{2}
. Let
H_{1}
be the stabilizer of
V_{1}
in
H
. Let
Z_{1}
be the center of
\textup{GL}(V_{1})
, and define
G_{1}=\iota(H_{1})Z_{1}
. The group
G_{1}
is transitive on nonzero vectors of
V_{1}
, and such subgroups have been determined in
[
31
]
. We follow the statement in
[
2
, Theorem 2.18]
. Since
m\geq 3
, we have one of the following cases: (H1)
G_{1}\unlhd\Gamma\textup{L}_{1}(q^{m})
, (H2)
\textup{SL}_{n}(q_{1})\unlhd G_{1}
with
q_{1}^{n}=q^{m}
and
n\geq 2
, (H3)
G_{2}(q_{1})^{\prime}\unlhd G_{1}
with
q_{1}^{6}=q^{m}
and
q
even, (H4)
p=3
and
\textup{SL}_{2}(5)\unlhd G_{1}\unlhd\Gamma\textup{L}_{2}(9)
, (H5)
G_{1}=A_{6}
or
A_{7}
with
q^{m}=2^{4}
, (H6)
G_{1}=\textup{SL}_{2}(13)
and
q^{m}=3^{6}
, (H8)
D_{8}\circ Q_{8}\unlhd G_{1}
with
q^{m}=3^{4}
.

We have
|{\mathcal{N}}_{2}|=(q^{m-1}-1)\frac{q^{m}-1}{q-1}
. Since
[H:\,H_{1}]=2
,
H_{1}
either is transitive on
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
or has two orbits of the same size on
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
. The latter case occurs only for odd
q
. In particular,
|{\mathcal{N}}_{2}|
divides
\gcd(2,q-1)\cdot|H_{1}|
. The group
H_{1}
lies in the preimage of
G_{1}
in
N_{\Gamma}(V_{1},V_{2})
under
\iota
, so
|H_{1}|
divides
(q-1)\cdot|G_{1}|
. We deduce that
|{\mathcal{N}}_{2}|
divides
\gcd(2,q-1)\cdot|G_{1}|
. This excludes (H4), (H6) and (H8) upon direct check. For (H2),
G_{1}^{\infty}=\textup{SL}_{n}(q_{1})
lies in
\textup{GL}(V_{1})^{\infty}=\textup{SL}_{m}(q)
, so is
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear. Together with the fact
b=1
, we deduce that
q_{1}=q
, i.e.,
G_{1}^{\infty}=\textup{SL}_{m}(q)
. For (H3), we have
q=q_{1}
as in the case (H2). We thus have
m=6
, and so
p^{5f}-1
has a primitive prime divisor
p^{\prime}
. We similarly have
p^{\prime}\geq 1+5f
and
\gcd(p^{\prime},6f)=1
. The group
H_{1}
has order dividing
f\cdot|G_{2}(q)|=fq^{6}(q^{2}-1)(q^{6}-1)
, while the latter is not divisible by
p^{\prime}
since
5f
does not divide
2f
or
6f
. This contradiction excludes (H3). For (H5),
(q,m)=(2,4)
, and
H
has order dividing
n:=2\cdot(q-1)^{2}\cdot|\textup{Aut}(A_{s})|
with
s\in\{6,7\}
. We have
|{\mathcal{N}}_{2}|=3\cdot 5\cdot 7
, so
s=7
by the fact that
|{\mathcal{N}}_{2}|
divides
n
. The group
\iota^{-1}(A_{7})
is transitive on
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
by direct check. For (H1),
|{\mathcal{N}}_{2}|
divides
2\cdot|\Gamma\textup{L}_{1}(q^{m})|
only if
m=3
and
q\in\{2,3,5,8,11\}
. In this case, we take the model
V={\mathbb{F}}_{q^{3}}\oplus{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{3}}
,
\kappa((x_{1},x_{2}))=\textup{Tr}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{3}}/{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(x_{1}x_{2})
, and define
\tau:\,(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto(x_{2},x_{1})
. Then
H_{1}
is a subgroup of
K_{1}:=\iota^{-1}\left(\Gamma\textup{L}_{1}(q^{3})\right)
which consists of
(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto(ax_{1}^{p^{i}},a^{-1}bx_{2}^{p^{i}})
, where
a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{3}}^{*}
,
b\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
and
0\leq i\leq 3f-1
. The group
H
is a subgroup of
K:=K_{1}\rtimes\langle\tau\rangle
. Take an element
\alpha\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{3}}^{*}
such that
\textup{Tr}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{3}}/{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(\alpha)=0
. The group
K
is transitive on
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
only if
q\in\{2,8\}
by direct check. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 5.10
.

If
H
preserves a tensor decomposition of
V
, then we have (T2) of Theorem
1.3
.

Proof.

We first handle the case where
H
is of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{7}
. Then
H
preserve a decomposition
{\mathcal{D}}:\,(V,\kappa)=(V_{1},f_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes(V_{t},f_{t})
,
where
V=V_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{t}
with
t\geq 2
, each
(V_{i},f_{i})
is a classical geometry similar to
(V_{1},f_{1})
and
\Omega(V_{1},f_{1})^{\prime}
is quasisimple. Let
N_{\Gamma}(D)
be the stabilizer of
{\mathcal{D}}
in
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
, and let
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
be the set of points
\langle v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{t}\rangle
such that
v_{i}\in V_{i}
for each
i
and
f_{j}(v_{j},v_{j})=0
for at least one
j
. Then
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
is a proper
N_{\Gamma}({\mathcal{D}})
-invariant subset of
{\mathcal{P}}
. For an nonzero vector
x=v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{t}
, we define
s(x):=\#\{1\leq j\leq t\mid f_{j}(v_{j},v_{j})=0\}
. Then the function
s(x)
is preserved by
N_{\Gamma}({\mathcal{D}})
. If
q
is odd and both
f_{1}
and
\kappa
are symmetric, then
(V_{1},f_{1})
has a nonzero singular vector since
\Omega(V_{1},f_{1})^{\prime}
is quasisimple. Then
\{s(x)\mid\langle x\rangle\in{\mathcal{N}}_{1}\}
has size at least
2
, so
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
comprises of at least two
N_{\Gamma}({\mathcal{D}})
-orbits already: a contradiction. The same arguments exclude the case where both
f_{1}
and
\kappa
are unitary. It remains to consider the cases where
f_{1}
is symplectic. We have
|{\mathcal{N}}_{1}|=\left(\frac{q^{m}-1}{q-1}\right)^{t}
, and
m
is even. The form
\kappa
is orthogonal with plus sign if
qt
is even, and it is symplectic otherwise. In either case, the rank of
{\mathcal{P}}
is
r=\frac{1}{2}m^{t}
. If
qt
is odd, then
t\geq 3
and so
r\geq 4
. If
qt
is even, then we have
d=m^{t}\geq 8
and so
r\geq 4
by the fact
t\geq 2
and our hypothesis that
{\mathcal{P}}\neq Q^{+}(3,q)
. If
q^{r}=2^{6}
, then we deduce that
(q,r)=(2,6)
which leads to the contradiction
2r=12=m^{t}
. By
[
68
]
,
q^{r}-1
has a primitive prime divisor
p^{\prime}
. We have
\textup{ord}_{p^{\prime}}(p)=fr
, where
q=p^{f}
with
p
prime. Since
p^{\prime}
divides
|{\mathcal{N}}_{1}|
, we deduce that
r\leq m
, i.e.,
\frac{1}{2}m^{t}\leq m
. It follows that
m=t=2
, which contradicts the bound
d=m^{t}\geq 8
for
qt
even. We conclude that
H
is not of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{7}
.

We then suppose that
H
is of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{4}
. It preserves a decomposition
{\mathcal{D}}:\,(V,\kappa)=(V_{1},f_{1})\otimes(V_{2},f_{2})
, where
V=V_{1}\otimes V_{2}
, and
(V_{1},f_{1})
is not similar to
(V_{2},f_{2})
. We write
m_{i}=\dim(V_{i})
for
i=1,2
. By
[
43
, Table 4.4.A]
, we assume that
m_{i}\geq 3
if
f_{i}
is symmetric. If both
f_{1},f_{2}
are symplectic or unitary, then we assume without loss of generality that
m_{1}<m_{2}
. Let
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
be the set of points
\langle v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\rangle
such that
v_{i}\in V_{i}
for
i=1,2
and
f_{j}(v_{j},v_{j})=0
for at least one
j
, and let
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
be its complement in
{\mathcal{P}}
. Then
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
is a proper
N_{\Gamma}({\mathcal{D}})
-invariant subset of
{\mathcal{P}}
in each case. If both of
(V_{1},f_{1})
,
(V_{2},f_{2})
contain singular points and at least one contains a nonsingular point, then
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
comprises of at least two
N_{\Gamma}({\mathcal{D}})
-orbits as in the proof of the
{\mathcal{C}}_{7}
case: a contradiction. This excludes all but the case where
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
are both symplectic. In this case,
\kappa
is orthogonal of plus sign. Choose a basis
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{m_{1}}\}
of
V_{1}
and a basis
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{m_{2}/2}\}
of a maximal totally isotropic subspace of
V_{2}
. Then
\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}\otimes e_{i}
,
1\leq k\leq\min\{m_{1},m_{2}/2\}
, are all singular vectors. They have different ranks, and so are in distinct
N_{\Gamma}({\mathcal{D}})
-orbits. It follows that
\min\{m_{1},m_{2}/2\}\leq 2
. Since
m_{1},m_{2}
are even and
m_{1}<m_{2}
, we deduce that
m_{1}=2
as desired. Take a basis
v_{1}
,
v_{2}
of
V_{1}
such that
f_{1}(v_{1},v_{2})=1
. If
\langle x\rangle\in{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
, then
x=v_{1}\otimes u_{1}+v_{2}\otimes u_{2}
for linearly independent vectors
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
in
V_{2}
. Since
\kappa
vanishes on
v_{1}\otimes u_{1}
and
v_{2}\otimes u_{2}
, we deduce that
\kappa(x)=0
if and only if
f_{2}(u_{1},u_{2})=0
, i.e.,
\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle
is a totally isotropic line of
V_{2}
. Therefore,
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
consists of the points
\langle v_{1}\otimes u_{1}+v_{2}\otimes u_{2}\rangle
with
\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle
a totally isotropic line of
V_{2}
. The group
\Delta(V_{1},f_{1})\circ\Delta(V_{2},f_{2})
is transitive on both
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

Suppose that
\kappa
is orthogonal and
H
preserves a tensor decomposition
(V,\kappa)=(V_{1},f_{1})\otimes(V_{2},f_{2})
, where
\dim(V_{1})=2
,
\dim(V_{2})=2m\geq 4
, and
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
are both symplectic. We write
N_{\Gamma}({\mathcal{D}})
,
N_{\Delta}({\mathcal{D}})
for the stabilizers of
{\mathcal{D}}
in
\Gamma(V)
,
\Delta(V)
respectively. We have
N_{\Delta}({\mathcal{D}})=\Delta(V_{1},f_{1})\circ\Delta(V_{2},f_{2})
. For
i=1,2
, we define

\pi_{i}:\,N_{\Delta}({\mathcal{D}})\rightarrow\textup{P}\Delta(V_{i},f_{i})
such that
\pi_{i}(g_{1}\circ g_{2})\mapsto\overline{g_{i}}
, and extend it to a homomorphism
\pi_{i}:\,N_{\Gamma}({\mathcal{D}})\rightarrow\textup{P}\Gamma(V_{i},f_{i})
in the natural way. Let
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
be the set of points
\langle v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\rangle
such that
v_{i}\in V_{i}\setminus\{0\}
for
i=1,2
, and let
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
be its complement in
{\mathcal{P}}
. Then
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
are the two
H
-orbits by the proof of Proposition
5.10
. Since
H
is transitive on
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
, we deduce that
\pi_{i}(H)
is transitive on the points of
\textup{PG}(V_{i})
for
i=1,2
. Since
H
is transitive on
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
, we deduce that
\pi_{2}(H)
is transitive on the totally isotropic lines of
(V_{2},f_{2})
. For the subgroups of
\textup{P}\Gamma\textup{L}_{2}(q)
that is transitive on the projective lines
\textup{PG}(1,q)
, we refer the reader to
[
28
, Theorem 3.1]
. Since
\pi_{2}(H)
is transitive on both the set of isotropic points and the set of totally isotropic lines of
(V_{2},f_{2})
, we have either
\textup{Sp}_{2m}(q)^{\prime}\unlhd\pi_{2}(H)
, or
(m,q)=(2,3)
by
[
28
, Theorem 5.2]
. For the case
(m,q)=(2,3)
, we check by Magma
[
6
]
that either
\textup{Sp}_{4}(3)\unlhd\pi_{2}(H)
or
2_{-}^{1+4}.A_{5}\unlhd\pi_{2}(H)
.

Proposition 5.12
.

If
H
is of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{5}
, then we have (T3) of Theorem
1.3
.

Proof.

There is a subfield
{\mathbb{F}}_{q_{0}}
of index
s>1
in
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
and an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q_{0}}
-span
V_{\#}
of an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-basis
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\}
of
V
such that
H\leq N_{\Gamma}(V_{\#}){\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
, where
N_{\Gamma}(V_{\#})
is the stabilizer of
V_{\#}
in
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
. We have
q=q_{0}^{s}
. Let
{\mathcal{P}}_{\#}
be the associated polar space of
(V_{\#},\kappa_{\#})
, where
\kappa_{\#}
is a proper scalar multiple of the restriction of
\kappa
to
V_{\#}
. Let
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
be the set of singular or isotropic points of
{\mathcal{P}}_{\#}
, and let
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
be its complement in
{\mathcal{P}}
. Both
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
are proper
N_{\Gamma}(V_{\#})
-invariant subsets of
{\mathcal{P}}
. Let
\theta
be the semilinear transformation of
V
that maps
\sum_{i}x_{i}e_{i}
to
\sum_{i}x_{i}^{q_{0}}e_{i}
for
x_{i}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. For a singular point
P=\langle u\rangle
not in
{\mathcal{P}}_{\#}
, write
P^{\theta}=\langle u\theta\rangle
, and let
PP^{\theta}
be the line that passes through
P
and
P^{\theta}
in
\textup{PG}(V)
. We observe that
\langle ug\theta\rangle=\langle u\theta g\rangle
for
g\in N_{\Gamma}(V_{\#})
, so if
Pg=P^{\prime}
then
(Pg)^{\theta}=P^{\prime\theta}
.

First consider the case where both
\kappa
and
\kappa_{\#}
are symplectic. Take nonzero vectors
u
,
v
and
w
in
V_{\#}
such that
\kappa(u,w)=0
,
\kappa(v,w)\neq 0
. We define
P_{1}=\langle u+\eta w\rangle
,
P_{2}=\langle v+\eta w\rangle
for an element
\eta\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\setminus{\mathbb{F}}_{\#}
. Then
P_{i}\neq P_{i}^{\theta}
for
i=1,2
, and they are not in
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
. The line
\langle u,w\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}
intersects
V_{\#}
in a totally singular subline
\langle u,w\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{\#}}
, and it contains
P_{1}
,
P_{1}^{\theta}
. The line
\langle v,w\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}
intersects
V_{\#}
in a subline
\langle v,w\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{\#}}
that is not totally singular, and it contains
P_{2}
,
P_{2}^{\theta}
. The points
P_{1},P_{2}
are not in the same
N_{\Gamma}(V_{\#})
-orbit, since otherwise
\langle u,w\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{\#}}
and
\langle v,w\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{\#}}
would be in the same
N_{\Gamma}(V_{\#})
-orbit: a contradiction. Therefore, this case is impossible.

We next consider the case where both
\kappa
and
\kappa_{\#}
are both orthogonal. Let
\ell
be a totally singular line of
V
that intersects
V_{\#}
in a subline, and take a point
P_{1}
outside
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
on
\ell
. Then
P_{1}\neq P_{1}^{\theta}
,
\ell
is stabilized by
\theta
and
\ell=P_{1}P_{1}^{\theta}
. Take a subline
\langle u,v\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{\#}}
of
V_{\#}
that contains no singular point, and set
\ell^{\prime}=\langle u,v\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}
. The quadratic polynomial
\kappa(u+xv)=0
in
x
has no solution in
{\mathbb{F}}_{\#}
, so is irreducible over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q_{0}}
. If it has a solution
x_{0}
in
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, then
P_{2}=\langle u+x_{0}v\rangle
is a singular point such that
P_{2}P_{2}^{\theta}=\ell^{\prime}
. We then deduce that
P_{1},P_{2}
are not in the same
N_{\Gamma}(V_{\#})
-orbits as in the symplectic case. Therefore,
\kappa(u+xv)=0
has no solution in
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, and so
s
is odd. It follows that
{\mathcal{P}}
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{\#}
has the same rank
r
and
sr\geq 6
. Then
\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
divides
|{\mathcal{N}}_{1}|=(q_{0}^{d-1-r}+1)\frac{q_{0}^{r}-1}{q_{0}-1}
. If
q^{r}=q_{0}^{sr}=2^{6}
, then
(q_{0},s,r)=(2,3,2)
and
{\mathcal{P}}
is one of
Q(4,2)
,
Q^{-}(5,2)
. If
{\mathcal{P}}_{\#}=Q(4,2)
, then
|{\mathcal{N}}_{1}|=15
is not divisible by
\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}=9
; if
{\mathcal{P}}_{\#}=Q^{-}(5,2)
, then
|{\mathcal{N}}_{2}|=2\cdot 3^{4}\cdot 7
does not divide
|N_{\Gamma}(V_{\#})|=2^{7}\cdot 3^{5}\cdot 5
. Therefore, we have
q^{r}\neq 2^{6}
.
Let
p^{\prime}
be a primitive prime divisor of
q^{r}-1
. Since
\textup{ord}_{p^{\prime}}(q_{0})=sr
, we deduce from
p^{\prime}
divides
|{\mathcal{N}}_{1}|
that
sr
divides
2(d-1-r)
. Since
d\leq 2r+2
, we have
2(d-1-r)\leq 2r+2\leq 3r
. Since
s
is odd and
sr\leq 3r
, we deduce that
s=3
,
r=2
and
d=2r+2=6
. In this case,
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(5,q_{0}^{3})
,
|{\mathcal{N}}_{2}|=q_{0}(q_{0}^{3}+1)(q_{0}^{8}-1)
, and a primitive prime divisor of
q_{0}^{8}-1
does not divide the order of
N_{\Gamma}(V_{\#})
upon direct check: a contradiction. To sum up, this case is not possible.

We then consider the cases where
\kappa
is unitary and
\kappa_{\#}
is orthogonal or unitary. We observe that each line of
\textup{PG}(V)
contains either
1
,
q^{1/2}+1
or
1+q
singular points of
\kappa
, and
q^{1/2}+1>\max\{2,1+q_{0}^{1/2}\}
. Take a totally singular lines
\ell
that intersects
V_{\#}
in a subline, and take a point
P_{1}
on it outside
{\mathcal{P}}_{\#}
. Take a line
\ell^{\prime}
that intersects
V_{\#}
in
2
or
q_{0}^{1/2}+1
singular points respectively according as
\kappa_{\#}
is orthogonal or unitary, and take a singular point
P_{2}
on it outside
{\mathcal{P}}_{\#}
. Then
P_{i}\neq P_{i}^{\theta}
for
i=1,2
, and the lines
\ell
,
\ell^{\prime}
are stabilized by
\theta
. It follows that
\ell=P_{1}P_{1}^{\theta}
,
\ell^{\prime}=P_{2}P_{2}^{\theta}
. We deduce that
P_{1},P_{2}
are in distinct
N_{\Gamma}({\mathcal{D}})
-orbits as in the previous cases. We conclude that those two cases are not possible.

Finally, we consider the case where
\kappa
is unitary and
\kappa_{\#}
is symplectic. In this case, we have
q=q_{0}^{2}
. By
[
15
, p. 991]
,
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
are the two
\textup{Sp}_{d}(q_{0})
-orbits. Assume that
H
does not contain
\textup{Sp}_{d}(q_{0})^{\prime}
from now on. For each
P\in{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
the line
PP^{\theta}
intersects
V_{\#}
in a totally singular subline. We deduce that
H
is transitive on both the set of points and the set of totally isotropic lines of
{\mathcal{P}}_{\#}
. By
[
28
, Theorem 5.2]
we deduce that the totally isotropic lines are maximal, so
d=4
. Moreover, either
q
is even and
\Omega_{3}(q_{0})\unlhd H
, or
q_{0}=3
by the same theorem. In the former case
H
is not transitive on
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
: a contradiction. For
q_{0}=3
, we examine all the subgroups of
N_{\Gamma}(V_{\#})
whose orders are multiples of
\textup{lcm}(|{\mathcal{N}}_{1}|,|{\mathcal{N}}_{2}|)=240
and there is no such subgroup that is transitive on both
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
besides those that contains
\textup{Sp}_{4}(3)
. This completes the proof.
∎

To consider the subgroups of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{6}
, we need the following technical lemma.

Lemma 5.13
.

Suppose that
n>0
and
p
is an odd prime. If
p^{\prime}
is an odd prime divisor of
p^{2^{n}}+1
, then
p^{\prime}
is a primitive prime divisor of
p^{2^{n+1}}-1
.

Proof.

Suppose to the contrary that
p^{\prime}
is an odd prime divisor of
p^{2^{n}}+1
that is not a primitive prime divisor of
p^{2^{n+1}}-1
. Then
m:=\textup{ord}_{p^{\prime}}(p)
is a proper divisor of
2^{n+1}
, i.e.,
m=2^{s}
with
s\leq n
. We have
0\equiv p^{2^{n}}+1\equiv 1^{2^{n-s}}+1\equiv 2\pmod{p^{\prime}}
which is a contradiction. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 5.14
.

Let
r_{0}
be a prime distinct from
p
. If
H
normalizes an absolutely irreducible
r_{0}
-group
R
of symplectic type and
H
is not contained in a subgroup of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{5}
, then we have one of the cases in Table
1.4
.

Proof.

We write
N_{\Gamma}(R)
and
N_{\Delta}(R)
for the normalizer of
R
in
\Gamma(V)
and
\Delta(V)
respectively. Let
e
be the smallest integer for which
p^{e}\equiv 1\pmod{|Z(R)|}
. Then
q=p^{e}
and
e
satisfies the restrictions in
[
43
, Table 4.6.B]
, since
N_{\Gamma}(R)
does not lie in a subgroup of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{5}
. The case where
R\cong 2_{-}^{1+2m}
and
\kappa
is symplectic has been handled in
[
47
, Case (IIe)]
, and we have one of the symplectic cases in Table
1.4
by (B) of the main theorem therein.

Suppose that
R\cong r_{0}^{1+2m}
(
r_{0}
odd) and
\kappa
is unitary. In this case,
d=r_{0}^{m}
,
e
is even,
|Z(R)|=r_{0}
,
N_{\Delta}(R)=N_{\textup{GL}(V)}(R)={\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}\circ(r_{0}^{1+2m}.\textup{Sp}_{2m}(r_{0}))
. The rank of
{\mathcal{P}}=H(d-1,q)
is
r=\frac{r_{0}^{m}-1}{2}
. If
p^{er}=2^{6}
, then
p=e=2
,
r=3
. It follows that
r_{0}^{m}=7
, i.e.,
r_{0}=7
and
m=1
. However,
p^{e}\equiv 1\pmod{|Z(R)|}
does not hold: a contradiction. Let
p^{\prime}
be a primitive prime divisor
p^{\prime}
of
p^{er}-1
, so that
\textup{ord}_{p^{\prime}}(p)=er
. We have
\gcd(p^{\prime},er)=1
, and
p^{\prime}\geq 1+\frac{e}{2}(r_{0}^{m}-1)\geq r_{0}^{m}
. Since
\gcd(p^{\prime},r)=1
and
r_{0}
is odd, we deduce that
p^{\prime}\geq r_{0}^{m}+2
. Since
\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
divides
|H|
, we deduce that
p^{\prime}
divides
|N_{\Gamma}(R)|
. It follows that
p^{\prime}
divides
\prod_{i=1}^{m}(r_{0}^{2i}-1)
. The prime numbers of the latter number do not exceed
\frac{1}{2}(r_{0}^{m}+1)
: a contradiction.

Suppose that
R\cong 4\circ 2^{1+2m}
and
\kappa
is unitary. In this case,
d=2^{m}
,
r_{0}=e=2
,
|Z(R)|=4
,
N_{\Delta}(R)=N_{\textup{GL}(V)}(R)={\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}\circ(2^{1+2m}.\textup{Sp}_{2m}(2))
. Since
e=2
, we deduce that
p\equiv 3\pmod{4}
. The rank of
{\mathcal{P}}
is
r=2^{m-1}\geq 2
, so
m\geq 2
. Let
p^{\prime}
be a primitive prime divisor of
p^{er}-1
, so that
\textup{ord}_{p^{\prime}}(p)=2^{m}
. We have
\gcd(p^{\prime},er)=1
and
p^{\prime}\geq 1+2^{m}
. Let
s
be the largest integer such that
p^{\prime s}
divides
p^{er}-1
. We argue as in the previous case to deduce that
p^{\prime s}
divides
x:=\prod_{i=1}^{m}(2^{2i}-1)
. By examining the prime divisors of
x
and using the fact
p^{\prime}\geq 1+2^{m}
, we deduce that
p^{\prime}=1+2^{m}
and
s=1
. Since
er=2^{m}
and
p^{2^{m-1}}+1\equiv 2\pmod{4}
, we deduce from Lemma
5.13
that
p^{2^{m-1}}+1=2(1+2^{m})
. It holds only if
m=2
,
p=3
. We check by Magma
[
6
]
that there are two
N_{\Gamma}(R)
-orbits of sizes
120,~160
respectively on
{\mathcal{P}}=H(3,9)
. Their sizes are not divisible by the ovoid number
28
, so the two orbits are both tight sets.

Finally, suppose that
R\cong 2_{+}^{1+2m}
and
\kappa
is hyperbolic of plus sign. In this case,
d=2^{m}
,
r_{0}=|Z(R)|=2
,
e=1
, and
N_{\Delta}(R)=N_{\textup{GL}(V)}(R)={\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}\circ(2_{+}^{1+2m}.\textup{GO}^{+}_{2m}(2))
.
Since we do not consider
Q^{+}(3,q)
, we have
m\geq 3
. The rank of
{\mathcal{P}}
is
r=2^{m-1}\geq 4
. Let
p^{\prime}
be an odd prime divisor of
p^{r/2}+1
, and let
s
be the largest integer such that
p^{\prime s}
divides
p^{r/2}+1
. Then
p^{\prime}
is a primitive prime divisor of
p^{r}-1
by Lemma
5.13
, and so
p^{\prime}\geq 1+2^{m-1}
. We deduce from
\frac{p^{r}-1}{p-1}
divides
N_{\Gamma}(R)
that
p^{\prime s}
divides

y=(2^{m}-1)\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}(2^{2i}-1)
. By examining the prime divisors of
y
and using the fact
p^{\prime}\geq 1+2^{m-1}
that
p^{\prime}=1+2^{m-1}
,
s=1
. It holds for each odd prime divisor
p^{\prime}
of
p^{r/2}+1
and
2
strictly divides
p^{r/2}+1
, so
p^{2^{m-2}}+1=2(1+2^{m-1})
. It holds if and only if
(p,m)=(3,3)
. We go over the subgroups of
N_{\Gamma}(R)
whose orders are multiple of
\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}=40
by Magma
[
6
]
that the possible sizes for
O_{1},~O_{2}
are
\{280,840\}
,
\{480,640\}
and
\{160,960\}
. They all yield tight sets upon direct check. This completes the proof.
∎

We have completed the analysis of the geometric Aschbacher class
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
and classes
{\mathcal{C}}_{4}
to
{\mathcal{C}}_{8}
, and we are now ready to handle the three remaining cases in class
{\mathcal{C}}_{3}
, cf. Remark
5.6
. Recall that
H
has two orbits
O_{1},~O_{2}
on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
which are
i_{1}
- and
i_{2}
-tight sets respectively, and the corresponding subsets
O_{1}^{\prime},~O_{2}^{\prime}
as in (
5.1
) are nonempty and form the two
H
-orbits on
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
, and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
has rank at least
2
. The parameters of
O_{1}
,
O_{2}
are the same as
O_{1}^{\prime}
,
O_{2}^{\prime}
by Proposition
5.5
. Since
H
is irreducible on
V
, it is irreducible on
V^{\prime}
. It is not of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{3}
as a subgroup of
\Gamma(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
by the choice of
b
.

Proposition 5.15
.

Suppose that
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}={\mathcal{P}}
and
b>1
, and write
r^{\prime}
for the rank of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
. Let
O_{1}^{\prime}
,
O_{2}^{\prime}
be as in (
5.1
), and assume that they are both proper subsets of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
. If
r^{\prime}\geq 2
, then we have (T1-a) of Theorem
1.3
or one of the cases in the third block of Table
1.3
.

Proof.

Suppose that we are in row 1 of Table
2.2
. In this case, both
\kappa
and
\kappa^{\prime}
are symplectic. First consider the case where
H
is a geometric subgroup of
\Gamma(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
. We go over the arguments for this section to see that: there are no feasible subgroups of classes
{\mathcal{C}}_{4}-{\mathcal{C}}_{7}
with
q^{b}
not a prime, and we obtain (T1-a) of Theorem
1.3
for class
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
. If
H
is of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{8}
, then
q
is even and there is a quadratic form
Q^{\prime}
of plus sign on
V^{\prime}
that polarizes to
\kappa^{\prime}
. The two
H
-orbits are thus
O_{1}=\{\langle v\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}\mid Q^{\prime}(v)=0,\,v\neq 0\}
,
O_{2}=\{\langle v\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}\mid Q^{\prime}(v)\neq 0\}
. Take a vector
v
such that
Q^{\prime}(v)=1
. Let
K
be the intersection of
\Gamma^{\#}
and the group of semisimilarities of
(V^{\prime},\,Q^{\prime})
. Since
H
lies in
K
and
K
stabilizes both
O_{1}
and
O_{2}
, we deduce that
O_{2}
is the
K
-orbit of
\langle v\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}
. The latter is equivalent to
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}={\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}^{*}
by considering the
Q^{\prime}
-values for both sets. It follows that
b=1
: a contradiction. Next consider the case where
H
is a non-geometric subgroup of
\Gamma(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
. By Proposition
4.1
and the fact
q^{b}
is not a prime, we see that
W(d/b-1,q^{b})=W(5,4)
,
H=J_{2}
, and the two orbits are
25,~40
-tight sets respectively. We check that the group
J_{2}
has two orbits on the nonzero vectors of
V^{\prime}
by using the Atlas data
[
13
]
. Since
4=2^{2}
, we deduce that
b=2
,
{\mathcal{P}}=W(11,2)
. The two
J_{2}
-orbits
O_{1},~O_{2}
on the points of
{\mathcal{P}}
are
25,~40
-tight sets respectively.

Suppose that we are in rows 9 or 10 of Table
2.2
with
b=2
. We have
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}=H(d/2-1,q^{2})
,
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{\pm}(d-1,q^{2})
, and
\kappa(x)=\kappa^{\prime}(x,x)
. First consider the case where
H
is a geometric subgroup of
\Gamma(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
. We go over the arguments in this section to obtain
6,~27
-tight sets of
Q^{-}(9,2)
from those of
H(4,4)
for class
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
,
\textup{Sp}_{2m}(q)^{\prime}
-invariant
(q+1)
and
(q^{2m-1}+1)
-tight sets of
Q^{+}(4m-1,q)
with
d=4m
for class
{\mathcal{C}}_{5}
,
N_{\Gamma}(R)
-invariant
12,~16
-tight sets of
Q^{+}(7,3)
from those of
H(3,9)
with
R=4\circ 2^{1+4}
for class
{\mathcal{C}}_{6}
, and there are no further instances. For the
{\mathcal{C}}_{5}
case, we remark that the group
\textup{Sp}_{d/2}(q)
has exactly two orbits on the nonzero singular vectors of the unitary form
\kappa^{\prime}
by
[
15
, p. 991]
. We tabulate those examples in the third block of Table
1.3
and indicate their Aschbacher classes as a subgroup of
\Gamma(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
in the Remark column. Next consider the case where
H
is a non-geometric subgroup of
\Gamma(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
. We go over Section
4
to obtain the rows with an
{\mathcal{S}}
entry in the Remark column of Table
1.3
. This completes the proof.
∎

Let us summarize what we have done so far. Let
{\mathcal{P}}
be a classical polar space of rank
r\geq 2
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
such that
{\mathcal{P}}\neq Q^{+}(3,q)
, and write
(V,\kappa)
for the ambient space and form. Suppose that
H
is an irreducible group of semisimilarities that has two orbits on the singular points that are tight sets of
{\mathcal{P}}
. The group
\Omega(V,\kappa)^{\prime}
is transitive on the singular points, so
H
does not contain
\Omega(V,\kappa)^{\prime}
. By
[
1
]
,
H
is either of Aschbacher class
{\mathcal{S}}
or is contained in a maximal subgroup of
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
of classes
{\mathcal{C}}_{2},\ldots,{\mathcal{C}}_{8}
. In Section
4
we have classified such subgroups of Aschbacher class
{\mathcal{S}}
. In Propositions
5.2
,
5.3
,
5.4
and
5.15
, we have classified such subgroups of Aschbacher class
{\mathcal{C}}_{3}
. If
H
is of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{8}
, then we have (T4) of Theorem
1.3
. In Propositions
5.8
, 5.9, 5.10, 5.12, and
5.14
we have classified such subgroups of the remaining geometric Aschbacher classes. This concludes the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

6.
Proof of Theorem
1.6

We have classified the
m
-ovoids of finite classical polar spaces that admit a transitive automorphism group of semisimilarities acting irreducibly on the ambient space in
[
23
]
. In this section, we prove Theorem
1.6
by making use of such classification results. Suppose that
H
is an irreducible subgroup of
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
that has exactly two orbits on the singular points of
{\mathcal{P}}
which are respectively
m_{1},~m_{2}
-ovoids. We check by using Magma
[
6
]
that: for
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(5,2)
there is no such irreducible subgroup
H
; for
{\mathcal{P}}=H(3,4)
the subgroup
H
is of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
and appears in Table
1.1
; for
{\mathcal{P}}=W(5,2)
either
H
stabilizes an elliptic quadric
Q^{-}(5,2)
and covered by Theorem
1.6
(M2), or it appears in row 5 of Table
1.7
. Similarly, for
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(7,2)
either
H^{\infty}\in\{A_{9},\textup{PSL}_{2}(8)\}
and one orbit is an ovoid, or
H\leq\textup{GO}_{2}^{-}(2)\wr S_{4}
and
\{m_{1},m_{2}\}=\{6,9\}
. The two cases appear in Table
1.9
and Table
1.8
respectively. We suppose that
{\mathcal{P}}
is none of those four polar spaces from now on.

Let
b
be the largest integer such that
H
lies in
\Gamma\textup{L}_{d/b}(q^{b})
. We first consider the case where
b>1
. There is a vector space
V^{\prime}
of dimension
m:=d/b
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}
equipped with a nondegenerate form
\kappa^{\prime}
such that
(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
is associated with
(V,\kappa)
as in Table
2.2
. Then
H
is a subgroup of
\Gamma^{\#}
, cf. (
2.2
). We define
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
as in (
2.3
) which corresponds to the nonzero singular points of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
, and let
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
be its complement in
{\mathcal{P}}
. We list the cases where both
{\mathcal{M}}_{1},~{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
are nonempty transitive
m
-ovoids in Table
1.7
by
[
23
, Examples 3.3, 3.14]
. There are no further such cases by the main theorems therein. We now derive the last column on
H
of Table
1.7
.

(1)

If
{\mathcal{P}}=H(3m-1,q)
and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}=H(m-1,q^{3})
, then
b=3
,
m\geq 3
is odd and
q\in\{2^{2},2^{6}\}
. The two
H
-orbits are
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
, so we deduce that
H
is a subgroup of
\Gamma(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
that is transitive on both the set of singular points and the set of nonsingular points of
V^{\prime}
. Since
q^{3}
is a cube and
q
is even, we deduce that
\textup{SU}_{m}(q^{3/2})\unlhd H
by Lemma
2.3
.

(2)

If
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(2m-1,q)
and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}=Q^{-}(m-1,q^{2})
, then
b=2
and
m\geq 4
is even. The two
H
-orbits are
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
, so we deduce that
H
is a subgroup of
\Gamma(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
that is transitive on both the set of singular points and one class of nonsingular points of
V^{\prime}
. Since
q^{2}
is a square, by Lemma
2.5
we deduce that either
\Omega_{m}^{-}(q^{2})\unlhd H
, or
m/2
is odd and
\textup{SU}_{m/2}(q^{4})\unlhd H
, or
m=q=4
and
\textup{GO}_{2}^{-}(4^{2}).4\unlhd H
. The last case does not occur, since otherwise
b=4
by the definition of
b
: a contradiction to
b=2
.

(3)

If
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(3m-1,q)
and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}=Q^{-}(m-1,q^{3})
, then
b=3
,
q\in\{2,2^{3}\}
and
m\geq 4
is even. The two
H
-orbits are
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
, so we deduce that
H
is a subgroup of
\Gamma(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
that is transitive on both the set of singular points and the set of nonsingular points of
V^{\prime}
. Since
q^{3}
is a cube, by Lemma
2.5
we deduce that either
\Omega_{m}^{-}(q^{3})\unlhd H
, or
m/2
is odd and
\textup{SU}_{m/2}(q^{3})\unlhd H
.

(4)

If
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{+}(2m-1,q)
and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}=Q(m-1,q^{2})
, then
b=2
,
qm
is odd and
m\geq 3
. The two
H
-orbits are
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
, so we deduce that
H
is a subgroup of
\Gamma(V^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
that is transitive on both the set of singular points and the set of nonsingular points of
V^{\prime}
. Moreover, the latter set has size
\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{M}}_{2}|=\frac{1}{2}q^{m-1}(q^{m-1}+1)
. By Lemma
2.4
we deduce that either
\Omega_{m}(q^{2})\unlhd H
, or
m=7
and
G_{2}(q^{2})\unlhd H
.

(5)

If
{\mathcal{P}}=W(2m-1,q)
and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}=H(m-1,q^{2})
, then
b=2
and
m\geq 3
is odd. The two
H
-orbits are
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
, so we deduce that
H
is transitive on both singular and nonsingular points of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
. By Lemma
2.3
, we have either
\textup{SU}_{m}(q)\unlhd H
, or
(q,m)=(2,3)
and
H=3_{+}^{1+2}\rtimes C_{8}
.

It remains to consider the cases where one of
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
is empty. There are no such instances for
H(d-1,q)
or
Q(d-1,q)
by
[
23
, Theorems 16, 17]
. For
{\mathcal{P}}=Q^{-}(d-1,q)
, there is a transitive
3
-ovid of
Q^{-}(5,5)
with
H^{\infty}=3.A_{7}
and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}=H(2,5^{2})
by
[
23
, Theorem 3.18]
. Since
3+3=\frac{5^{r}-1}{5-1}
with
r=2
, it is a candidate case. There are exactly two
3.A_{7}
-orbits of the same sizes on the nonsingular vectors of
H(2,5^{2})
, so we have row 1 of Table
1.8
. For
{\mathcal{P}}=W(d-1,q)
, we have row 3 of Table
1.8
from
[
23
, Example 3.13]
. We consider case (b) in
[
23
, Theorem 3.20]
, where
{\mathcal{P}}=W(3,q)
and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}=H(0,q^{4})
. We take the following model for
(V,\kappa)
:
V={\mathbb{F}}_{q^{4}}
,
\kappa(x,y)=\textup{Tr}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{4}}/{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(\delta xy)
with
\delta^{q}+\delta=0
. For
a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{4}}^{*}
and
0\leq i\leq 4f-1
, define
\psi_{a,i}\mid x\mapsto ax^{p^{i}}
. Then
H_{0}
is a subgroup of
M=\{\psi_{a,i}\mid a^{q^{2}+1}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*},\,0\leq i\leq 4f-1\}
, where
|M|=4f(q^{2}+1)(q-1)
. An
M
-orbit is
O_{1}=\{\langle x\rangle\mid x^{q^{2}+1}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}\}
, so the
H
-orbits should be
O_{1}
and its complement
O_{2}
. We have
|O_{1}|=q^{2}+1
,
|O_{2}|=q(q^{2}+1)
, and
\textup{lcm}(|O_{1}|,|O_{2}|)
divides
4f(q^{2}+1)
if and only if
q
is one of
2,4,16
. It is straightforward to check that
M
is transitive on
O_{2}
if and only if
q=2,4
. This yields row 2 of Table
1.8
. By
[
23
, Theorem 3.20]
there are no further instances where
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
or
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
is empty and there are transitive
m_{1},~m_{2}
-ovoids with
m_{1}+m_{2}=\frac{q^{r}-1}{q-1}
. This completes the analysis of the case
b>1
.

We next consider the case where
b=1
. Then
H
is not of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{3}
as a subgroup of
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
by the choice of
b
. We now go over
[
23
, Theorems 16-20]
for the subgroups of each Aschbacher class in
\Gamma(V,\kappa)
except for
{\mathcal{C}}_{3}
. If
H
is of class
{\mathcal{S}}
, then we have one of the cases in Table
1.9
. If
H
is of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
, i.e.,
H
preserves a decomposition
V=V_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus V_{t}
with
t\geq 2
and
m=\dim(V_{i})
for each
i
, then
m=1
and
{\mathcal{P}}
is one of
Q(4,3)
,
Q(6,3)
and
Q^{+}(7,3)
. This leads to the examples in Table
1.1
by examining all the irreducible subgroups whose orders are divisible by the respective ovoid numbers using Magma. We remark that the
Q^{+}(7,2)
row of Table
1.8
also falls in this class with each
V_{i}
elliptic of dimension
2
. There are no candidates for
H
in the classes
{\mathcal{C}}_{4}
,
{\mathcal{C}}_{5}
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{7}
by the main theorems in
[
23
]
. If
H
is of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{6}
, then we have the example in Theorem
1.6
(M1) from
[
23
, Example 3.4]
and there are no further cases. If
H
is of class
{\mathcal{C}}_{8}
, then we have the example in Theorem
1.6
(M2) from
[
23
, Example 3.1]
. This completes the proof of Theorem
1.6
.
